A poisonous example to explicit resolutions of unbounded complexes Thanks: The first author was supported by the Spanish State Research Agency, through the Severo Ochoa and María de Maeztu Program for Centers and Units of Excellence in R& D (CEX2020-001084-M). The first and the fourth authors were partially supported by the projects MIMECO PID2020-113047GB-I00 financed by the Spanish Government. The first, second, and fourth authors are members of the LIGAT (Interactions Lab between Geometry, Algebra, and Topology), Research Group of the Generalitat de Catalunya number 2021 SGR 01015. The second author was supported by the FEDER/MEC grant “Homotopy theory of combinatorial and algebraic structures” PID2020-116481GB-I00. The third author was supported by the grant PID2020-113206GB-I00, funded by MCIN/AEI/10.13039/501100011033, and the project 22004/PI/22 of the “Fundación Séneca” (Murcia).
Dolors Herbera, Wolfgang Pitsch, Manuel Saorín, Simone Virili

Abstract
. We show that various methods for explicitly building resolutions of unbounded complexes in fact fail when applied to a rather simple and explicit complex. We show that one way to rescue these methods is to assume Roos (Ab.4 ∗ )- k k axiom, which we adapt to encompass also resolutions in the framework of relative homological algebra. In the end we discuss the existence of model structures for relative homological algebra for unbounded complexes under the relative (Ab.4 ∗ )- k k condition, and present a variety of examples where our results apply.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 18G25, Secondary 18E10,18G10

Introduction

After the publication of Verdier’s seminal thesis in 1967 (see
[
34
]
for the later complete publication), the study of the derived categories of Abelian categories has received considerable attention and found widespread applications across many areas of abstract mathematics. It is important to underline that the initial focus was mostly restricted to bounded or half-bounded derived categories, where it was already known that, when starting with an Abelian category with enough injectives (or projectives), each lower (upper) bounded complex is quasi-isomorphic to a lower (upper) bounded complex of injectives (projectives). Additionally, the morphisms in the derived category between two such bounded complexes of injectives (projectives) correspond precisely to the homotopy classes of chain maps between them. This suffices to ensure that the corresponding half-bounded derived categories are locally small, i.e., they have small
\mathrm{Hom}
-sets. In contrast, the extension of this result to unbounded derived categories presented a considerable challenge.

One of the main difficulties that arises in the unbounded setting is the necessity to find, for each complex, a quasi-isomorphism to a complex of injectives that, furthermore, is
K
-injective (or, dually, from a
K
-projective complex of projectives). In other words, one needs to build
dg
-injective (
dg
-projective) resolutions of unbounded complexes. Even so, such constructions were not available at the time, even for commonly used Grothendieck categories, like categories of modules or of quasi-coherent sheaves. The first breakthrough in this direction was pioneered by Spaltenstein
[
31
]
in 1988, who was able to build a
dg
-projective resolution for each
X^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathrm{Mod}(R))
, with
R
a ring, and a
dg
-injective resolution for each
X^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
, where
\mathcal{A}=\mathrm{Sheaf}_{\Theta}(X)
is the category of sheaves of modules, over a sheaf of rings
\Theta
on a topological space
X
. These constructions have the advantage of not being merely existence results, as they rely on very explicit constructions of resolutions.

The history of the proof of the existence of
dg
-injective resolutions of unbounded complexes for general Grothendieck categories and of the techniques used to obtain the result is complicated. In 2000, Alonso-Tarrío, Jeremias and Souto
[
2
, Theorem 5.4]
proved it as a consequence of the existence of
dg
-injective resolutions for module categories combined with Gabriel and Popescu embedding Theorem. In 2003, C. Serpé, in
[
29
, Theorem 3.13]
, gives an alternative proof by extending Spaltenstein’s approach to general Grothendieck categories. Serpe’s proof has a variation of the small object argument as a central point.

The first published proof of the existence of a model structure over a Grothendieck category is due to Hovey
[
15
]
and it was published in 2001. A very similar argument had already been outlined by Joyal in a private correspondence with Grothendieck dated back to 1984 (nowaday, the letter is available at
[
17
]
). Both authors use variations of the small object argument to establish the existence of
dg
-injective resolutions in complete generality. The resolutions produced by these methods are non-explicit in nature, rendering them impractical for the computation of
dg
-resolutions in concrete situations.

At this point, the following questions arise naturally:

Questions.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a complete Abelian category with enough injectives, and take
X^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
.

(Q.1)

Is it always possible to find a
dg
-injective resolution for
X^{\bullet}
?

(Q.2)

If
X^{\bullet}
has a
dg
-injective resolution, can one build such a resolution explicitly (i.e., with a step-by-step procedure that allows for a complete description of the final result, thus excluding, in principle, all constructions based on the small object argument)?

In the literature, one can find currently two separate papers –
[
28
]
and
[
37
]
– each claiming to give positive answers to both questions. Unfortunately, both constructions fail when applied to the “poisonous example” mentioned in the title, which is an unbounded complex
X^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G})
, where
\mathcal{G}
is a localization of
\mathrm{Mod}(R)
, with
R
being Nagata’s classical example of a commutative Noetherian ring with infinite Krull dimension (see
[
21
]
). Let us clarify that we are not claiming that the above questions have a negative answer, and so we do not discard the possibility that the derived category
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
is locally small whenever
\mathcal{A}
is a complete Abelian category with enough injectives. For example, for our “poisonous example”
X^{\bullet}
, both (Q.1) and (Q.2) can be answered in the positive. Indeed, take a
dg
-injective resolution
X^{\bullet}\to E^{\bullet}
in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathrm{Mod}\text{-}(R))
(e.g., using Spaltenstein’s construction); the inclusion of the torsion part
T^{\bullet}\leq E^{\bullet}
is degreewise split (and, arguably, easy to describe), so
E^{\bullet}/T^{\bullet}
is a complex of torsion-free injectives. In particular, this quotient lives in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G})
, and
X^{\bullet}\to E^{\bullet}/T^{\bullet}
is a
dg
-injective resolution (see Section
3
for details).

On the positive side, the second named author together with Chachólski, Neeman and Scherer
[
3
]
gave affirmative answers to both questions, adapting Spaltenstein’s construction, under the extra hypothesis that the category
\mathcal{A}
is (Ab
4^{*}
)-
k
(in the sense of Roos
[
27
]
), for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
. More generally, given an injective class (i.e., a preenveloping class closed under summands)
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
, they proved that any complex
X^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
has an
\mathcal{I}
-fibrant replacement, provided
\mathcal{A}
satisfies the (Ab.4
∗
)-
\mathcal{I}
-
k
condition – a version of Roos’ (Ab
4^{*}
)-
k
relative to
\mathcal{I}
. To recover the results about
dg
-injective resolutions, it is then enough to take
\mathcal{I}=\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{A})
, the class of all injective objects in
\mathcal{A}
. The existence of
\mathcal{I}
-fibrant replacements, when combined with results of
[
4
]
, allows us to build and study a suitable model structure in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
whose homotopy category is the
\mathcal{I}
-derived category
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A};\,\mathcal{I})
from
[
3
,
4
]
; the usual derived category
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
is equivalent to
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A};\,\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{A}))
.

The paper is organized as follows: In Section
1
, after fixing the needed conventions and notations about cochain complexes, we review some basic definitions and results regarding weak factorization systems, model categories, cotorsion pairs and Hovey’s correspondence. In Section
2
we show that, whenever
\mathcal{A}
is a complete (Ab.
4^{*}
)-
k
Abelian category with enough injectives, any
X^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
has a
dg
-injective resolution. Although this may be deduced from more general results in
[
3
]
, we include a short direct proof. The “poisonous example” mentioned in the title is introduced and studied in Section
3
, where it is used to show that the (Ab.
4^{*}
)-
k
condition is really necessary for the constructions of Section
2
. Section
4
is devoted to injective Cartan-Eilenberg (
ce
-)resolutions: after reviewing the definitions and some classical results, we prove that, to build a
dg
-injective resolution of an
X^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
(with
\mathcal{A}
as in Section
2
) one can just totalize a
ce
-resolution of
X^{\bullet}
. Furthermore, this strategy fails when applied to our “poisonous example”, showing that (Ab.
4^{*}
)-
k
is necessary also in this approach. In Section
5
we briefly review the construction of
[
28
]
and we show that it fails to produce a
dg
-injective resolution for the “poisonous example”. Similarly, in Section
6
, we review the construction of
[
37
]
and we show that it does not work when applied to our “poisonous example”, also observing that the gap in the argument disappears if one assumes that the ambient category is (Ab.
4^{*}
). In Section
7
, we concentrate on the construction of the so-called
\mathcal{I}
-injective model structure on
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
, relative to a given injective class
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
. After recalling some general results from
[
4
]
, we describe the construction of Spaltenstein towers of partial
\mathcal{I}
-injective resolutions given in
[
3
]
. In the last part of the section, we prove a number of results related to the (Ab.4
∗
)-
\mathcal{I}
-
k
condition, showing that it implies the existence of the
\mathcal{I}
-injective model structure on
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
. Finally, in Section
8
, we include a number of examples and applications of the formalism introduced in Section
7
. In particular, we first consider those injective classes with the additional property of being cogenerating, we then study the injective classes contained in
\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{A})
, and the injective class of the pure-injective objects in a locally finitely presented Grothendieck category. Moreover, we introduce a notion of global dimension for
\mathcal{A}
relative to an injective class
\mathcal{I}
; if this invariant is finite, then
\mathcal{A}
is (Ab.4
∗
)-
\mathcal{I}
-
k
. Using the relative global dimension, we give a new characterization of the
n
-tilting cotorsion pairs. Finally, we give an application to categories of quasi-coherent sheaves over a scheme.

[
37
]
.

We also thank Leo Alonso-Tarrío and Ana Jeremías for their remarks on the history of the existence of
dg
-injective resolutions over general Grothendieck categories.

Last but not least, we would like to thank Leonid Positselski for bringing
[
25
]
to our attention, and for his remarks and insights on quasi-coherent sheaves; this interaction motivated us to add section
8.6
to the paper.

1.
Preliminaries and notations

, we denote by

the category of unbounded cochain complexes and maps of complexes. In particular, given

we denote by
B^{n}(X^{\bullet}):=\mathrm{Im}(d^{n-1})
,
Z^{n}(X^{\bullet}):=\mathrm{Ker}(d^{n})
, and
H^{n}(X^{\bullet}):=Z^{n}(X^{\bullet})/B^{n}(X^{\bullet})
, the
n
-th
coboundaries
,
cocycles
, and
cohomology
of
X^{\bullet}
, respectively. We let
\Sigma\colon\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
be the “shift to the left” auto-equivalence of
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
, that is,
(\Sigma X^{\bullet})^{n}:=X^{n+1}
.

A given morphism of complexes
\phi^{\bullet}\colon X^{\bullet}\to Y^{\bullet}
is said to be a
quasi-isomorphism
if
H^{n}(\phi^{\bullet})
is an isomorphism for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. We also say that a complex
X^{\bullet}
is
exact
(or
acyclic
) if
H^{n}(X^{\bullet})=0
, for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
or, equivalently,
X^{\bullet}\to 0
is a quasi-isomorphism.

For each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, we denote by
\mathrm{Ch}^{\geq k}(\mathcal{A})
(resp.,
\mathrm{Ch}^{\leq k}(\mathcal{A})
) the full subcategory of the
X^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
such that
X^{n}=0
, for all
n<k
(resp.,
n>k
). Moreover,
\mathrm{Ch}^{+}(\mathcal{A}):=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{Ch}^{\geq n}(\mathcal{A})
denotes the full subcategory of bounded below complexes. Given
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, the inclusion
\mathrm{Ch}^{\geq k}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
has a left adjoint, called the
k
-th
left truncation functor

\tau^{\geq k}\colon\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathrm{Ch}^{\geq k}(\mathcal{A})
such that:

Given an object
A\in\mathcal{A}
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
we let
S^{k}(A)
denote the following
stalk complex
:

S^{k}(A):=(\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 6.75pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.75pt\raise 24.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 24.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 53.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 53.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 85.25pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 85.25pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 114.25pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 114.25pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\cdots}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces)\in\mathrm{Ch}^{\geq k}(\mathcal{A})\cap\mathrm{Ch}^{\leq k}(\mathcal{A});

for
n=0
we sometimes denote
S^{0}(A)
simply by
A
.
Moreover, we denote by
D^{k}(A)
the following
disk complex
:

D^{k}(A):=(\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 6.75pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 24.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 24.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 53.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 53.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 67.0694pt\raise 5.93333pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.92778pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}_{A}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 85.25pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 85.25pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 116.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 116.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 145.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 145.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\cdots}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces)\in\mathrm{Ch}^{\geq k}(\mathcal{A})\cap\mathrm{Ch}^{\leq k+1}(\mathcal{A}).

Observe that
D^{k}(A)
is an exact complex, that is,
H^{i}(D^{k}(A))=0
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. Moreover, as co/products of isomorphisms are isomorphisms, any co/product of disc complexes is still exact. Finally, observe that, if
Y^{\bullet}
is such a co/product of disk complexes, and
X^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
is any complex, then
H^{i}(X^{\bullet}\oplus Y^{\bullet})\cong H^{i}(X^{\bullet})\oplus H^{i}(Y^{\bullet})\cong H^{i}(X^{\bullet})
, for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. This easy observation is often useful when we need to modify a given complex
X^{\bullet}
without changing its cohomologies.

Weak factorization systems.
Given a category
\mathcal{A}
, denote by
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbf{2}}
its category of morphisms. Given
\phi
and
\psi\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathbf{2}}
, one says that
\phi
is
left weakly orthogonal
to
\psi
(and, equivalently, that
\psi
is
right weakly orthogonal
to
\phi
), in symbols
\phi\boxslash\psi
, if for any solid commutative square

\textstyle{X_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\scriptstyle{a}
\textstyle{Y_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b}
\scriptstyle{\exists\,d}
\textstyle{Y_{1}}

there is a (not necessarily unique) diagonal
d\colon X_{1}\to Y_{0}
such that
a=d\circ\phi
and
b=\psi\circ d
.

Given a class of objects
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{\mathbf{2}}
, we denote by

{}^{\boxslash}\mathcal{X}:=\{l\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathbf{2}}:l\boxslash x,\ \forall x\in\mathcal{X}\}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{X}^{\boxslash}:=\{r\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathbf{2}}:x\boxslash r,\ \forall x\in\mathcal{X}\},

the classes of all morphisms in
\mathcal{A}
which are weakly left or right orthogonal, respectively, to
\mathcal{X}
. Using another common terminology,
l\in{}^{\boxslash}\mathcal{X}
(resp.,
r\in\mathcal{X}^{\boxslash}
), if it has the
left
(
right
)
lifting property
with respect to
\mathcal{X}
. The following general lemma is often useful in practice:

Lemma 1.1

(
[
26
, Lemma 11.1.4]
)
.

Any class of arrows of the form
{}^{\boxslash}\mathcal{X}
is closed under coproducts, push-outs, transfinite composition, retracts, and it contains the isomorphisms. The class
\mathcal{X}^{\boxslash}
has the dual closure properties.

In particular, we will need the following consequence of the above lemma: Suppose that
(A_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is an inverse system in
\mathcal{A}
such that
\varprojlim_{\mathbb{N}}A_{n}
exists in
\mathcal{A}
. If
(A_{n+1}\to A_{n})\in\mathcal{X}^{\boxslash}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, then the canonical map
\varprojlim_{\mathbb{N}}A_{n}\to A_{0}
is also in
\mathcal{X}^{\boxslash}
.

A pair of subclasses
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
of
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbf{2}}
is a
weak factorization system
if the following hold:

(WFS.1)

\mathcal{X}^{\boxslash}=\mathcal{Y}
and
\mathcal{X}={}^{\boxslash}\mathcal{Y}
;

(WFS.2)

each
\phi\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathbf{2}}
can be written as
\phi=y\circ x
, with
x\in\mathcal{X}
and
y\in\mathcal{Y}
.

Model categories.

A
model structure
on a bicomplete category
\mathcal{M}
is a triple
(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F})
of classes of maps, called respectively
weak equivalences
,
cofibrations
and
fibrations
, such that:

(MS.1)

\mathcal{W}
contains all the isomorphisms and has the
2
-out-of-
3
property;

(MS.2)

both
(\mathcal{C}\cap\mathcal{W},\mathcal{F})
and
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}\cap\mathcal{W})
are weak factorization systems.

In this case, the quadruple
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{W},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F})
is called a
model category
. The morphisms in
\mathcal{C}\cap\mathcal{W}
and
\mathcal{F}\cap\mathcal{W}
are called
trivial cofibrations
and
trivial fibrations
, respectively. Furthermore, an object
F\in\mathcal{M}
is said to be
fibrant
if the terminal map
F\to*
is a fibration while, for
\mathcal{M}
pointed, we say that
F
is
trivial
if the trivial map
0\to F
is a weak equivalence. Finally, a
fibrant replacement
for an object
X\in\mathcal{M}
, is a weak equivalence
X\to F
, with
F
fibrant. Cofibrant objects and cofibrant replacements are defined dually.

Remark 1.2
.

Suppose that we are in the following situation: we have a bicomplete category
\mathcal{M}
and two classes of maps,
\mathcal{W}
, whose members we call weak equivalences, and
\mathcal{C}
, whose members we call cofibrations, that we would like to use to construct a model structure on
\mathcal{M}
. In this setting, we are forced to take
\mathcal{F}:=(\mathcal{C}\cap\mathcal{W})^{\boxslash}
as a class of fibrations. Even if we do not know whether
(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F})
is a model structure, let us abuse terminology and call an object
X\in\mathcal{M}
fibrant if
X\to*
belongs to
\mathcal{F}
. Consider an inverse system
(F_{n})_{\mathbb{N}}
of fibrant objects and suppose that
(F_{n+1}\to F_{n})\in\mathcal{F}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, then
F:=\varprojlim_{\mathbb{N}}F_{n}
is a fibrant object.

Proof.

Consider the inverse system
(F^{\prime}_{n})_{\mathbb{N}}
, where
F^{\prime}_{0}=*
is the terminal object, and
F^{\prime}_{n+1}:=F_{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then, as
\mathcal{F}=(\mathcal{C}\cap\mathcal{W})^{\boxslash}
, the unique map
\varprojlim_{\mathbb{N}}F_{n}\cong\varprojlim_{\mathbb{N}}F_{n}^{\prime}\to F^{\prime}_{0}=*
does still belong to
\mathcal{F}
, as discussed briefly after Lemma
1.1
.
∎

Hovey correspondence.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an Abelian category; for any subclass
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
, and any subset
I\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
, we use the following notations for the corresponding right and left orthogonal classes of
\mathcal{C}
:

\mathcal{C}^{\perp_{I}}:=\{A\in\mathcal{A}:\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{i}(C,A)=0,\text{ for all $i\in I$ and all $C\in\mathcal{C}$}\}\quad\text{and}

{}^{\perp_{I}}\mathcal{C}:=\{A\in\mathcal{A}:\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{i}(A,C)=0,\text{ for all $i\in I$ and all $C\in\mathcal{C}$}\}.

Moreover let
\mathcal{C}^{\perp_{n}}:=\mathcal{C}^{\perp_{\{n\}}}
(for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
), and
\mathcal{C}^{\perp}:=\mathcal{C}^{\perp_{0}}
, with analogous conventions on the left.

A pair
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
of classes in
\mathcal{A}
is said to be a
cotorsion pair
if
\mathcal{X}={}^{\perp_{1}}\mathcal{Y}
, and
\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{X}^{\perp_{1}}
. A cotorsion pair
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
is
left
(resp.,
right
)
complete
if for all
A\in\mathcal{A}
there is an exact sequence

0\longrightarrow Y\longrightarrow X\longrightarrow A\longrightarrow 0\quad(\text{resp., }0\longrightarrow A\longrightarrow Y^{\prime}\longrightarrow X^{\prime}\longrightarrow 0),

with
X,X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
and
Y,Y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Y}
, called a
left
(resp.,
right
)
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
-approximation
of
A
. Moreover, we say that
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
is
complete
if it is left and right complete, and
hereditary
if
\mathcal{X}
is closed under kernels of
epimorphisms or, equivalently,
\mathcal{Y}
is closed under cokernels of
monomorphisms.

Recall that a
model structure
on a bicomplete Abelian category
\mathcal{A}
is said to be
Abelian
if:

•

the (trivial) cofibrations are the monomorphisms that have a (trivially) cofibrant cokernel;

•

the (trivial) fibrations are the epimorphisms that have a (trivially) fibrant kernel.

By a famous result of Hovey (see
[
16
]
), Abelian model structures on a bicomplete Abelian category
\mathcal{A}
are in bijection with the so-called
Hovey triples
, which are triples of classes of objects
(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{C}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{F}})
such that
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}
is thick (i.e., it is closed under summands and, given any short exact sequence
0\to A\to B\to C\to 0
in
\mathcal{A}
, if two elements of the set
\{A,B,C\}
belong in
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}
, then so does the third) and both
(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{C}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}\cap\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{F}})
and
(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{C}}\cap\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{F}})
are complete cotorsion pairs. In this case, the class
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{C}}
is precisely the class of cofibrant
objects,
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{F}}
is the class of fibrant objects, and
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}
is the class of trivial objects in
the corresponding Abelian model structure.

Lifting of cotorsion pairs.

Given a cotorsion pair
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
in
\mathcal{A}
, Gillespie
[
8
, Definition 3.3]
introduced the following classes, with the goal of inducing a Hovey triple (and so also an Abelian model structure) in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
:

•

\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
(resp.,
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}
) is the class of all acyclic
A^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
such that
Z^{n}(A^{\bullet})\in\mathcal{X}
(resp.,
Z^{n}(A^{\bullet})\in\mathcal{Y}
);

•

\mathrm{dg}\mathcal{X}
is the class of those
A^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
such that
A^{n}\in\mathcal{X}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and for which the total hom-complex
\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(A^{\bullet},Y^{\bullet})
is exact for all
Y^{\bullet}\in\widetilde{Y}
;

•

\mathrm{dg}\mathcal{Y}
is the class of those
A^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
such that
A^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and for which the total hom-complex
\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(X^{\bullet},A^{\bullet})
is exact for all
X^{\bullet}\in\widetilde{X}
.

Proposition 1.3

(
[
8
, Lemma 3.4, Proposition 3.6, Lemma 3.14]
and
[
16
, Lemma 6.2]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a bicomplete Abelian category,
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
a cotorsion pair, and
\mathcal{E}
the class of exact complexes. Then, the following statements hold true

(1)

a complex
A^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}^{+}(\mathcal{A})
belongs to
\mathrm{dg}\mathcal{Y}
if and only if
A^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}
, for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
;

(2)

if
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
is complete, both
(\widetilde{\mathcal{X}},\mathrm{dg}\mathcal{Y})
and
(\mathrm{dg}\mathcal{X},\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}})
are cotorsion pairs in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
. Hence, if
(A_{n})_{\mathbb{N}}
is an inverse system such that
A_{0}\in\mathrm{dg}\mathcal{Y}
and
A_{n+1}\to A_{n}
is an epimorphism with
\ker(A_{n+1}\to A_{n})\in\mathrm{dg}\mathcal{Y}
(for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
), then
\lim_{\mathbb{N}}A_{n}\in\mathrm{dg}\mathcal{Y}
;

(3)

if
(\mathrm{dg}\mathcal{X},\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}})
is a complete cotorsion pair, and
\mathrm{dg}\mathcal{X}\cap\mathcal{E}=\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
, then
\mathrm{dg}\mathcal{Y}\cap\mathcal{E}=\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}
.

As a consequence of the above proposition, one can deduce the following criterion for a cotorsion pair in
\mathcal{A}
to induce a model structure on complexes:

Corollary 1.4
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a bicomplete Abelian category, let
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
be a cotorsion pair in
\mathcal{A}
, let
\mathcal{E}
be the class of exact complexes and suppose that:

(1)

(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
,
(\mathrm{dg}\mathcal{X},\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}})
and
(\widetilde{\mathcal{X}},\mathrm{dg}\mathcal{Y})
are complete;

(2)

\mathrm{dg}\mathcal{X}\cap\mathcal{E}=\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
.

Then
(\mathcal{E},\mathrm{dg}\mathcal{X},\mathrm{dg}\mathcal{Y})
is a Hovey triple and, therefore, it induces an Abelian model structure on
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
such that the weak equivalences are the quasi-isomorphisms, the cofibrations are the monomorphisms with cokernel in
\mathrm{dg}\mathcal{X}
, and the
fibrations are the epimorphisms with kernel in
\mathrm{dg}\mathcal{Y}
.

2.
dg
-injective resolutions of complexes via truncations

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an Abelian category with enough injectives, that is, for all
A\in\mathcal{A}
there is a monomorphism
A\to E
, with
E\in\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{A})
, and consider the complete cotorsion pair
(\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{A}))
in
\mathcal{A}
. Then:

•

\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}=\mathcal{E}
is the class of exact complexes;

•

\widetilde{\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{A})}
is the class of contractible complexes of injectives, that coincides with the class
\mathrm{Inj}(\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A}))
of injective objects in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
(see
[
36
, Exercise 2.2.1]
);

•

\mathrm{dg}\mathcal{A}=\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
and
\mathrm{dg}(\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{A}))
is, by definition, the class of
dg
-injective complexes
.

In particular, the cotorsion pairs induced in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
are
(\mathrm{Ch}(A),\mathrm{Inj}(\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})))
, which is complete (see
[
36
, Exercise 2.2.2]
), and
(\mathcal{E},\mathrm{dg}(\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{A})))
. Furthermore, condition (2) in Corollary
1.4
is trivial in this case. Hence, when
\mathcal{A}
is bicomplete, we get a model structure in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
provided each
X^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
has a
dg
-injective resolution
, that is, a quasi-isomorphism
\lambda^{\bullet}\colon X^{\bullet}\to E^{\bullet}
, with
E^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{dg}(\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{A}))
. In fact, this is equivalent to the fact that, in the short exact sequence

(2.1)

0\longrightarrow\Sigma^{-1}E^{\bullet}\longrightarrow\Sigma^{-1}\mathrm{cone}(\lambda^{\bullet})\longrightarrow X^{\bullet}\longrightarrow 0,

\mathrm{cone}(\lambda^{\bullet})\in\mathcal{E}
and
E^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{dg}(\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{A}))
, so
(\mathcal{E},\mathrm{dg}(\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{A})))
is left complete. Note also that, up to adding a bunch of disc complexes to
E^{\bullet}
, we may assume that
\lambda^{\bullet}
is a monomorphism. Hence,
0\to X^{\bullet}\to E^{\bullet}\to E^{\bullet}/X^{\bullet}\to 0
is a right approximation sequence, and
(\mathcal{E},\mathrm{dg}(\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{A})))
is complete.

In this section we give a complete proof, based on ideas from
[
3
]
, of the fact that Spalstenstein’s construction of
dg
-injective resolutions in the category of complexes of modules (over a ring or, more generally, over a sheaf of rings) works in every complete Abelian category
\mathcal{A}
with enough injectives that satisfies Roos’ condition (Ab.
4^{*}
)-
k
(for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
).

2.1.
Resolution of bounded-below complexes

Consider a complex
X^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}^{\geq k}(\mathcal{A})
(for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}
), where
\mathcal{A}
is any Abelian category with enough injectives. In what follows we show how to construct a monomorphic quasi-isomorphism
\lambda^{\bullet}\colon X^{\bullet}\to E^{\bullet}
with
E^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}^{\geq k}(\mathcal{A})
and
E^{i}
injective for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
; then this is a
dg
-injective resolution, by Proposition
1.3
(1). Suppose for simplicity that
k=0
, that is, we start with
X^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}^{\geq 0}(\mathcal{A})
, and we proceed inductively to construct

\lambda^{\bullet}\colon X^{\bullet}\longrightarrow E^{\bullet}:=(\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 6.75pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 24.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 24.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 53.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 53.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{E^{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 74.34596pt\raise 6.12001pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.12001pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{e^{0}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 90.19443pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 90.19443pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{E^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 110.79039pt\raise 6.12001pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.12001pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{e^{1}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 126.63885pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 126.63885pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\cdots}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces).

Indeed, let
E^{n}=0
,
e^{n}=0
and
\lambda^{n}=0
, for all
n<0
and, for
n=0
, define
\lambda^{0}\colon X^{0}\to E^{0}
to be a chosen monomorphism with
E^{0}\in\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{A})
. Now, given
n\geq 0
, if we have already built
e^{i-1}\colon E^{i-1}\to E^{i}
and
\lambda^{i}\colon X^{i}\to E^{i}
, for all
i\leq n
, such that:

(1
n
)

\lambda^{i}\circ d^{i-1}=e^{i-1}\circ\lambda^{i-1}
for all
i\leq n
;

(2
n
)

e^{i-1}\circ e^{i-2}=0
for all
i\leq n
;

(3
n
)

the map
H^{i}(X^{\bullet})\to H^{i}(E^{\bullet})
induced by
\lambda^{i}
is an isomorphism for all
i\leq n-1
;

(4
n
)

the map
\bar{\lambda}^{n}\colon X^{n}/B^{n}(X^{\bullet})\to E^{n}/B^{n}(E^{\bullet})
induced by
\lambda^{n}
is a monomorphism;

we consider the following push-out diagram:

\textstyle{X^{n}/B^{n}(X^{\bullet})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
P.O.
\scriptstyle{\bar{\lambda}^{n}}
\scriptstyle{\bar{d}^{n}}
\textstyle{X^{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu^{n+1}}
\textstyle{E^{n}/B^{n}(E^{\bullet})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{y^{n}}
\textstyle{P^{n+1}.}

In particular, the map
\bar{\mu}^{n+1}\colon(X^{n+1}/B^{n+1}(X^{\bullet})\cong)\mathrm{Coker}(\bar{d}^{n})\to\mathrm{Coker}(y^{n})
is an isomorphism.
Moreover, as
\bar{\lambda}^{n}
is a monomorphism by hypothesis, the above square is also a pullback, so that
\bar{\lambda}^{n}
induces an isomorphism between
\mathrm{Ker}(\bar{d}^{n})=H^{n}(X^{\bullet})
and
\mathrm{Ker}(y^{n})
. Choose now a monomorphism
\iota^{n+1}\colon P^{n+1}\to E^{n+1}
, with
E^{n+1}\in\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{A})
. Then, the following square remains a pullback:

\textstyle{X^{n}/B^{n}(X^{\bullet})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
P.B.
\scriptstyle{\bar{\lambda}^{n}}
\scriptstyle{\bar{d}^{n}}
\textstyle{X^{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda^{n+1}:=\iota^{n+1}\circ\mu^{n+1}}
\textstyle{E^{n}/B^{n}(E^{\bullet})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota^{n+1}\circ y^{n}}
\textstyle{E^{n+1}.}

Finally define
e^{n}:=\iota^{n+1}\circ y^{n}\circ\pi^{n}\colon E^{n}\to E^{n+1}
, where
\pi^{n}\colon E^{n}\to E^{n}/B^{n}(E^{\bullet})
is the canonical projection. Conditions (1
n+1
)–(4
n+1
) hold by construction, so we can continue with the induction.

2.2.
Spaltenstein towers of partial resolutions

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an Abelian category with enough injectives and fix a complex
X^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
. We start by considering the following inverse system of successive truncations:

\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{2}^{\bullet}}
\textstyle{\tau^{\geq-2}(X^{\bullet})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{1}^{\bullet}}
\textstyle{\tau^{\geq-1}(X^{\bullet})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{0}^{\bullet}}
\textstyle{\tau^{\geq 0}(X^{\bullet}),}

where
\rho_{n}^{\bullet}
is the canonical projection. As
\tau^{\geq-n}(X^{\bullet})\in\mathrm{Ch}^{\geq-n}(\mathcal{A})
, the argument of Subsection
2.1
gives a monomorphic
dg
-injective resolution
\lambda_{n}^{\bullet}\colon\tau^{\geq-n}(X^{\bullet})\to E^{\bullet}_{n}
, with
E_{n}^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}^{\geq-n}(\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{A}))
, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. We get the following solid diagram:

(2.2)

\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{2}^{\bullet}}
\textstyle{\tau^{\geq-2}(X^{\bullet})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{2}^{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{1}^{\bullet}}
\textstyle{\tau^{\geq-1}(X^{\bullet})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{1}^{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{0}^{\bullet}}
\textstyle{\tau^{\geq 0}(X^{\bullet})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{0}^{\bullet}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t_{2}^{\bullet}}
\textstyle{E^{\bullet}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t_{1}^{\bullet}}
\textstyle{E^{\bullet}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t_{0}^{\bullet}}
\textstyle{E^{\bullet}_{0};}

and we claim that it can be completed in a commutative way by the dotted arrows. Indeed, consider the following short exact sequence for each
n>0

0\rightarrow\tau^{\geq-n}(X^{\bullet})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lambda_{n}^{\bullet}}}{{\longrightarrow}}E_{n}^{\bullet}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Coker}(\lambda_{n}^{\bullet})\rightarrow 0,

and apply the functor
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})}(-,E_{n-1}^{\bullet})
to get the following exact sequence in
\mathrm{Ab}
:

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})}(E_{n}^{\bullet},E_{n-1}^{\bullet})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\lambda_{n}^{\bullet})^{*}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})}(\tau^{\geq-n}(X^{\bullet}),E_{n-1}^{\bullet})\longrightarrow\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})}^{1}(\mathrm{Coker}(\lambda_{n}^{\bullet}),E_{n-1}^{\bullet})=0.

The last equality to zero is due to the fact
\mathrm{Coker}(\lambda_{n}^{\bullet})\in\mathcal{E}
,
E_{n-1}^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{dg}(\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{A}))
and
(\mathcal{E},\mathrm{dg}(\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{A})))
is a cotorsion pair in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
. It then follows that
\lambda_{n-1}^{\bullet}\circ\rho_{n-1}^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Im}((\lambda_{n}^{\bullet})^{*})
, so we have a map
t_{n-1}^{\bullet}\colon E_{n}^{\bullet}\to E_{n-1}^{\bullet}
, such that
t_{n-1}^{\bullet}\circ\lambda_{n}^{\bullet}=\lambda_{n-1}^{\bullet}\circ\rho_{n-1}^{\bullet}
, as desired.

Observe also that, possibly adding a number of disk complexes to our partial resolutions if needed, one can always suppose that
t_{n}^{\bullet}
is a (degreewise split) epimorphism with
\ker(t_{n}^{\bullet})\in\mathrm{Ch}^{+}(\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{A}))
, whence with
dg
-injective kernel, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Definition 2.1
.

A
Spaltenstein tower
of partial resolutions for
X^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
is a commutative diagram like the one in (
2.2
) where
\lambda^{\bullet}_{n}
is a
dg
-injective resolution for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and
t_{n}^{\bullet}
is a (degreewise split) epimorphism with
dg
-injective kernel, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

In particular, in the above discussion we have just shown that:

Proposition 2.2
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an Abelian category with enough injectives. Then any complex in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
has a Spaltenstein tower of partial resolutions.

2.3.
Injective resolutions and the (Ab.
4^{*}
)-
k
condition

The goal of this subsection is to prove that, under suitable conditions, the inverse limit of a Spaltenstein tower of partial resolutions actually produces a
dg
-injective resolution. We start with two easy observations:

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a complete Abelian category and consider a sequence of complexes

\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t^{\bullet}_{2}}
\textstyle{X^{\bullet}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t^{\bullet}_{1}}
\textstyle{X^{\bullet}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t^{\bullet}_{0}}
\textstyle{X^{\bullet}_{0}}

such that, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the chain map
t^{\bullet}_{n}
is degreewise a split-epimorphism. Then, there is the following degreewise split-exact sequence in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\varprojlim_{\mathbb{N}}X^{\bullet}_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\prod_{\mathbb{N}}X^{\bullet}_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1-t}
\textstyle{\prod_{\mathbb{N}}X^{\bullet}_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

where the map
1-t
is described by a matrix with identities on the main diagonal,
-t^{\bullet}_{n}
as the
n
-th entry of the first superdiagonal, and
0
’s everywhere else.

Proof.

The universal property defining
\varprojlim_{\mathbb{N}}X^{\bullet}_{n}
is clearly equivalent to the universal property defining the kernel of
1-t
. Moreover, to prove that
1-t
is a degreewise split-epimorphism, it is enough to find, for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, a right-inverse for the following matrix:

{\scriptsize\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{id}_{X^{k}_{0}}&-t_{0}^{k}&0&0&0&\ldots\\
0&\mathrm{id}_{X^{k}_{1}}&-t_{1}^{k}&0&0&\ldots\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots\end{pmatrix}}

Fix, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, a split-monomorphism
s^{k}_{n}\colon X^{k}_{n}\to X^{k}_{n+1}
such that
t^{k}_{n}\circ s^{k}_{n}=\mathrm{id}_{X^{k}_{n}}
. Then, the right-inverse we are looking for is the following row-finite matrix:

{\scriptsize\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0&0&0&\ldots\\
-s_{0}^{k}&0&0&0&0&\ldots\\
-s^{k}_{1}\circ s_{0}^{k}&-s_{1}^{k}&0&0&0&\ldots\\
-s^{k}_{2}\circ s^{k}_{1}\circ s_{0}^{k}&-s^{k}_{2}\circ s_{1}^{k}&-s^{k}_{2}&0&0&\ldots\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots\end{pmatrix}}\qed

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a complete Abelian category and fix some integer
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. Consider also two families of complexes
(X^{\bullet}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
,
(Y^{\bullet}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
, and maps of complexes
\phi^{\bullet}_{n}\colon X^{\bullet}_{n}\to Y^{\bullet}_{n}
(for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
), such that
\phi^{i}_{n}
is an isomorphism for all
i\geq k
. Letting

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 66.32474pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-66.32474pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\phi^{\bullet}:=\prod_{\mathbb{N}}\phi^{\bullet}_{n}\colon\prod_{\mathbb{N}}X^{\bullet}_{n}\longrightarrow\prod_{\mathbb{N}}Y^{\bullet}_{n}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

\phi^{i}
is an isomorphism for all
i\geq k
. In particular,
H^{i}(\prod_{\mathbb{N}}X^{\bullet}_{n})\cong H^{i}(\prod_{\mathbb{N}}Y^{\bullet}_{n})
, for all
i>k
.

Let us point out that the isomorphism for the
i
-th cohomologies of the products holds for all
i>k
, but it may actually fail for
i=k
, even if
H^{k}(X^{\bullet}_{n})\cong H^{k}(Y^{\bullet}_{n})
, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

As products are built componentwise, the result follows by the observation that the class of isomorphisms in
\mathcal{A}
is closed under products.
∎

The following definition was introduced by Roos
[
27
]
:

Definition 2.5
.

A complete Abelian category
\mathcal{A}
with enough injectives is (Ab.
4^{*}
)-
k
, for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
if, for any family
(A_{\lambda})_{\Lambda}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
, the
n
-th derived functor
\prod_{\Lambda}^{(n)}A_{\lambda}
vanishes for all
n>k
.

In particular,
\mathcal{A}
is (Ab.
4^{*}
)-
0
if and only if it satisfies Grothendieck’s axiom (Ab.
4^{*}
), that is, products are exact in
\mathcal{A}
. In the following lemma we show a direct consequence of the
(Ab.
4^{*}
)-
k
condition, which is precisely what is needed for the construction of
dg
-injective resolutions:

Lemma 2.6
.

Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
h\in\mathbb{Z}
, and let
\mathcal{A}
be a complete (Ab.
4^{*}
)-
k
Abelian category. Given a family
(X^{\bullet}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
such that
H^{i}(X^{\bullet}_{n})=0
and with
X^{i}_{n}
an injective object, for all
i>h
, we have that
H^{i}\left(\prod_{\mathbb{N}}X_{n}^{\bullet}\right)=0
, for all
i>h+k+1
.

Proof.

Observe that
(\tau^{\geq h}X^{\bullet}_{n})_{\mathbb{N}}
is a family of injective resolutions for

(S^{h}(X^{h}_{n}/B^{h}(X_{n}^{\bullet})))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. Hence, the
h+i
-th cohomology of
\prod_{\mathbb{N}}\tau^{\geq h}(X^{\bullet}_{n})
is precisely the
i
-th derived functor
\prod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}^{(i)}(X^{h}_{n}/B^{h}(X_{n}^{\bullet}))
. In particular, by Definition
2.5
, we deduce that
H^{i}\left(\prod_{\mathbb{N}}\tau^{\geq h}X_{n}^{\bullet}\right)=0
, for all
i>h+k
. Moreover, the canonical map
X^{\bullet}_{n}\to\tau^{\geq h}X^{\bullet}_{n}
(for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
) is an isomorphism in all degrees
>h
. Conclude by the first part and Lemma
2.4
.
∎

Combining the above lemma with Lemma
2.3
, we deduce the following:

Proposition 2.7
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a complete (Ab.
4^{*}
)-
k
Abelian category (for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
) with enough injectives, and consider the following sequence in
\mathrm{Ch}^{+}(\mathcal{A})
:

\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K_{2}^{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t_{1}^{\bullet}}
\textstyle{K_{1}^{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t_{0}^{\bullet}}
\textstyle{K^{\bullet}_{0}.}

We suppose that this sequence satisfies the following conditions:

(1)

K^{i}_{n}
is injective for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
(so each
K^{\bullet}_{n}
is
dg
-injective);

(2)

t_{n}^{\bullet}
is a degreewise split-epimorphism, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
;

(3)

there is an integer
h\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
H^{i}(K^{\bullet}_{n})=0
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and all
i\geq h
.

Then,
H^{i}(\varprojlim_{\mathbb{N}}K_{n}^{\bullet})=0
, for all
i>h+k+2
.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.3
and condition (2), there is a short exact sequence

(2.3)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\varprojlim_{\mathbb{N}}K_{n}^{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\prod_{\mathbb{N}}K_{n}^{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\prod_{\mathbb{N}}K_{n}^{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

Furthermore, by Lemma
2.6
and conditions (1) and (3),
H^{i}(\prod_{\mathbb{N}}K_{n}^{\bullet})=0
for all
i\geq h+k+1
. To conclude, just look at the long exact sequence in cohomologies induced by (
2.3
).∎

Let us also record the following useful consequence:

Corollary 2.8
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a complete (Ab.
4^{*}
)-
k
Abelian category (for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
) with enough injectives, and let
E^{\bullet}:=\varprojlim_{\mathbb{N}}E^{\bullet}_{n}
by the limit of the following sequence of bounded below complexes:

\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E_{2}^{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t_{1}^{\bullet}}
\textstyle{E_{1}^{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t_{0}^{\bullet}}
\textstyle{E^{\bullet}_{0}.}

We suppose that this sequence satisfies the following conditions:

(1)

E^{i}_{n}
is injective for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
(so each
E^{\bullet}_{n}
is
dg
-injective);

(2)

t_{n}^{\bullet}
is a degreewise split-epimorphism, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
(i.e., each
t^{\bullet}_{n}
is an
\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{A})
-fibration);

(3)

H^{-n-1}(E_{n}^{\bullet})=0
, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
;

(4)

H^{i}(t_{n}^{\bullet})\colon H^{i}(E_{n+1}^{\bullet})\to H^{i}(E_{n}^{\bullet})
is an isomorphism, for all
i\geq-n
(with
i\in\mathbb{Z}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
).

Then, for each
h\in\mathbb{N}
,
H^{i}(E^{\bullet})\cong H^{i}(E_{h}^{\bullet})
, for all
i\geq-h+k+3
.

Proof.

Fix an
h\in\mathbb{N}
and, for each
n>h
, consider the composition

c_{n}^{\bullet}:=t_{h}^{\bullet}\circ\ldots\circ t_{n-1}^{\bullet}\colon E^{\bullet}_{n}\to E_{h}^{\bullet}.

By (2), each
c_{n}^{\bullet}
is a degreewise split-epimorphism, so
K^{\bullet}_{n}:=\mathrm{Ker}(c_{n}^{\bullet})
is
dg
-injective and it fits into a degreewise split-exact sequence:

0\to K_{n}^{\bullet}\to E^{\bullet}_{n}\to E_{h}^{\bullet}\to 0
.
Consider the associated long exact sequence of cohomologies and deduce by (3) and (4) that
H^{i}(K_{n}^{\bullet})=0
, for all
i\geq-h
. Taking the limit for
n>h
, we get the following short exact sequence:

(2.4)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\varprojlim_{n>h}K_{n}^{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E^{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E_{h}^{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

where the central term is
E^{\bullet}
since
\mathbb{N}_{>h}
is cofinal in
\mathbb{N}
. By Proposition
2.7
,
H^{i}(\varprojlim_{\mathbb{N}}K_{n}^{\bullet})=0
, for all
i>-h+k+2
. Conclude by looking at the long cohomology sequence associated to (
2.4
).
∎

Finally, we are able to prove the main result of this section:

Theorem 2.9
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a complete (Ab.
4^{*}
)-
k
(for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
) Abelian category with enough injectives and let
X^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
. Then, there is a Spaltenstein tower of partial resolutions of
X^{\bullet}
:

\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\tau^{\geq-2}(X^{\bullet})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{2}^{\bullet}}
\textstyle{\tau^{\geq-1}(X^{\bullet})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{1}^{\bullet}}
\textstyle{\tau^{\geq 0}(X^{\bullet})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{0}^{\bullet}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t_{2}^{\bullet}}
\textstyle{E^{\bullet}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t_{1}^{\bullet}}
\textstyle{E^{\bullet}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t_{0}^{\bullet}}
\textstyle{E^{\bullet}_{0}.}

Define
E^{\bullet}:=\varprojlim_{\mathbb{N}}E^{\bullet}_{n}
, and let
\lambda^{\bullet}:=\varprojlim_{\mathbb{N}}\lambda_{n}^{\bullet}\colon X^{\bullet}\cong\varprojlim_{\mathbb{N}}\tau^{\geq-n}(X^{\bullet})\to E^{\bullet}
be the induced map. Then,
\lambda^{\bullet}\colon X^{\bullet}\to E^{\bullet}
is a
dg
-injective resolution.

Proof.

The existence of Spaltenstein towers follows by Proposition
2.2
. Furthermore, by Proposition
1.3
(2),
E^{\bullet}
is
dg
-injective, so it is enough to prove that
\lambda^{\bullet}
is a quasi-isomorphism. Observe that, for each
h\in\mathbb{N}
, Corollary
2.8
implies that
H^{i}(E^{\bullet})\cong H^{i}(E_{h}^{\bullet})\cong H^{i}(X^{\bullet})
, for all
i\geq-h+k+3
. As this holds for all
h\in\mathbb{N}
, we conclude that
H^{i}(E^{\bullet})\cong H^{i}(X^{\bullet})
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.
∎

3.
The “go-to” counter-example for unbounded resolutions

The goal of this section is to construct a suitable bicomplete Abelian category with enough injectives
\mathcal{G}
(that, in fact, will be a Grothendieck category) which is not (Ab.
4^{*}
)-
k
for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and a particular unbounded complex
X^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G})
such that the construction discussed in Section
2
fails to produce a
dg
-injective resolution for
X^{\bullet}
, showing that the (Ab.
4^{*}
)-
k
condition is needed for that construction.
As we will see in Sections
4
,
5
and
6
, the complex
X^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G})
can be used to show that several other constructions of
dg
-injective resolutions fail in general.

3.1.
Construction of the category
\mathcal{G}

We start recalling Nagata’s construction
[
21
, Example 1, Appendix A.1]
of a commutative Noetherian ring of infinite Krull dimension. Let
\mathbf{k}
be a field, consider the polynomial ring on countably many variables

\mathbf{k}[\underline{x}]:=\mathbf{k}[x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},\ldots],

and the following sequence of prime ideals in
\mathbf{k}[\underline{x}]
:

\mathfrak{p}_{1}:=(x_{0},x_{1}),\ \mathfrak{p}_{2}:=(x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}),\ \mathfrak{p}_{3}:=(x_{5},x_{6},x_{7},x_{8}),\ \ldots

where the depth of
\mathfrak{p}_{i}
is
i+1
, for all
i\geq 1
. Let
S
be the multiplicative set of those elements of
\mathbf{k}[\underline{x}]
which are not in any of the
\mathfrak{p}_{i}
’s, and define
R
as the ring of
S
-fractions of
\mathbf{k}[\underline{x}]
, that is,

S:=\mathbf{k}[\underline{x}]\setminus\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathfrak{p}_{i}\quad\text{and}\quad R:=S^{-1}(\mathbf{k}[\underline{x}]).

Lemma 3.1

(
[
21
]
)
.

With the above notation,
R
is a commutative Noetherian ring of infinite Krull dimension. In fact, the maximal ideals of
R
are of the form
\mathfrak{m}_{i}:=S^{-1}\mathfrak{p}_{i}
, with
i\geq 1
, and this is a sequence of ideals of strictly increasing height.

Consider the hereditary torsion class
\mathcal{T}:=\mathrm{Loc}(R/\mathfrak{m}_{i}:i\geq 1)
generated by the simple
R
-modules (that is, the first layer of the Gabriel filtration of
\mathrm{Mod}(R)
) and define

\mathcal{G}:=\mathrm{Mod}(R)/\mathcal{T}

as the Gabriel quotient of
\mathrm{Mod}(R)
over
\mathcal{T}
, identified with the Giraud subcategory of
\mathrm{Mod}(R)
of those
R
-modules
M
such that both
M
and
E(M)/M
belong to

\mathcal{F}:=\{R/\mathfrak{m}_{i}:i\geq 1\}^{\perp}=\{M\in\mathrm{Mod}(R):\mathrm{Hom}_{R}(R/\mathfrak{m}_{i},M)=0,\text{ for all $i\geq 1$}\}\subseteq\mathrm{Mod}(R).

It is well-known that
\mathcal{G}
is a Grothendieck category. Moreover, the inclusion
\iota\colon\mathcal{G}\to\mathrm{Mod}(R)
has an exact left adjoint
\mathbf{Q}\colon\mathrm{Mod}(R)\to\mathcal{G}
such that

\mathrm{Ker}(\mathbf{Q}):=\{M\in\mathrm{Mod}(R):\mathbf{Q}(M)=0\}=\mathcal{T}
.
The product
\mathrlap{\text{\tiny\hskip 4.3pt$\mathcal{G}$}}\prod_{I}X_{i}
in
\mathcal{G}
of a family
(X_{i})_{I}\subseteq\mathcal{G}
can be computed as
\mathbf{Q}(\mathrlap{\text{\tiny\hskip 3.5pt$R$}}\prod_{I}X_{i})
, where
\mathrlap{\text{\tiny\hskip 3.5pt$R$}}\prod
denotes the product in the category
\mathrm{Mod}(R)
.

The injective objects in
\mathcal{G}
are exactly the injective
R
-modules in
\mathcal{F}
. Furthermore, as
R
is commutative Noetherian, it is well-known (see
[
33
, Chap. VII, Proposition 4.5]
) that
\mathcal{T}
is stable (i.e., closed under taking injective envelopes in
\mathrm{Mod}(R)
). As a consequence, any injective module
E\in\mathrm{Mod}(R)
decomposes as

E\cong t(E)\oplus\mathbf{Q}(E),

where
t(E)
is an injective module in
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathbf{Q}(E)
is an injective object in
\mathcal{G}
. Note also that, if we denote by
\mathrm{Sp}(R)
the prime ideal spectrum of
R
, and by
\mathrm{MSp}(R)=\{\mathfrak{m}_{i}:i\in\mathbb{N}\}\subseteq\mathrm{Sp}(R)
the maximal ideal spectrum, by the classification of injectives over commutative Noetherian rings given by Matlis
[
20
]
, we have that
E\cong\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{p}\in\mathrm{Sp}(R)}E(R/\mathfrak{p})^{(I_{\mathfrak{p}})}
(for suitable sets
I_{\mathfrak{p}}
), so that

t(E)\cong\bigoplus_{i=1}^{\infty}E(R/\mathfrak{m}_{i})^{(I_{\mathfrak{m}_{i}})}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\mathbf{Q}(E)\cong\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{p}\in\mathrm{Sp}(R)\setminus\mathrm{MSp}(R)}E(R/\mathfrak{p})^{(I_{\mathfrak{p}})}.

3.2.
The complex
X^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G})

With the usual abuse of notation, let
\mathbf{Q}\colon\mathrm{Ch}(\mathrm{Mod}(R))\to\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G})
be the functor on cochain complexes obtained by applying
\mathbf{Q}
componentwise. Observe that, as
\mathbf{Q}
is exact, it commutes with cohomologies, that is, given
X^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathrm{Mod}(R))
, we have:

H^{i}_{\mathcal{G}}(\mathbf{Q}(X^{\bullet}))\cong\mathbf{Q}(H^{i}_{R}(X^{\bullet})),\quad\text{for all $i\in\mathbb{Z}$,}

where
H^{i}_{\mathcal{G}}(-)
and
H^{i}_{R}(-)
denote the
i
-th cohomology in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G})
and in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathrm{Mod}(R))
, respectively.

Let
\mu\colon R\to(\iota\circ\mathbf{Q})(R)
be the component at
R
of the unit of the adjunction
\mathbf{Q}\dashv\iota\colon\mathrm{Mod}(R)\rightleftarrows\mathcal{G}
and fix an injective resolution
\lambda\colon R\to E^{\bullet}
of
R
in
\mathrm{Mod}(R)
. In the language of
[
3
]
, the composition
\mathbf{Q}(\lambda)\circ\mu\colon R\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathbf{Q}(R)\to\mathbf{Q}(E^{\bullet})
is a relative
(\mathcal{F}\cap\mathrm{Inj}(\mathrm{Mod}(R))
-injective resolution. By
[
3
, Lemma 8.2]
,
H^{0}(\mathbf{Q}(E^{\bullet}))\cong R
, that is,
\mu
is an isomorphism. As a consequence:

Lemma 3.2

(
[
3
, Lemma 8.2]
)
.

Let
\lambda\colon R\to E^{\bullet}:=(E^{0}\overset{e^{0}}{\longrightarrow}E^{1}\overset{e^{1}}{\longrightarrow}\dots\overset{}{\longrightarrow}E^{n}\overset{e^{n}}{\longrightarrow}\dots)
be an injective resolution of the right
R
-module
R_{R}
and consider
\mathbf{Q}(E^{\bullet})\in\mathrm{Ch}^{\geq 0}(\mathrm{Mod}(R))
. Then,

H_{R}^{i}(\mathbf{Q}(E^{\bullet}))\cong\begin{cases}R&\text{if $i=0$;}\\
E(R/\mathfrak{m}_{i})&\text{otherwise;}\end{cases}\ \ \text{and}\ \ H_{\mathcal{G}}^{i}(\mathbf{Q}(E^{\bullet}))\cong\begin{cases}\mathbf{Q}(R)\cong R&\text{if $i=0$;}\\
\mathbf{Q}(E(R/\mathfrak{m}_{i}))=0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

In particular,
\mathbf{Q}(\lambda)\colon R\to\mathbf{Q}(E^{\bullet})
is an injective resolution in
\mathcal{G}
.

We are now ready to introduce the central example for our paper. Indeed, consider:

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 31.57675pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-31.57675pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrlap{\text{\tiny\hskip 3.5pt$R$}}\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}S^{i}(R)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces:=\ \big(\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 6.75pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 13.75694pt\raise 5.25555pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.25555pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{0}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 30.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 30.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{R\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 51.42708pt\raise 5.25555pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.25555pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{0}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 68.42014pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 68.42014pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{R\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 89.09721pt\raise 5.25555pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.25555pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{0}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 106.09027pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 106.09027pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 126.59721pt\raise 5.25555pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.25555pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{0}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 143.59027pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 143.59027pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{R\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 164.26735pt\raise 5.25555pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.25555pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{0}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 181.2604pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 181.2604pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\cdots}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\big)\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathrm{Mod}(R)).

As in Lemma
3.2
, let
E^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathrm{Mod}(R))
be an injective resolution of
R
, viewed as a complex concentrated in non-negative degrees. Then,
\mathrlap{\text{\tiny\hskip 3.5pt$R$}}\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\Sigma^{i}E^{\bullet}
is a
dg
-injective resolution of
\mathrlap{\text{\tiny\hskip 3.5pt$R$}}\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}S^{i}(R)
. Our example is the complex

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 86.01616pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-86.01616pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X^{\bullet}:=\mathbf{Q}(\mathrlap{\text{\tiny\hskip 3.5pt$R$}}\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}S^{i}(R))\cong\mathrlap{\text{\tiny\hskip 4.3pt$\mathcal{G}$}}\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}S^{i}(R)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G}).

Proposition 3.3
.

In the above setting, let
\lambda\colon R\to E^{\bullet}
be an injective resolution in
\mathrm{Mod}(R)
. Then,

(1)

\mathrlap{\text{\tiny\hskip 3.5pt$R$}}\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\Sigma^{i}\lambda\colon\mathrlap{\text{\tiny\hskip 3.5pt$R$}}\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}S^{i}(R)\to\mathrlap{\text{\tiny\hskip 3.5pt$R$}}\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\Sigma^{i}E^{\bullet}
is a quasi-isomorphism in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathrm{Mod}(R))
;

(2)

\mathrlap{\text{\tiny\hskip 4.3pt$\mathcal{G}$}}\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\Sigma^{i}\mathbf{Q}(\lambda)\colon X^{\bullet}\to\mathrlap{\text{\tiny\hskip 4.3pt$\mathcal{G}$}}\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\Sigma^{i}\mathbf{Q}(E^{\bullet})
is not a quasi-isomorphism in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G})
.

Proof.

(1) Since
\mathrm{Mod}(R)
is (Ab.
4^{*}
), the class of quasi-isomorphisms is closed under products.

(2) Take the mapping cone
Z^{\bullet}:=\mathrm{cone}(\mathrlap{\text{\tiny\hskip 4.3pt$\mathcal{G}$}}\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\Sigma^{i}\mathbf{Q}(\lambda))
. By the proof of
[
3
, Theorem 8.4]
, viewing
Z^{\bullet}
as a complex in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathrm{Mod}(R))
, one has
H_{R}^{n}(Z^{\bullet})\cong\prod_{j=1}^{\infty}E(R/\mathfrak{m}_{j})\notin\mathcal{T}
, for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Hence,
H_{\mathcal{G}}^{n}(Z^{\bullet})\cong\mathbf{Q}(H_{R}^{n}(Z^{\bullet}))\neq 0
, showing that
Z^{\bullet}
is not exact as a complex in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G})
.
∎

Observe that, as
\mathcal{G}
is a Grothendieck category, our complex
X^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G})
is known to have a
dg
-injective resolution (see, e.g.,
[
2
]
or
[
15
]
). On the other hand, the following corollary shows that the construction of Section
2
fails to produce a
dg
-injective resolution for
X^{\bullet}
:

Corollary 3.4
.

In the above setting, the following statements hold for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
:

(1)

\tau^{\geq-n}(X^{\bullet})=\mathrlap{\text{\tiny\hskip 4.3pt$\mathcal{G}$}}\prod_{i\geq-n}S^{i}(R)
;

(2)

the diagonal
\tau^{\geq-n}(X^{\bullet})\to\mathrlap{\text{\tiny\hskip 4.3pt$\mathcal{G}$}}\prod_{i\leq n}\Sigma^{i}(\mathbf{Q}(E^{\bullet}))
is a
dg
-injective resolution;

(3)

the kernel of the projection
\mathrlap{\text{\tiny\hskip 4.3pt$\mathcal{G}$}}\prod_{i\leq n+1}\Sigma^{i}(\mathbf{Q}(E^{\bullet}))\to\mathrlap{\text{\tiny\hskip 4.3pt$\mathcal{G}$}}\prod_{i\leq n}\Sigma^{i}(\mathbf{Q}(E^{\bullet}))
is
dg
-injective;

(4)

the diagonal map
X^{\bullet}\to\mathrlap{\text{\tiny\hskip 4.3pt$\mathcal{G}$}}\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\Sigma^{i}(\mathbf{Q}(E^{\bullet}))
is the limit of a Spaltenstein tower of partial resolutions of
X^{\bullet}
, but it is not a
dg
-injective resolution of
X^{\bullet}
.

Proof.

(1) is trivial while (2) follows since, even if we are considering infinite products, they are degreewise finite products, so they can be seen also as coproducts (which are exact in
\mathcal{G}
), showing that the map in the statement is a quasi-isomorphism. Moreover,
\mathrlap{\text{\tiny\hskip 4.3pt$\mathcal{G}$}}\prod_{i\leq n}\Sigma^{i}(\mathbf{Q}(E^{\bullet}))
is
dg
-injective by Proposition
1.3
(1). Part (3) is also trivial since the kernel of the projection in the statement is
\Sigma^{n+1}(\mathbf{Q}(E^{\bullet}))
. Finally, part (4) follows by (1–3) and Proposition
3.3
(2).
∎

4.
Cartan-Eilenberg resolutions and their totalization

In this section we recall the construction of the Cartan-Eilenberg resolution of a complex and we show that its totalization, when applied to the example of Section
3
fails to produce a
dg
-injective resolution. On the other hand, as for Spaltenstein’s approach, Cartan-Eilenberg resolutions produce
dg
-injective resolutions, provided the base category is (Ab.
4^{*}
)-
k
, for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

4.1.
Bicomplexes and their totalizations

As a Cartan-Eilenberg resolution is, actually, a bicomplex, we start recalling that a
bicomplex

C^{\bullet,\bullet}
over an Abelian category
\mathcal{A}
is given by

•

a family of objects
C^{\bullet,\bullet}:=(C^{n,m})_{(n,m)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}}
;

•

differentials

d_{0}^{\bullet,\bullet}\colon C^{\bullet,\bullet}\to C^{\bullet,\bullet+1}
and
d_{1}^{\bullet,\bullet}\colon C^{\bullet,\bullet}\to C^{\bullet+1,\bullet}
, called
horizontal
and
vertical
, respectively, that satisfy the following formulas for all
(n,m)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}
:

d_{0}^{n,m+1}\circ d_{0}^{n,m}=0,\ d_{1}^{n+1,m}\circ d_{1}^{n,m}=0,\ \text{and}\ d_{1}^{n,m+1}\circ d_{0}^{n,m}+d_{0}^{n+1,m}\circ d_{1}^{n,m}=0.

In other words, a bicomplex
C^{\bullet,\bullet}
is represented by the following diagram

(4.1)

\textstyle{\vdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\vdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\vdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C^{n,m-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{1}^{n,m-1}}
\scriptstyle{d_{0}^{n,m-1}}
\textstyle{C^{n,m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{0}^{n,m}}
\scriptstyle{d_{1}^{n,m}}
\textstyle{C^{n,m+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{1}^{n,m+1}}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C^{n+1,m-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{0}^{n+1,m-1}}
\textstyle{C^{n+1,m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{0}^{n+1,m}}
\textstyle{C^{n+1,m+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}

where all rows and columns are cochain complexes, and all the little squares anticommute. With reference to the representation (
4.1
), we say that
C^{\bullet,\bullet}
is a
lower half-plane
bicomplex provided
C^{n,m}=0
for all
n<0
,
m\in\mathbb{Z}
. A
morphism of bicomplexes
is just a family of morphisms
\phi^{\bullet,\bullet}\colon X^{\bullet,\bullet}\to Y^{\bullet,\bullet}
that commutes both with horizontal and vertical differentials. We denote by
\mathrm{bCh}(\mathcal{A})
the category of bicomplexes over
\mathcal{A}
.

In fact,
\mathrm{bCh}(\mathcal{A})
is equivalent to the category
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A}))
obtained as follows: start with
\mathcal{A}
and form the category of cochain complexes
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
, which is itself an Abelian category; iterating the construction once more, one gets the category
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A}))
of
double complexes
, where a double complex can be represented by a diagram like (
4.1
), where all rows and columns are cochain complexes but the small squares actually commute. The canonical functor
\mathrm{bCh}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathrm{Ch}(\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A}))
is constructed via the following
sign trick
: given
C^{\bullet,\bullet}\in\mathrm{bCh}(\mathcal{A})
take, for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
, the complex

C_{0}^{\bullet,m}=(\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 6.75pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 26.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 26.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C_{0}^{n-1,m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 60.81859pt\raise 6.99501pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.24501pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{(-1)^{m}d_{1}^{n-1,m}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C_{0}^{n,m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 138.36641pt\raise 6.5pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{(-1)^{m}d_{1}^{n,m}}$}}}\kern
