0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\cdots}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces)

Then, the horizontal differentials of
C^{\bullet,\bullet}
induce chain maps
C_{0}^{\bullet,m}\to C_{0}^{\bullet,m+1}
for all
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, so that

C^{\bullet,\bullet}_{0}:=\quad(\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 6.75pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 30.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 30.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C_{0}^{\bullet,m-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 63.11957pt\raise 6.62001pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.62001pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{d_{0}^{\bullet,m-1}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 89.2503pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 89.2503pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C_{0}^{\bullet,m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 117.82129pt\raise 6.24155pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.24155pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{d_{0}^{\bullet,m}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 141.07004pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 141.07004pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C_{0}^{\bullet,m+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 175.49515pt\raise 6.67558pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.67558pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{d_{0}^{\bullet,m+1}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 203.0148pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 203.0148pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\cdots}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces)\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})).

In particular, to
C^{\bullet,\bullet}\in\mathrm{bCh}(\mathcal{A})
we associate, for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the complex of
n
-coboundaries
B^{n}(C^{\bullet,\bullet}_{0})
, that of
n
-cocycles
Z^{n}(C^{\bullet,\bullet}_{0})
and that of
n
-cohomology
H^{n}(C^{\bullet,\bullet}_{0})
, which are all objects of
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
.

Finally, if
\mathcal{A}
is complete, we can also consider the
totalization
of the bicomplex
C^{\bullet,\bullet}
. This is the cochain complex
\mathrm{Tot}(C^{\bullet,\bullet})\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
, defined by letting
\mathrm{Tot}(C^{\bullet,\bullet})^{n}:=\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}C^{i,n-i}
, for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, and with the
n
-th differential
\partial^{n}:=(\partial^{n}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\colon\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}C^{i,n-i}\to\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}C^{i,n+1-i}
, where
\partial^{n}_{i}
is
(d_{0}^{i,n-i},\,d_{1}^{i,n-i})^{t}\colon C^{i,n-i}\to C^{i,n+1-i}\times C^{i+1,n-i}
composed with the obvious projection and inclusion.

4.2.
Classical Cartan-Eilenberg resolutions

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an Abelian category with enough injectives, and consider a (possibly unbounded) complex

A^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
. An
injective Cartan-Eilenberg resolution
or, more briefly, a
ce
-resolution
of
A^{\bullet}
is given by a lower half-plane bicomplex
C^{\bullet,\bullet}
of injectives, and a homomorphism of complexes
\lambda^{\bullet}\colon A^{\bullet}\to C^{0,\bullet}
:

\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A^{n-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a^{n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lambda^{n-1}}
\textstyle{A^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a^{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lambda^{n}}
\textstyle{A^{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a^{n+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lambda^{n+1}}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C^{0,n-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{1}^{0,n-1}}
\scriptstyle{d_{0}^{0,n-1}}
\textstyle{C^{0,n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{0}^{0,n}}
\scriptstyle{d_{1}^{0,n}}
\textstyle{C^{0,n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{0}^{0,n+1}}
\scriptstyle{d_{1}^{0,n+1}}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C^{1,n-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{0}^{1,n-1}}
\scriptstyle{d_{1}^{1,n-1}}
\textstyle{C^{1,n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{0}^{1,n}}
\scriptstyle{d_{1}^{1,n}}
\textstyle{C^{1,n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{0}^{1,n+1}}
\scriptstyle{d_{1}^{1,n+1}}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}

that satisfy the following properties:

(
ce
.
1
)

for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, if
A^{n}=0
, then the column
C^{\bullet,n}
is constantly
0
;

(
ce
.
2
)

B^{n}(\lambda^{\bullet})\colon B^{n}(A^{\bullet})\to B^{n}(C^{\bullet,\bullet}_{0})
is an injective resolution of
B^{n}(A^{\bullet})
, for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
;

(
ce
.
3
)

H^{n}(\lambda^{\bullet})\colon H^{n}(A^{\bullet})\to H^{n}(C^{\bullet,\bullet}_{0})
is an injective resolution of
H^{n}(A^{\bullet})
, for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

If these three properties hold true, then one also gets the following two extra properties (which are sometimes included in the definition) for free (see
[
36
, Exercise 5.7.1]
):

(
ce
.
4
)

Z^{n}(\lambda^{\bullet})\colon Z^{n}(A^{\bullet})\to Z^{n}(C^{\bullet,\bullet}_{0})
is an injective resolution of
Z^{n}(A^{\bullet})
, for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
;

(
ce
.
5
)

\lambda^{n}\colon A^{n}\to C_{0}^{\bullet,n}
is an injective resolution of
A^{n}
, for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

It is well-known (see, e.g.,
[
36
, Lemma 5.7.2]
) that any complex
A^{\bullet}
admits a
ce
-resolution. Let us briefly go through the (dual of the) construction given by Weibel. The whole process is based on the Horseshoe Lemma, so let us briefly recall it here:

Lemma 4.1

(Horseshoe Lemma)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an Abelian category and consider the following solid diagram, whose first and third row are injective resolutions of
X
and
Z
, respectively, the columns are exact, with
\iota^{n}
and
\pi^{n}
(
n\in\mathbb{N}
) being the obvious inclusions and projections:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E^{0}_{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota^{0}}
\textstyle{E_{X}^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota^{1}}
\textstyle{E_{X}^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota^{2}}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E^{0}_{X}\oplus E^{0}_{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{0}}
\textstyle{E_{X}^{1}\oplus E^{1}_{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{1}}
\textstyle{E_{X}^{2}\oplus E^{2}_{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{2}}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E^{0}_{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E_{Z}^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E_{Z}^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0}

One can then complete the second row to an injective resolution, so that the diagram commutes.

To construct a
ce
-resolution of
A^{\bullet}
, one proceeds as follows:

(step.1)

for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, fix injective resolutions
\lambda_{B^{n}}\colon B^{n}(A^{\bullet})\to E_{B^{n}}^{\bullet}
and
\lambda_{H^{n}}\colon H^{n}(A^{\bullet})\to E_{H^{n}}^{\bullet}
;

(step.2)

for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, use the Horseshoe Lemma to combine these resolutions along the sequence
0\to B^{n}(A^{\bullet})\to Z^{n}(A^{\bullet})\to H^{n}(A^{\bullet})\to 0
, to get a new resolution
\lambda_{Z^{n}}\colon Z^{n}(A^{\bullet})\to E_{Z^{n}}^{\bullet}
;

(step.3)

for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, use the Horseshoe Lemma to combine the previous injective resolutions along
0\to Z^{n}(A^{\bullet})\to A^{n}\to B^{n+1}(A^{\bullet})\to 0
, to get a new injective resolution
\lambda_{A^{n}}\colon A^{n}\to E_{A^{n}}^{\bullet}
;

(step.4)

for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
, let
C^{m,n}:=E_{A^{n}}^{m}
, with
d_{1}^{\bullet,n}
using the differentials of
E_{A^{n}}^{\bullet}
multiplied by
(-1)^{n}
, build
d_{0}^{\bullet,n}
as a composition
E_{A^{n}}^{\bullet}\twoheadrightarrow E^{\bullet}_{B^{n+1}}\hookrightarrow E_{Z^{n+1}}^{\bullet}\hookrightarrow E_{A^{n+1}}^{\bullet}
of the projection and embeddings given by the Horseshoe Lemma, and let
\lambda^{\bullet}\colon A^{\bullet}\to C^{0,\bullet}
be
\lambda^{n}:=\lambda_{A^{n}}
.

By
[
36
, Lemma 5.7.2]
, these
C^{\bullet,\bullet}
and
\lambda^{\bullet}\colon A^{\bullet}\to C^{0,\bullet}
are a
ce
-resolution. Furthermore, the following lemma is a consequence of the dual of
[
36
, Exercise 5.7.1]
:

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a complete Abelian category with enough injectives,
A^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}^{+}(\mathcal{A})
, and take a
ce
-resolution
\lambda^{\bullet}\colon A^{\bullet}\to C^{\bullet,\bullet}
. Then,
\mathrm{Tot}(\lambda^{\bullet})\colon A^{\bullet}\to\mathrm{Tot}(C^{\bullet,\bullet})
is a quasi-isomorphism.

Observe that, in the setting of the above lemma,
\mathrm{Tot}(C^{\bullet,\bullet})
is degreewise injective and, by (
ce
.
1
), this complex is bounded below. Hence, by Proposition
1.3
,
\mathrm{Tot}(\lambda^{\bullet})\colon A^{\bullet}\to\mathrm{Tot}(C^{\bullet,\bullet})
is a
dg
-injective resolution. Therefore, the totalization of
ce
-resolutions provides a valid alternative to the construction in Subsection
2.1
.

4.3.
ce
-resolutions and the (Ab.
4^{*}
)-
k
condition

In
[
36
, Exercise 5.7.1]
, Weibel suggests that Lemma
4.2
works for unbounded complexes, provided
\mathcal{A}
is (Ab.
4^{*}
). In fact, this assumption can be weakened to (Ab.
4^{*}
)-
k
:

Theorem 4.3
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a complete Abelian category with enough injectives, let
A^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
be a complex, and consider a
ce
-resolution
\lambda^{\bullet}\colon A^{\bullet}\to C^{\bullet,\bullet}
. If
\mathcal{A}
is (Ab.
4^{*}
)-
k
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the induced map
A^{\bullet}\to\mathrm{Tot}(C^{\bullet,\bullet})
is a
dg
-injective resolution of
A^{\bullet}
.

Proof.

By definition of
ce
-resolution, we get injective resolutions
\lambda_{B^{n}}\colon B^{n}(A^{\bullet})\to E_{B^{n}}^{\bullet}:=B^{n}(C^{\bullet,\bullet}_{0})
and
\lambda_{H^{n}}\colon H^{n}(A^{\bullet})\to E_{H^{n}}^{\bullet}:=H^{n}(C^{\bullet,\bullet}_{0})
, for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, so that
C^{n,m}\cong E_{B^{n}}^{m}\oplus E_{H^{n}}^{m}\oplus E_{B^{n+1}}^{m}
, for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Observe that, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a
ce
-resolution
\lambda_{n}^{\bullet}\colon\tau^{\geq-n}(A^{\bullet})\to C_{\geq-n}^{\bullet,\bullet}
, where
C_{\geq-n}^{\bullet,i}=C^{\bullet,i}
for all
i>-n
,
C_{\geq-n}^{\bullet,i}=0
for all
i<n
, and
C_{\geq-n}^{\bullet,n}=E_{H^{n}}^{m}\oplus E_{B^{n+1}}^{m}
(with differentials induced by those of
C^{\bullet,\bullet}
). Denoting by
\pi_{n}^{\bullet}\colon\tau^{\geq-n-1}A^{\bullet}\to\tau^{\geq-n}(A^{\bullet})
and
\gamma^{\bullet,\bullet}_{n}\colon C_{\geq-n-1}^{\bullet,\bullet}\to C_{\geq-n}^{\bullet,\bullet}
the obvious projections and taking the totalizations, we obtain the following Spaltenstein tower of partial resolutions (see Lemma
4.2
):

\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{\bullet}_{-2}}
\textstyle{\tau^{\geq-2}(A^{\bullet})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{2}^{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{\bullet}_{-1}}
\textstyle{\tau^{\geq-1}(A^{\bullet})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{1}^{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{\bullet}_{0}}
\textstyle{\tau^{\geq 0}(A^{\bullet})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{0}^{\bullet}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Tot}(\gamma_{-2}^{\bullet,\bullet})}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Tot}(C^{\bullet,\bullet}_{\geq-2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Tot}(\gamma_{-1}^{\bullet,\bullet})}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Tot}(C^{\bullet,\bullet}_{\geq-1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Tot}(\gamma_{0}^{\bullet,\bullet})}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Tot}(C^{\bullet,\bullet}_{\geq 0}).}

By Theorem
2.9
, the complex
\varprojlim_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\mathrm{Tot}(C_{\geq-i}^{\bullet,\bullet})\cong\mathrm{Tot}(C^{\bullet,\bullet})
is a
dg
-injective resolution of
A^{\bullet}
.
∎

To understand what can go wrong if we do not assume (Ab.
4^{*}
)-
k
, let us construct a
ce
-resolution for the complex
X^{\bullet}:=\mathrlap{\text{\tiny\hskip 4.3pt$\mathcal{G}$}}\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}S^{i}(R)\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G})
from Section
3
. For this specific complex:
B^{n}(X^{\bullet})=0
and
Z^{n}(X^{\bullet})\cong X^{n}\cong H^{n}(X^{\bullet})\cong R
, for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. As in Lemma
3.2
, we choose an injective resolution

\lambda\colon R\to E^{\bullet}

of
R_{R}\in\mathrm{Mod}(R)
, so that
\mathbf{Q}(E^{\bullet})\in\mathrm{Ch}^{\geq 0}(\mathcal{G})
is an injective resolution of
R\in\mathcal{G}
. Hence, the following is a
ce
-resolution of
X^{\bullet}
:

(4.2)

\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{Q}(\lambda)}
\textstyle{R\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{Q}(\lambda)}
\textstyle{R\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{Q}(\lambda)}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Q}(E^{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{Q}(e^{0})}
\scriptstyle{0}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Q}(E^{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{Q}(e^{0})}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Q}(E^{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{Q}(e^{0})}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Q}(E^{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{Q}(e^{1})}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Q}(E^{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{Q}(e^{1})}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Q}(E^{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{Q}(e^{1})}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}

Totalizing this
ce
-resolution one gets the diagonal map
\mathrlap{\text{\tiny\hskip 4.3pt$\mathcal{G}$}}\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\Sigma^{i}\mathbf{Q}(\lambda)\colon X^{\bullet}\to\mathrlap{\text{\tiny\hskip 4.3pt$\mathcal{G}$}}\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\Sigma^{i}\mathbf{Q}(E^{\bullet})
, which is not a quasi-isomorphism by Proposition
3.3
.

5.
Saneblidze’s construction through multicomplexes

A different construction of general
dg
-injective resolutions for (possibly unbounded) complexes is proposed in
[
28
]
. Given an Abelian category
\mathcal{A}
, which is just supposed to have countable products and enough injectives, and
X^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
, Saneblidze constructs what he calls a homological injective multicomplex
E^{\bullet,\bullet}
(see below for unexplained terminology) whose totalization provides, supposedly, a
dg
-injective resolution for
X^{\bullet}
. In this section, after recalling the necessary background and some details about the construction proposed in
[
28
]
, we show that the same example considered in Section
3
is also a counterexample to this construction.

5.1.
Cohomological injective multicomplexes and resolutions

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an Abelian category. A
multicomplex

C^{\bullet,\bullet}
over
\mathcal{A}
is given by the following data:

•

a family of objects
\mathcal{C}^{\bullet,\bullet}=\{C^{n,m}:(n,m)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}\}
in
\mathcal{A}
;

•

for each
r\in\mathbb{N}
a degree
r
differential
d_{r}^{\bullet,\bullet}\colon C^{\bullet,\bullet}\to C^{\bullet+r,\bullet-r+1}
, such that the following condition is satisfied for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
and all
(n,m)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}

(5.1)

\sum_{r+s=i}d_{s}^{n+r,m-r+1}\circ d_{r}^{n,m}=0.

As we did for bicomplexes in Subsection
4
, we restrict our attention to
lower half-plan multicomplexes

C^{\bullet,\bullet}
, which can be visualized as follows (where labels denote the degree of the arrows)

\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boxed{\text{\tiny$0$}}}
\textstyle{C^{0,m-2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boxed{\text{\tiny$1$}}}
\scriptstyle{\boxed{\text{\tiny$0$}}}
\textstyle{C^{0,m-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boxed{\text{\tiny$2$}}}
\scriptstyle{\boxed{\text{\tiny$1$}}}
\scriptstyle{\boxed{\text{\tiny$0$}}}
\textstyle{C^{0,m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boxed{\text{\tiny$0$}}}
\scriptstyle{\boxed{\text{\tiny$1$}}}
\scriptstyle{\boxed{\text{\tiny$3$}}}
\scriptstyle{\boxed{\text{\tiny$2$}}}

\scriptstyle{\boxed{\text{\tiny$3$}}}
\scriptstyle{\boxed{\text{\tiny$2$}}}
\scriptstyle{\boxed{\text{\tiny$0$}}}
\scriptstyle{\boxed{\text{\tiny$1$}}}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boxed{\text{\tiny$0$}}}
\textstyle{C^{1,m-2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boxed{\text{\tiny$0$}}}
\scriptstyle{\boxed{\text{\tiny$1$}}}
\textstyle{C^{1,m-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boxed{\text{\tiny$2$}}}
\scriptstyle{\boxed{\text{\tiny$0$}}}
\scriptstyle{\boxed{\text{\tiny$1$}}}
\textstyle{C^{1,m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boxed{\text{\tiny$2$}}}
\scriptstyle{\boxed{\text{\tiny$0$}}}
\scriptstyle{\boxed{\text{\tiny$1$}}}
\textstyle{C^{1,m+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boxed{\text{\tiny$2$}}}
\scriptstyle{\boxed{\text{\tiny$0$}}}
\scriptstyle{\boxed{\text{\tiny$1$}}}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boxed{\text{\tiny$0$}}}
\textstyle{C^{2,m-2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boxed{\text{\tiny$0$}}}
\scriptstyle{\boxed{\text{\tiny$1$}}}
\textstyle{C^{2,m-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boxed{\text{\tiny$0$}}}
\scriptstyle{\boxed{\text{\tiny$1$}}}
\textstyle{C^{2,m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boxed{\text{\tiny$0$}}}
\scriptstyle{\boxed{\text{\tiny$1$}}}
\textstyle{C^{2,m+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boxed{\text{\tiny$0$}}}
\scriptstyle{\boxed{\text{\tiny$1$}}}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boxed{\text{\tiny$0$}}}
\textstyle{C^{3,m-2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boxed{\text{\tiny$0$}}}
\textstyle{C^{3,m-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boxed{\text{\tiny$0$}}}
\textstyle{C^{3,m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boxed{\text{\tiny$0$}}}
\textstyle{C^{3,m+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boxed{\text{\tiny$0$}}}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}

For
i=0
, (
5.1
) means that each row is a cochain complex while, for
i=1
, it says that the little squares anticommute. Moreover, if
d_{0}^{\bullet,\bullet}=0
or
d_{2}^{\bullet,\bullet}=0
, then (
5.1
) for
i=2
says that each column is a cochain complex. Hence, a bicomplex is just a multicomplex such that
d^{\bullet,\bullet}_{r}=0
, for all
r\geq 2
.

A
morphism of multicomplexes

(\phi_{r}^{\bullet,\bullet})_{r\in\mathbb{N}}\colon X^{\bullet,\bullet}\to Y^{\bullet,\bullet}
is specified by a family of morphisms

\phi^{(n,m)}_{r}\colon X^{(n,m)}\to Y^{(n+r,m-r)}

which is compatible with differentials in the following sense:

\sum_{r+s=i}\phi^{(n+r,m-r+1)}_{s}\circ d^{(n,m)}_{r}=\sum_{r+s=i}d^{(n+r,m-r)}_{s}\circ\phi^{(i,j)}_{r}\qquad\text{for all $(n,m)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}$ and $i\in\mathbb{N}$.}

Definition 5.1

(
[
28
]
)
.

A lower half-plane multicomplex of injectives
E^{\bullet,\bullet}
is said to be a
homological injective multicomplex
if:

•

d_{0}^{\bullet,\bullet}=0
(so that each column
E^{\bullet,m}\in\mathrm{Ch}^{\geq 0}(\mathcal{A})
with the degree
1
differentials);

•

for each
m\in\mathbb{Z}
,
H^{i}(E^{\bullet,m})=0
for all
i\geq 1
, that is, the column
E^{\bullet,m}
is an injective resolution of the object
H^{0}(E^{\bullet,m})=\mathrm{Ker}(d_{1}^{0,m})\in\mathcal{A}
.

Finally, if
\mathcal{A}
is complete, given a lower half-plane multicomplex
C^{\bullet,\bullet}
, one can consider its
totalization
, which is a cochain complex
\mathrm{Tot}(C^{\bullet,\bullet})\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
such that
\mathrm{Tot}(C^{\bullet,\bullet})^{i}:=\prod_{j=0}^{\infty}C^{j,i-j}
, for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, where the
n
-th differential is

\partial^{i}:=(\partial^{i}_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\colon\prod_{j\in\mathbb{N}}C^{j,i-j}\longrightarrow\prod_{j\in\mathbb{N}}C^{j,i+1-j},

where
\partial^{i}_{j}
is determined by its components
(d_{0}^{j,i-j},\,d_{1}^{j,i-j},\,d_{2}^{j,i-j},\dots)^{t}\colon C^{j,i-j}\to\prod_{r\in\mathbb{N}}C^{j+r,i+1-j-r}
.

Definition 5.2

(
[
28
]
)
.

A
homological injective resolution
of a complex
X^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
is a morphism of multicomplexes
\lambda=(\lambda^{\bullet}_{r})_{r\in\mathbb{N}}\colon X^{\bullet}\to E^{\bullet,\bullet}
such that
E^{\bullet,\bullet}
is a homological injective multicomplex and
\mathrm{Tot}(\lambda)\colon X^{\bullet}\to\mathrm{Tot}(E^{\bullet,\bullet})
is a quasi-isomorphism.

5.2.
A counterexample for the construction

In
[
28
, Proposition 3]
, Saneblidze states that, if
\mathcal{A}
is a cocomplete Abelian category with enough projectives, then every complex has a homological projective resolution. In the proof of
[
28
, Proposition 3]
one can find a rather involved construction of a homological resolution of an arbitrary complex. Let us recall the first few steps (in the dual setting of homological injective resolutions). Indeed, fix a generic complex
A^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
. To build a homological injective resolution
\lambda=(\lambda^{\bullet}_{r})_{r\in\mathbb{N}}\colon A^{\bullet}\to E^{\bullet,\bullet}
. Consider, for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, an injective resolution
\lambda^{n}\colon H^{n}(A^{\bullet})\to E_{H^{n}}^{\bullet}=(E_{H^{n}}^{0}\to E_{H^{n}}^{1}\to\cdots)
and define:

•

E^{m,n}:=E^{m}_{H^{n}}
for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
;

•

define the differentials of degree
0
of
E^{\bullet,\bullet}
to be trivial;

•

use the differentials of
E_{H^{n}}^{\bullet}
to define the differentials of degree
1
in
E^{\bullet,\bullet}
;

•

let
\lambda^{n}_{0}\colon A^{n}\to E^{0}_{H^{n}}
be any lifting of the composition
Z^{n}(A^{\bullet})\to H^{n}(A^{\bullet})\to E_{H^{n}}^{0}
along the inclusion
Z^{n}(A^{\bullet})\to A^{n}
(which exists by the injectivity of
E_{H^{n}}^{0}
).

Hence, after this first step of the construction, we have all the object components of
E^{\bullet,\bullet}
, its horizontal and vertical differentials, and the
0
-degree component of
\lambda
. As it turns out, since our goal is to apply the construction to the complex
X^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G})
of Section
3
, which is a complex with trivial differentials, we can just stop here with the construction:

Remark 5.3

(
[
28
, pag. 320]
)
.

As observed by Saneblidze right after
[
28
, Proposition 3]
, and as it can be easily checked in his proof, if all the differentials of
A^{\bullet}
are trivial, that is, if
A^{\bullet}\cong\prod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}S^{-n}(A^{n})
, one can take
d^{\bullet,\bullet}_{r}=0
for all
r\geq 2
and
\lambda^{\bullet}_{s}=0
, for all
s\geq 1
. Hence, in this case,
E^{\bullet,\bullet}
is just a bicomplex with trivial horizontal differentials, and
\lambda=\lambda^{\bullet}\colon A^{\bullet}\to E^{0,\bullet}
.

By the above discussion and remark, it is easy to see that the result of applying Saneblidze’s construction to the complex
X^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G})
from Section
3
is the bicomplex we have constructed in (
4.2
) when studying
ce
-resolutions. As we have already seen, the totalization of that bicomplex is not quasi-isomorphic to
X^{\bullet}
and, therefore, Saneblidze’s construction does not produce a homological injective resolution in this case.

6.
Ding and Yang’s construction via repeated killing of coboundaries

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a bicomplete Abelian category,
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
a complete hereditary cotorsion pair in
\mathcal{A}
, and consider the induced cotorsion pairs
(\mathrm{dg}\mathcal{X},\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}})
and
(\widetilde{\mathcal{X}},\mathrm{dg}\mathcal{Y})
in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
, introduced by Gillespie
[
9
]
. In many important cases, these cotorsion pairs are complete, hereditary and compatible, so they give rise to an Abelian model structure in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
. On the other hand, to the best of our knowledge, the following question remains open:

Question 6.1
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a bicomplete Abelian category and let
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
be a complete and hereditary cotorsion pair in
\mathcal{A}
. Are the induced cotorsion pairs
(\mathrm{dg}\mathcal{X},\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}})
and
(\widetilde{\mathcal{X}},\mathrm{dg}\mathcal{Y})
complete in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
?

An attempt to solve this problem in the positive was made by N. Ding and X. Yang. The argument they used is based on
[
37
, Lemma 2.1]
, whose proof contains a very concrete construction, for each
A^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
, of a
Y^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{dg}\mathcal{Y}
which is quasi-isomorphic to
A^{\bullet}
. In particular, if we start with the trivial cotorsion pair
(\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{A}))
in
\mathcal{A}
(assuming that
\mathcal{A}
has enough injectives), this construction should produce a
dg
-injective resolution of
A^{\bullet}
.

In this section we test the proof of
[
37
, Lemma 2.1]
against the complex
X^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G})
from Section
3
, showing that it actually fails to produce the desired resolution. Furthermore, we briefly analyze Ding and Yang’s proof, pointing out a gap in the argument, and showing that the problem completely disappears if one assume that
\mathcal{A}
is (Ab.
4^{*}
).

6.1.
The Ding-Yang construction in concrete situations

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a bicomplete Abelian category with enough injectives, so that the cotorsion pair
(\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{A}))
is complete. Let us start recalling the idea of the proof of
[
37
, Lemma 2.1]
in this very special case. Indeed, the construction of Ding and Yang is based on a very simple operation, of “killing coboundaries” that we have condensed in the following

Construction 6.2

(Killing coboundaries)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an Abelian category with enough injectives, fix a complex
X^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Denote by
\rho^{n}\colon X^{n}\to X^{n}/B^{n}(X^{\bullet})
the obvious projection and by
\iota^{n}\colon X^{n}/B^{n}(X^{\bullet})\to E
an inclusion into an injective object
E\in\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{A})
. Define a new complex:

K{(X^{\bullet},n)}\ :\qquad\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 6.75pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 26.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 26.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X^{n-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 66.52791pt\raise 6.12001pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.12001pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{d^{n-1}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 99.94333pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 73.94333pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 99.94333pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 129.91139pt\raise 12.31052pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\tiny\left[\begin{matrix}{d^{n}}\\
\iota_{n}\rho_{n}\end{matrix}\right]}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 166.45609pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 140.45609pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 166.45609pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X^{n+1}\oplus E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 222.1577pt\raise 6.4782pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.2282pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\tiny\left[\begin{matrix}d^{n+1}&0\end{matrix}\right]}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 261.05215pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 235.05215pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 261.05215pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X^{n+2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 289.8856pt\raise 6.17558pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.17558pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{d^{n+2}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 311.68994pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 311.68994pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\cdots}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

and a morphism of complexes
\pi^{\bullet}_{n}\colon K{(X^{\bullet},n)}\to X^{\bullet}
such that

\pi_{n}^{m}:=\begin{cases}\mathrm{id}_{X^{m}}&\text{for all $m\neq n+1$;}\\
\text{the obvious projection $X^{n+1}\oplus E\to X^{n+1}$}&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Then,
H^{n}(K{(X^{\bullet},n)})=0
,
\pi^{\bullet}_{n}
is a degreewise split-epic, and
\mathrm{Ker}(\pi^{\bullet}_{n})=S^{n+1}(E)
is
dg
-injective.

Observe that
K{(X^{\bullet},n)}
depends on the choice of the embedding
\iota^{n}\colon X^{n}/B^{n}(X^{\bullet})\to E
. In some cases this choice can be made canonical, e.g., whenever
X^{n}/B^{n}(X^{\bullet})
is trivial or, more generally, when it is an injective object, it is natural to take
\iota^{n}:=\mathrm{id}
.
To illustrate the construction in some basic case, take an injective object
E\in\mathcal{A}
and consider the stalk complex
S^{i}(E)
and the disk complex
D^{i}(E)
, for some
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then,

K(S^{i}(E),j)=\begin{cases}S^{i}(E)&\text{if $j\neq i$;}\\
D^{i}(E)&\text{if $j=i$;}\end{cases}\qquad\text{and}\qquad K(D^{i}(E),j)=\begin{cases}D^{i}(E)&\text{if $j\neq i$;}\\
D^{i}(E)\oplus S^{i+1}(E)&\text{if $j=i$.}\end{cases}

In fact, it is not completely trivial to verify that
K(D^{i}(E),i)\cong D^{i}(E)\oplus S^{i+1}(E)
since the
i
-th differential of
K(D^{i}(E),i)
is, a priori, just a triangular (but not diagonal) matrix. In fact, this kind of complications with the differentials can be solved with the following general lemma:

Lemma 6.3
.

Given
X^{\bullet}=(X^{n},d^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
such that, for a given
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
X^{n}\cong Y^{(k)}
is a coproduct of
k
-many copies of a given object
Y\in\mathcal{A}
. If
d^{n-1}=(\varphi,\varphi,\dots,\varphi)^{t}\colon X^{n-1}\to Y^{(k)}
is the diagonal map for some morphism
\varphi\colon X^{n-1}\to Y
. Then, there is an isomorphism of complexes
\phi^{\bullet}\colon X^{\bullet}\to(X^{\prime})^{\bullet}=((X^{\prime})^{n},(d^{\prime})^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
, where
(X^{\prime})^{i}:=X^{i}
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
,
(d^{\prime})^{i}:=d^{i}
for all
i\neq n-1,\ n
,
(d^{\prime})^{n-1}:=(\varphi,0,\dots,0)^{t}
, and
(d^{\prime})^{n}:=(\psi_{1}+\ldots+\psi_{k},\psi_{2},\dots,\psi_{k})
, where
d^{n}=(\psi_{1},\psi_{2},\dots,\psi_{k})\colon Y^{(k)}\to X^{n+1}
.

Proof.

One can just define
\phi^{i}:=\mathrm{id}_{X^{i}}
for all
i\neq n
, and

\phi^{n}:={\tiny\left[\begin{matrix}+\mathrm{id}_{Y}&0&\ldots&0&0\\
-\mathrm{id}_{Y}&\mathrm{id}_{Y}&\ldots&0&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
-\mathrm{id}_{Y}&0&\ldots&\mathrm{id}_{Y}&0\\
-\mathrm{id}_{Y}&0&\ldots&0&\mathrm{id}_{Y}\end{matrix}\right]}\in\mathrm{Mat}_{k\times k}(\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{A}}(Y))\cong\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{A}}(Y^{(k)}).

∎

Consider now a family of complexes
\{X^{\bullet}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
such that

\left|\{n\in\mathbb{N}:X_{n}^{i}\neq 0\}\right|<\infty\ ,\quad\text{ for each $i\in\mathbb{Z}$.}

Observe that, for such a family,
\prod_{\mathbb{N}}X^{\bullet}_{n}=\bigoplus_{\mathbb{N}}X^{\bullet}_{n}
. Hence, letting
Y^{\bullet}:=\prod_{\mathbb{N}}X^{\bullet}_{n}=\bigoplus_{\mathbb{N}}X^{\bullet}_{n}
, we have that
B^{i}(Y^{\bullet})=\bigoplus_{\mathbb{N}}B^{i}(X^{\bullet}_{n})=\prod_{\mathbb{N}}B^{i}(X^{\bullet}_{n})
(where both equalities use that finite products coincide with finite coproducts in any Abelian category and, hence, they are exact). Similarly, one can verify that
Z^{i}(Y^{\bullet})=\prod_{\mathbb{N}}Z^{i}(X^{\bullet}_{n})=\bigoplus_{\mathbb{N}}Z^{i}(X^{\bullet}_{n})
and, combining these two, one even gets
H^{i}(Y^{\bullet})=\prod_{\mathbb{N}}H^{i}(X^{\bullet}_{n})=\bigoplus_{\mathbb{N}}H^{i}(X^{\bullet}_{n})
.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
\{X^{\bullet}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
be a family as above and let
Y^{\bullet}:=\prod_{\mathbb{N}}X^{\bullet}_{n}
. Then, for each
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, one can choose suitable embeddings into injectives in Construction
6.2
so to obtain
K{(Y^{\bullet},i)}=\prod_{\mathbb{N}}K(X_{n}^{\bullet},i)
.

Going back to Ding and Yang’s proof of their Lemma 2.1, they fix an arbitrary surjection
\tau\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{Z}
with
n_{i}:=\tau(i)
, for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
, with the property of having infinite fibers. As we are trying to do some concrete computations, we need to make a choice. Indeed, we fix the following sequence of integers (in which every integer appears infinitely many times, as desired):

\displaystyle n_{0}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle n_{1}=-1,\ n_{2}

\displaystyle=0,\ n_{3}=1,

\displaystyle n_{4}=-2,\ n_{5}=-1,\ n_{6}

\displaystyle=0,\ n_{7}=1,\ n_{8}=2,

\displaystyle n_{9}=-3,\ n_{10}=-2,\ n_{11}=-1,\ n_{12}

\displaystyle=0,\ n_{13}=1,\ n_{14}=2,\ n_{15}=3,

\displaystyle\ldots

Now, starting with our complex
X^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
, the idea of the Ding-Yang construction is to iterate Construction
6.2
in order to build the following sequential inverse system:

\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y^{\bullet}_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{\bullet}_{i}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{\bullet}_{2}}
\textstyle{Y^{\bullet}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{\bullet}_{1}}
\textstyle{Y^{\bullet}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{\bullet}_{0}}
\textstyle{Y^{\bullet}_{-1}:=X^{\bullet}}

in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
,

where
Y^{\bullet}_{i}:=K(Y^{\bullet}_{i-1},n_{i})
and
\pi^{\bullet}_{i}
is the obvious degreewise split-epimorphism with
dg
-injective kernel, for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Defining
Y^{\bullet}:=\varprojlim_{\mathbb{N}}Y^{\bullet}_{i}
, one gets an exact sequence
0\to E^{\bullet}\to Y^{\bullet}\to X^{\bullet}\to 0
in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
, with
E^{\bullet}
a
dg
-injective complex. The claim made in
[
37
]
is that the complex
Y^{\bullet}
is exact, so there is a quasi-isomorphism
X^{\bullet}\to\Sigma E^{\bullet}
, which is precisely the
dg
-injective resolution of
X^{\bullet}
we are looking for. In the rest of this section we will apply this strategy in some easy cases: first with
X^{\bullet}
a stalk complex, and then a product of stalk complexes. As we will see, if the product of stalk complexes
X^{\bullet}
is the complex from Section
3
, then the resulting complex
Y^{\bullet}
is not exact, showing that the proof of
[
37
, Lemma 2.1]
may fail even for the trivial cotorsion pair
(\mathcal{G},\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{G}))
, with
\mathcal{G}
a Grothendieck category.

As a first example, let us apply the Ding-Yang construction to a stalk complex
X=S^{0}(A)
concentrated in degree
0
, for some
A\in\mathcal{A}
. Fix an injective resolution for
A
, and use it to build the following exact complex concentrated in non-negative degrees:

E_{A}^{[0,\infty)}:=(\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 6.75pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 57.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 57.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 71.25002pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 75.62013pt\raise 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 129.69443pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 129.69443pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 150.8159pt\raise 6.12001pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.12001pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\lambda^{2}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 168.13885pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 168.13885pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 189.26033pt\raise 6.12001pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.12001pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\lambda^{3}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 206.58328pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 206.58328pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\cdots).}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

For each
m\in\mathbb{N}
, let
E_{A}^{[0,m]}
be the naive truncation of
E_{A}^{[0,\infty)}
so that, for example,

E_{A}^{[0,0]}=S^{0}(A),\quad E_{A}^{[0,1]}=(\cdots\longrightarrow 0\longrightarrow A\longrightarrow E^{1}\longrightarrow 0\longrightarrow\cdots),\quad\text{ and so on.}

We can now start computing the complexes of the form
Y^{\bullet}_{i}:=K(Y^{\bullet}_{i-1},{n_{i}})
, for
i\in\mathbb{N}
:

(
i=0
):

Y^{\bullet}_{0}=K(S^{0}(A),0)=E^{[0,1]}_{A}
;

(
i=1
):

Y^{\bullet}_{1}=Y^{\bullet}_{0}
;

(
i=2
):

Y^{\bullet}_{2}=E^{[0,1]}_{A}\oplus S^{1}(E^{1})
(by a suitable application of Lemma
6.3
);

(
i=3
):

Y^{\bullet}_{3}=E^{[0,2]}_{A}\oplus D^{1}(E^{1})
(use Lemma
6.4
);

(
i=4,5
):

Y^{\bullet}_{5}=Y^{\bullet}_{4}=Y^{\bullet}_{3}
;

(
i=6
):

Y^{\bullet}_{6}=E^{[0,2]}_{A}\oplus S^{1}(E^{1})\oplus D^{1}(E^{1})
;

(
i=7
):

Y^{\bullet}_{7}=E^{[0,2]}_{A}\oplus D^{1}((E^{1})^{2})\oplus S^{2}(E^{1}\oplus E^{2})
;

(
i=8
):

Y^{\bullet}_{8}=E^{[0,3]}_{A}\oplus D^{1}((E^{1})^{2})\oplus D^{2}(E^{1}\oplus E^{2})
;

(
i=9,10,11
):

Y^{\bullet}_{11}=Y^{\bullet}_{10}=Y^{\bullet}_{9}=Y^{\bullet}_{8}
;

(
i=12
):

Y^{\bullet}_{12}=E^{[0,3]}_{A}\oplus S^{1}(E^{1})\oplus D^{1}((E^{1})^{2})\oplus D^{2}(E^{1}\oplus E^{2})
;

\dots\quad

After computing these first steps it seems clear that the limit
Y^{\bullet}:=\varprojlim(\dots\to Y^{\bullet}_{n}\to\dots\to Y^{\bullet}_{0})
will be the following complex:

\textstyle{Y^{\bullet}\cong E^{[0,\infty)}_{A}\times\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{i}(D^{i}(E^{j}))^{\mathbb{N}}\right).}

In other words,
Y^{\bullet}
is a product of our chosen resolution of
A
(including
A
, so this part is an exact complex) with a bunch of disk complexes, so
Y^{\bullet}
is an exact complex. This means that the Ding-Yang construction works just fine when applied to a single stalk complex.

Suppose now that, instead of starting with a single stalk complex, we start with the complex
X^{\bullet}=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}S^{n}(A)=\prod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}S^{n}(A)
, for some object
A\in\mathcal{A}
, that is:

X^{\bullet}=\ \lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 6.75pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 13.75694pt\raise 5.25555pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.25555pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{0}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 30.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 30.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 68.25pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 68.25pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 105.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 105.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 126.25694pt\raise 5.25555pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.25555pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{0}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 143.25pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 143.25pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 163.75694pt\raise 5.25555pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.25555pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{0}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 180.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 180.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\cdots}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

By Lemma
6.4
, if we choose carefully the embeddings into injective objects, the resulting
Y^{\bullet}
is:

\textstyle{Y^{\bullet}=\prod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\Sigma^{n}\left(E^{[0,\infty)}_{A}\times D^{0}\left(\prod_{j=0}^{\infty}(E^{j})^{\mathbb{N}}\right)\right).}

It is easy to deduce from this description that
Y^{\bullet}
is quasi-isomorphic to
\prod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\Sigma^{n}E^{[0,\infty)}_{A}
; we claim that such a product of exact complexes is not necessarily exact. Indeed, if
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{G}
is the Grothendieck category we have introduced in Section
3
, and if
A:=R\in\mathcal{G}
, then the object
X^{\bullet}:=\mathrlap{\text{\tiny\hskip 4.3pt$\mathcal{G}$}}\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}S^{i}(R)\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G})
is the same complex we have considered in Section
3
. Take the following short exact sequence of complexes:

\textstyle{0\longrightarrow X^{\bullet}\longrightarrow\mathrlap{\text{\tiny\hskip 4.5pt$\mathcal{G}$}}\prod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\Sigma^{n}E^{[0,\infty)}_{R}\longrightarrow\mathrlap{\text{\tiny\hskip 4.5pt$\mathcal{G}$}}\prod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\Sigma^{n}(E^{\bullet})\longrightarrow 0.}

If, looking for a contradiction,
\mathrlap{\text{\tiny\hskip 4.5pt$\mathcal{G}$}}\prod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\Sigma^{n}E^{[0,\infty)}_{R}
was acyclic, all of its cohomologies would be trivial and, analyzing the long exact sequence of cohomologies, this would force isomorphisms
H_{\mathcal{G}}^{i}(\mathrlap{\text{\tiny\hskip 4.2pt$\mathcal{G}$}}\prod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\Sigma^{n}(E^{\bullet}))\cong H_{\mathcal{G}}^{i+1}(X^{\bullet})=R
, for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. By Proposition
3.3
, we know that this is not the case, so
\mathrlap{\text{\tiny\hskip 4.5pt$\mathcal{G}$}}\prod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\Sigma^{n}E^{[0,\infty)}_{R}
cannot be exact in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G})
.

6.2.
The problem in the proof of
[
37
, Lemma 2.1]

As shown in the previous subsection, the proof of
[
37
, Lemma 2.1]
fails in general. For this reason, it may be interesting to identify the concrete problem in Ding and Yang’s argument and, if possible, to find additional hypotheses under which the proposed construction can be made to work. The unique problem we could identify in
[
37
]
is the following: on page 3210 in [Op.Cit.], in the last part of the proof of Lemma 2.1, there is an inverse system (in the notation of [Op.Cit.]):

\cdots\overset{\mu^{i+1}}{\longrightarrow}Y^{i}\overset{\mu^{i}}{\longrightarrow}\cdots\overset{\mu^{2}}{\longrightarrow}Y^{1}\overset{\mu^{1}}{\longrightarrow}Y^{0}\qquad\subseteq\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G})

and an
l\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
H_{l}(Y^{i})=0
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
. The authors want to prove that the following map

\textstyle{1-\nu\colon\prod_{i=0}^{\infty}Y^{i}\longrightarrow\prod_{i=0}^{\infty}Y^{i}}

induces an isomorphism in homology at degree
l
. They call
\pi^{j}\colon\prod_{i=0}^{\infty}Y^{i}\to Y^{j}
the canonical projection, for each
j\geq 0
, they consider
\pi^{j}\circ(1-\nu)=\pi^{j}-\mu^{j+1}\circ\pi^{j+1}
and correctly verify that

(6.1)

\mu_{l}^{j+1}\pi_{l}^{j+1}\left(Z_{l}^{\prod_{i=0}^{\infty}Y^{i}}\right)\subseteq B_{l}^{Y_{j}},\qquad\text{for all $j\in\mathbb{Z}$.}

Unfortunately, (
6.1
) does not imply that

Z_{l}^{\prod_{i=0}^{\infty}Y^{i}}\subseteq B_{l}^{\prod_{i=0}^{\infty}Y^{i}}

but just the weaker inclusion:

Z_{l}^{\prod_{i=0}^{\infty}Y^{i}}\subseteq\prod_{i=0}^{\infty}B_{l}^{Y^{i}}.

In fact, it may happen that
\prod_{i=0}^{\infty}B_{l}^{Y^{i}}\neq B_{l}^{\prod_{i=0}^{\infty}Y^{i}}
(e.g., in the setting of Section
3
): the property “the boundaries of the product coincide with the product of boundaries” (if required for all possible products) is in fact equivalent to the (Ab.
4^{*}
) condition on
\mathcal{G}
. In particular, adding the (Ab.
4^{*}
) condition to the hypotheses, the proof of
[
37
, Lemma 2.1(1)]
works perfectly. We will also obtain the conclusion of
[
37
, Lemma 2.1(1)]
under a different set of hypotheses in Remark
8.6
.

7.
Model structures for relative homological algebra

In this section we combine some of the main results of
[
3
]
about model approximations for relative homological algebra, and a general criterion for the existence of suitable model categories from
[
4
]
to verify that, if
\mathcal{A}
is a bicomplete Abelian category, and
\mathcal{I}
an injective class of objects such that
\mathcal{A}
is (Ab.
4^{*}
)-
\mathcal{I}
-
k
(for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
), then there is an induced
\mathcal{I}
-injective model structure on
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
; in particular, the
\mathcal{I}
-derived category
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A};\mathcal{I})
is locally small.

7.1.
Injective classes

Given a class
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
, a morphism
\phi\colon X\to Y
in
\mathcal{A}
is said to be an
\mathcal{I}
-monomorphism
if

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(\phi,I)\colon\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(Y,I)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(X,I)

is surjective (in
\mathrm{Ab}
), for all
I\in\mathcal{I}
. With this concept at hand, we can now introduce the following:

Definition 7.1
.

A class of objects
\mathcal{I}
in an Abelian category
\mathcal{A}
is said to be an
injective class
if:

(IC.1)

\mathcal{I}
is closed under products and summands;

(IC.2)

for each
A\in\mathcal{A}
there is an
\mathcal{I}
-monomorphism
\phi\colon A\to I
, with
I\in\mathcal{I}
.

Observe that a class
\mathcal{I}
that satisfies the property (IC.2) is usually called
pre-enveloping
. In the following lemma we show that the above definition is slightly redundant, in fact, there is no need to ask that
\mathcal{I}
is closed under products in (IC.1):

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a complete Abelian category. Then, a pre-enveloping class
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
which is closed under direct summands is also closed under products.

Proof.

Consider a set
\{X_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
of objects in
\mathcal{I}
, let
X:=\prod_{\Lambda}X_{\lambda}
and for each
\lambda\in\Lambda
denote by
\pi_{\lambda}\colon X\to X_{\lambda}
the canonical projection. By hypothesis, there is an
\mathcal{I}
-monomorphism
\varphi\colon X\to I
, for some
I\in\mathcal{I}
. Since
X_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{I}
for each
\lambda\in\Lambda
, the following map is surjective:

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(\varphi,X_{\lambda})\colon\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(I,X_{\lambda})\longrightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(X,X_{\lambda}),

so there is
\psi_{\lambda}\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(I,X_{\lambda})
such that
\psi_{\lambda}\circ\varphi=\pi_{\lambda}
. By the universal property of the product, there is a unique
\psi\colon I\to X
such that
\pi_{\lambda}\circ\psi=\psi_{\lambda}
, for all
\lambda\in\Lambda
. Moreover, for each
\lambda\in\Lambda
:

\pi_{\lambda}\circ\psi\circ\varphi=\psi_{\lambda}\circ\varphi=\pi_{\lambda}=\pi_{\lambda}\circ\mathrm{id}_{X}.

By the uniqueness in the universal property of the product,
\psi\circ\varphi=\mathrm{id}_{X}
and, therefore,
X
is a summand of
I\in\mathcal{I}
. Hence,
X\in\mathcal{I}
, as desired.
∎

If
\mathcal{A}
is an Abelian category with enough injectives (e.g., if
\mathcal{A}
is Grothendieck) then
\mathcal{I}:=\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{A})
, the class of all the injective objects in
\mathcal{A}
, is an injective class for which the
\mathcal{I}
-monomorphisms are the usual monomorphisms. We refer to Section
8
for other concrete examples.

7.2.
Relative
\mathcal{I}
-injective resolutions of objects

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an Abelian category. Given
X^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
and
A\in\mathcal{A}
, we define the cochain complex
\mathrm{Hom}(X^{\bullet},A)\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathrm{Ab})
as follows:

•

for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
\mathrm{Hom}(X^{\bullet},A)^{n}:=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(X^{-n},A)
;

•

for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the
(n-1)
-th differential of
\mathrm{Hom}(X^{\bullet},A)
is the following map:

(d^{-n})^{*}\colon\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(X^{-n+1},A)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(X^{-n},A),

such that
(d^{-n})^{*}(f):=f\circ d^{-n}
, for all
f\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(X^{-n+1},A)
.

Moreover, any morphism
\phi^{\bullet}\colon X^{\bullet}\to Y^{\bullet}
in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
, induces the following morphism in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathrm{Ab})
:

\mathrm{Hom}(\phi^{\bullet},A)\colon\mathrm{Hom}(Y^{\bullet},A)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Hom}(X^{\bullet},A)\quad\text{such that}\quad(\mathrm{Hom}(\phi^{\bullet},A))(g):=g\circ\phi^{\bullet},

for all
g\in\mathrm{Hom}(Y^{\bullet},A)
. In particular, this gives a functor
\mathrm{Hom}(-,A)\colon(\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A}))^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Ch}(\mathrm{Ab})
.

Definition 7.3
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a complete Abelian category, let
\mathcal{I}
be an injective class, and let
A\in\mathcal{A}
. A
relative
\mathcal{I}
-injective resolution
of
A
is a pair
(I^{\bullet},u\colon S^{0}(A)\to I^{\bullet})
such that:

•

I^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}^{\geq 0}(\mathcal{I})\subseteq\mathrm{Ch}^{\geq 0}(\mathcal{A})
;

•

\mathrm{Hom}(u,I)\colon\mathrm{Hom}(I^{\bullet},I)\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}(S^{0}(A),I)
is a quasi-isomorphism, for all
I\in\mathcal{I}
.

Let us give some equivalent reformulations of the above definition:

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a complete Abelian category,
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
an injective class, and take a complex:

\tilde{I}^{\bullet}\colon\quad\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 204.38776pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 204.38776pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 220.9782pt\raise 5.5853pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5853pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{d^{n}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 236.01163pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 236.01163pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\cdots\ \ \in\mathrm{Ch}^{\geq-1}(\mathcal{A}),}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

with
I^{j}\in\mathcal{I}
, for all
j\geq 0
. Denote by
I^{\bullet}:=(\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 6.75pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 37.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 37.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 51.3838pt\raise 6.12001pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.12001pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{d^{0}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 63.41666pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 63.41666pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 86.91666pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 86.91666pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{I^{n-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 104.80568pt\raise 6.12001pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.12001pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{d^{n-1}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 120.2211pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 120.2211pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{I^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 134.31154pt\raise 5.5853pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5853pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{d^{n}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 146.84497pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 146.84497pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\cdots}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces)
the naive truncation above
0
, and by
d^{-1}\colon S^{0}(A)\to I^{\bullet}
the obvious map. Then, the following are equivalent:

(1)

(I^{\bullet},d^{-1}\colon S^{0}(A)\to I^{\bullet})
is a relative
\mathcal{I}
-injective resolution of
A
;

(2)

\mathrm{Hom}(\tilde{I}^{\bullet},I)
is exact, for all
I\in\mathcal{I}
;

(3)

\tilde{I}^{k}/B^{k}(\tilde{I}^{\bullet})\rightarrow I^{k+1}
is an
\mathcal{I}
-monomorphism, for all
k\geq-1
.

Proof.

The equivalence “(1)
\Leftrightarrow
(2)” is trivial; let us verify that (2) is also equivalent to (3). Indeed, for each
I\in\mathcal{I}
and
k\geq-1
, consider the following sequence:

(7.1)

\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(\tilde{I}^{k+1},I)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(d^{k})^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(\tilde{I}^{k},I)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(d^{k-1})^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(\tilde{I}^{k-1},I),}

Observe that, being
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(-,I)\colon\mathcal{A}^{{\mathrm{op}}}\to\mathrm{Ab}
a left-exact functor, there is a canonical isomorphism
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(\tilde{I}^{k}/B^{k}(\tilde{I}^{\bullet}),I)=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathrm{Coker}(d^{k-1}),I)\cong\ker((d^{k-1})^{*})
. In particular, the sequence (
7.1
) is exact precisely when the induced map
\tilde{I}^{k}/B^{k}(\tilde{I}^{\bullet})\rightarrow I^{k+1}
is an
\mathcal{I}
-monomorphism.
∎

Given an Abelian category
\mathcal{A}
and an injective class
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
, since
\mathcal{A}
has enough
\mathcal{I}
-injectives, it is easy to construct a relative
\mathcal{I}
-injective resolution for any object
A\in\mathcal{A}
by induction, just using again and again the equivalence “(1)
\Leftrightarrow
(3)” in the above lemma.

In this relative context for resolutions, the classical statement about extension and unicity up to homotopy of maps between resolutions (see
[
19
, Chap. III, Par. 6 and 7]
) still holds true

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an Abelian category, let
\mathcal{I}
be an injective class in
\mathcal{A}
, let
(C^{\bullet},\partial^{\bullet})
and
(D^{\bullet},d^{\bullet})\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
be two cochain complexes, and let
0\leq r
be a natural number such that,

(1)

D^{i}\in\mathcal{I}
, for all
i>r
;

(2)

H^{-i}(\mathrm{Hom}(C^{\bullet},I))=0
, for all
I\in\mathcal{I}
and all
i\geq r
.

Then, any family of morphisms
(f^{k}\colon C^{k}\to D^{k})_{k\leq r}
such that
f^{k}\circ\delta^{k-1}=d^{k-1}\circ f^{k-1}
, for all
k\leq r
, extends to a morphism of complexes
f^{\bullet}\colon C^{\bullet}\to D^{\bullet}
. Moreover, any two such extensions are homotopic via a homotopy
(h^{n}\colon C^{n}\to D^{n-1})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
such that
h^{k}=0
, for all
k\leq r
.

Proof.

Our hypothesis (2) means that the following sequence is exact in
\mathrm{Ab}

(7.2)

\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(C^{j+1},I)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\delta^{j})^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(C^{j},I)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\delta^{j-1})^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(C^{j-1},I),}

for all
I\in\mathcal{I}
and all
j\geq r
. Then, by (1), the sequence (
7.2
) is exact provided
I=D^{j+1}
and, for
j>r
, also for
I=D^{j}
.

We can now proceed to construct
f^{\bullet}\colon C^{\bullet}\to D^{\bullet}
by induction. Indeed, suppose that we have morphisms
(f^{n}\colon C^{n}\rightarrow D^{n})_{n\leq j}
, for some
j\geq r
, such that
f^{n}\circ\delta^{n-1}=d^{n-1}\circ f^{n-1}
, for all
n\leq j
. In particular, taking
I=D^{j+1}
in (
7.2
), we deduce that

(\delta^{j-1})^{*}(d^{j}\circ f^{j})=d^{j}\circ f^{j}\circ\delta^{j-1}=d^{j}\circ d^{j-1}\circ f^{j-1}=0,

that is,
d^{j}\circ f^{j}\in\ker((\delta^{j-1})^{*})=\mathrm{Im}((\delta^{j})^{*})
and, therefore, there is some
f^{j+1}\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(C^{j+1},D^{j+1})
such that
(\delta^{j})^{*}(f^{j+1})=f^{j+1}\circ\delta^{j}=d^{j}\circ f^{j}
. The family
(f^{n}\colon C^{n}\rightarrow D^{n})_{n\leq j+1}
now satisfies
f^{n}\circ\delta^{n-1}=d^{n-1}\circ f^{n-1}
, for all
n\leq j+1
, and the induction can continue.

Assume now that
f^{\bullet},\,g^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})}(C^{\bullet},D^{\bullet})
are both extensions of the family
(f^{k})_{k\leq r}
. We proceed by induction to construct
(h^{n}\colon C^{n+1}\to D^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
such that
f^{n}-g^{n}=d^{n-1}\circ h_{n-1}+h^{n}\circ\delta^{n}
, for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Indeed, let
h^{i}=0
, for all
i\leq r
and suppose that, for some
j>r
, we have constructed a family
(h^{n}\colon C^{n+1}\to D^{n})_{n\leq j}
such that
f^{n}-g^{n}=d^{n-1}\circ h^{n-1}+h^{n}\circ\delta^{n}
, for all
n\leq j
. In particular, using that
(f^{\bullet}-g^{\bullet})
is a map of complexes, we deduce that

(f^{j+1}-g^{j+1})\circ\delta^{j}=d^{j}\circ(f^{j}-g^{j})=d^{j}\circ d^{j-1}\circ h^{j-1}+d^{j}\circ h^{j}\circ\delta^{j}=d^{j}\circ h^{j}\circ\delta^{j}.

Hence, taking
I=D^{j}
in (
7.2
), and using the above computation, we get:

(\delta^{j})^{*}(f^{j+1}-g^{j+1}-d^{j}\circ h^{j})=(\delta^{j})^{*}(f^{j+1}-g^{j+1})-d^{j}\circ h^{j}\circ\delta^{j}=0,

that is,
f^{j+1}-g^{j+1}-d^{j}\circ h^{j}\in\ker((\delta^{j})^{*})=\mathrm{Im}((\delta^{j+1})^{*})
and so there is some
h^{j+1}\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(C^{j},D^{j+1})
such that
(\delta^{j+1})^{*}(h^{j+1})=f^{j+1}-g^{j+1}-d^{j}\circ h^{j}
, that is,
f^{n}-g^{n}=d^{n-1}\circ h^{n-1}+h^{n}\circ\delta^{n}
holds for all
n\leq j+1
, and the induction can continue.
∎

As a consequence, we obtain that relative
\mathcal{I}
-injective resolutions are unique up to homotopy:

Corollary 7.6
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an Abelian category and let
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
be an injective class. If
u\colon S^{0}(A)\rightarrow I^{\bullet}
and
v\colon S^{0}(A)\rightarrow J^{\bullet}
are both relative
\mathcal{I}
-injective resolutions of an object
A\in\mathcal{A}
, then there is a homotopy equivalence
f^{\bullet}\colon I^{\bullet}\rightarrow J^{\bullet}
such that
f^{\bullet}\circ u=v
, and
f^{\bullet}
is unique up to homotopy.

Proof.

Use Lemma
7.5
to extend
\mathrm{id}_{A}\colon A\to A
to two morphisms
f^{\bullet}\colon I^{\bullet}\to J^{\bullet}
and
g^{\bullet}\colon J^{\bullet}\to I^{\bullet}
, such that
f^{\bullet}\circ u=v
and
g^{\bullet}\circ v=u
. The uniqueness of liftings up to homotopy forces
g^{\bullet}\circ f^{\bullet}
and
f^{\bullet}\circ g^{\bullet}
to be homotopic to
\mathrm{id}_{I^{\bullet}}
and
\mathrm{id}_{J^{\bullet}}
, respectively.
∎

7.3.
Generalities about the
\mathcal{I}
-injective model structure

Given an injective class
\mathcal{I}
in an Abelian category
\mathcal{A}
, one can introduce the following classes of morphisms in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
that, under suitable hypotheses, make
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
into a model category:

Definition 7.7
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an Abelian category and
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
an injective class. We say that a morphism
\phi^{\bullet}\colon X^{\bullet}\to Y^{\bullet}
is

•

an
\mathcal{I}
-cofibration
if
\phi^{n}
is an
\mathcal{I}
-monomorphism, for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
;

•

an
\mathcal{I}
-weak equivalence
if
\mathrm{Hom}(\phi^{\bullet},I)
is a quasi-isomorphism in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathrm{Ab})
, for all
I\in\mathcal{I}
;

•

an
\mathcal{I}
-fibration
if it has the right lifting property with respect to the trivial
\mathcal{I}
-cofibrations.

We denote these classes of maps by
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{I}}
,
\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{I}}
, and
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{I}}
, respectively. Furthermore, we say that a complex
X^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
is
\mathcal{I}
-acyclic
provided
\mathrm{Hom}(X^{\bullet},I)
is acyclic in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathrm{Ab})
, for all
I\in\mathcal{I}
.

Remark 7.8
.

We take advantage of this paper to correct an annoying misprint in the statement of
[
3
, Theorem 2.3]
: the
\mathcal{I}
-cofibrations in the relative model structure on
\mathrm{Ch}_{\leq n}
should be
\mathcal{I}
-monomorphisms in degrees
i<n
, and not
i\leq n
.

In the following lemma, which is a consequence of
[
4
, Proposition 2.5 and Lemma 2.7(b)]
, we collect some properties of
\mathcal{I}
-fibrations and of
\mathcal{I}
-fibrant objects:

Lemma 7.9
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a bicomplete Abelian category and let
\mathcal{I}
be an injective class in
\mathcal{A}
. Then:

(1)

the
\mathcal{I}
-fibrations are precisely the degreewise split epimorphisms with
\mathcal{I}
-fibrant kernel;

(2)

a bounded below complex
X^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}^{+}(\mathcal{A})
is
\mathcal{I}
-fibrant, provided
X^{i}\in\mathcal{I}
, for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.

More generally, let us recall the following important result by Christensen and Hovey (they actually state the dual result for projective classes, but it is an easy exercise to show that their statement is equivalent to the following one):

Theorem 7.10

(
[
4
, Theorem 2.2]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a bicomplete Abelian category and
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
an injective class such that, for each
X^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
, there is an
\mathcal{I}
-fibrant replacement
\lambda^{\bullet}\colon X^{\bullet}\to F^{\bullet}
(i.e.,
\lambda^{\bullet}\in\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{I}}
and
F^{\bullet}
is
\mathcal{I}
-fibrant). Then,
(\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A}),\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{I}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{I}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{I}})
is a model category.

In the following proposition (adapted from
[
3
]
) we establish the existence of “Spaltenstein towers of partial
\mathcal{I}
-fibrant replacements”, in complete analogy to our discussion in Section
2
for the case
\mathcal{I}=\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{A})
. This construction produces a standard candidate to
\mathcal{I}
-fibrant replacement for any given
X^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
. In Subsection
7.4
, we will discuss a sufficient condition to ensure that the construction below always produces an
\mathcal{I}
-fibrant replacement.

Proposition 7.11
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a bicomplete Abelian category and let
\mathcal{I}
be an injective class in
\mathcal{A}
. Then, for each
X^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
, there is an inverse system (a
tower
) of complexes

(7.3)

\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t_{2}^{\bullet}}
\textstyle{E_{2}^{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t_{1}^{\bullet}}
\textstyle{E_{1}^{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t_{0}^{\bullet}}
\textstyle{E_{0}^{\bullet}}

that satisfies the following conditions, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
:

(1)

E_{n}^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}^{\geq-n}(\mathcal{A})
and
E_{n}^{i}\in\mathcal{I}
, for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
(so
E_{n}^{\bullet}
is
\mathcal{I}
-fibrant);

(2)

the morphism
t_{n}^{\bullet}
is an
\mathcal{I}
-fibration;

(3)

there is an
\mathcal{I}
-weak equivalence
\lambda_{n}^{\bullet}\colon\tau^{\geq-n}(X^{\bullet})\to E_{n}^{\bullet}
such that

\lambda_{n}^{\bullet}\circ\pi_{n}^{\bullet}=t_{n}^{\bullet}\circ\lambda_{n+1}^{\bullet},\quad\text{for all $n\in\mathbb{N}$,}

where
\pi_{n}^{\bullet}\colon\tau^{\geq-n-1}(X^{\bullet})\to\tau^{\geq-n}(X^{\bullet})
is the canonical projection.

Proof.

By the results in Sections 4-5 in
[
3
]
, there is model category
\mathrm{Tow}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{I})
of towers of complexes. Given
X^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
, one can consider
\mathrm{tow}(X^{\bullet})\in\mathrm{Tow}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{I})
, the tower of successive truncations of
X^{\bullet}
. A sequence (
7.3
), with the properties (1–3) in the statement, is precisely a fibrant replacement of
\mathrm{tow}(X^{\bullet})
in the model category
\mathrm{Tow}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{I})
.
∎

7.4.
The (Ab.
4^{*}
)-
k
condition and the injective model structure relative to
\mathcal{I}

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a bicomplete Abelian category,
X^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
,
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
an injective class, and define
E^{\bullet}:=\varprojlim_{\mathbb{N}}E_{n}^{\bullet}
as the inverse limit of the tower (
7.3
) described by Proposition
7.11
. Then,
E^{\bullet}:=\varprojlim_{\mathbb{N}}E_{n}^{\bullet}
is
\mathcal{I}
-fibrant (by Remark
1.2
) and, by condition (3) in the proposition, there is a canonical morphism
\lambda^{\bullet}\colon X^{\bullet}\to E^{\bullet}
. To determine whether
\lambda^{\bullet}
is an
\mathcal{I}
-weak equivalence (i.e., if
E^{\bullet}
is an
\mathcal{I}
-fibrant replacement for
X^{\bullet}
), it is useful to introduce a relative version of Roos’ (Ab.
4^{*}
)-
k
condition (see
[
3
, Definition 6.1]
). We do it after the following technical lemma:

Lemma 7.12
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a complete Abelian category, let
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
be an injective class, and let
(u_{\lambda}\colon A_{\lambda}\rightarrow I_{\lambda}^{\bullet})_{\Lambda}
be a family of relative
\mathcal{I}
-injective resolutions of objects of
\mathcal{A}
. Then, the following assertions are equivalent for any
k\geq 0
:

(1)

H^{-n}(\mathrm{Hom}(\prod_{\Lambda}I_{\lambda}^{\bullet},I))=0
, for all
I\in\mathcal{I}
and all
n>k
;

(2)

the induced map
\mathrm{Coker}(\prod d_{\lambda}^{n-1})\rightarrow\prod_{\Lambda}I_{\lambda}^{n+1}
is an
\mathcal{I}
-monomorphsm, for all
n>k
.

Proof.

We set by convention
I_{\lambda}^{-1}:=A_{\lambda}
, for all
\lambda\in\Lambda
. Condition (1) means that the following sequences are exact in
\mathrm{Ab}
for all
I\in\mathcal{I}
and all
n>k
:

(7.4)

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 155.2785pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-155.2785pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}\left(\prod_{\Lambda}I_{\lambda}^{n+1},I\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\prod d_{\lambda}^{n})^{*}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}\left(\prod_{\Lambda}I_{\lambda}^{n},I\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\prod d_{\lambda}^{n-1})^{*}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}\left(\prod_{\Lambda}I_{\lambda}^{n-1},I\right)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

that is, we want the morphism
(\prod d_{\lambda}^{n})^{*}\colon\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}\left(\prod_{\Lambda}I_{\lambda}^{n+1},I\right)\to\mathrm{Ker}((\prod d_{\lambda}^{n-1})^{*})
to be surjective. As
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(-,I)
is left exact, there is an isomorphism
\mathrm{Ker}((\prod d_{\lambda}^{n-1})^{*})\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathrm{Coker}(\prod d_{\lambda}^{n-1}),I)
. In particular, this shows that the sequences in (
7.4
) are exact for all
I\in\mathcal{I}
if, and only if,
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}\left(\prod_{\Lambda}I_{\lambda}^{n+1},I\right)\to\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathrm{Coker}(\prod d_{\lambda}^{n-1}),I)
is surjective for all
I\in\mathcal{I}
, that is, if the morphism
\mathrm{Coker}(\prod d_{\lambda}^{n-1})\to\prod_{\Lambda}I_{\lambda}^{n+1}
is an
\mathcal{I}
-monomorphism, which is condition (2).
∎

Definition 7.13
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a complete Abelian category, let
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
be an injective class, and let
k
be a natural number. Then,
\mathcal{A}
is said to be
(Ab.4
∗
)-
\mathcal{I}
-
k
when, for each family
(A_{\lambda})_{\Lambda}
of objects of
\mathcal{A}
, and for some (or, equivalently, any) choice of relative
\mathcal{I}
-injective resolutions
(I_{\lambda}^{\bullet},u_{\lambda}\colon S^{0}(A_{\lambda})\rightarrow I_{\lambda}^{\bullet})_{\Lambda}
, the equivalent conditions (1) and (2) in Lemma
7.12
hold true.

Observe that varying the chosen relative
\mathcal{I}
-injective resolutions does not affect the above definition. This is a consequence of Corollary
7.6
, which implies that, if
(I_{\lambda}^{\bullet},u_{\lambda})_{\Lambda}
and
(J_{\lambda}^{\bullet},v_{\lambda})_{\Lambda}
are two families of relative
\mathcal{I}
-injective resolutions corresponding to
(A_{\lambda})_{\Lambda}
, then there are homotopy equivalences
(f_{\lambda}\colon I_{\lambda}^{\bullet}\to J_{\lambda}^{\bullet})_{\Lambda}
, whose product gives a homotopy equivalence
\prod_{\Lambda}f_{\lambda}\colon\prod_{\Lambda}I^{\bullet}_{\lambda}\to\prod_{\Lambda}J^{\bullet}_{\lambda}
, which then induces an homotopy equivalence between
\mathrm{Hom}(\prod_{\Lambda}I^{\bullet}_{\lambda},I)
and
\mathrm{Hom}(\prod_{\Lambda}J^{\bullet}_{\lambda},I)
, for all
I\in\mathcal{I}
. In particular, these two complexes of Abelian groups have the same cohomology groups. Hence, condition (1) in Lemma
7.12
can be checked on either complex with the same result.

Corollary 7.14
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a complete Abelian category, let
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
be an injective class, and consider the following assertions:

(1)

each product of
\mathcal{I}
-preenvelopes is an
\mathcal{I}
-preenvelope;

(2)

each product of
\mathcal{I}
-monomorphisms is an
\mathcal{I}
-monomorphism;

(3)

\mathcal{A}
is (Ab.4
∗
)-
\mathcal{I}:=
(Ab.4
∗
)-
\mathcal{I}
-
0
.

Then, the equivalence “(1)
\Leftrightarrow
(2)” holds in general, while “(1,2)
\Rightarrow
(3)” holds if
\mathcal{A}
is (Ab.4
∗
).

Proof.
