As “(2)
\Rightarrow
(1)” is trivial, we just verify that “(1)
\Rightarrow
(2)”: let
(\iota_{\lambda}\colon X_{\lambda}\rightarrow Y_{\lambda})_{\Lambda}
be a family of
\mathcal{I}
-monomorphisms in
\mathcal{A}
. Choose, for each
\lambda\in\Lambda
, an
\mathcal{I}
-preenvelope
u_{\lambda}\colon X_{\lambda}\rightarrow I_{\lambda}
. As each
\iota_{\lambda}
is an
\mathcal{I}
-monomorphism, there are morphisms
v_{\lambda}\colon Y_{\lambda}\rightarrow I_{\lambda}
such that
v_{\lambda}\circ\iota_{\lambda}=u_{\lambda}
, for all
\lambda\in\Lambda
. By (1), the product map
\prod u_{\lambda}=(\prod v_{\lambda})\circ(\prod\iota_{\lambda})
is an
\mathcal{I}
-preenvelope, and so also an
\mathcal{I}
-monomorphism. As a consequence, also
\prod\iota_{\lambda}
is an
\mathcal{I}
-monomorphism, as desired.

For the implication “(2)
\Rightarrow
(3)” recall that, by Lemma
7.12
, for
\mathcal{A}
to be (Ab.4
∗
)-
\mathcal{I}
it is enough that, for any family
(X_{\lambda})_{\Lambda}
and a corresponding choice of relative
\mathcal{I}
-injective resolutions
(I_{\lambda}^{\bullet},u_{\lambda})_{\Lambda}
, the induced map
(\prod_{\Lambda}I_{\lambda}^{n})/B^{n}(\prod_{\Lambda}I_{\lambda}^{\bullet})\to\prod_{\Lambda}I_{\lambda}^{n+1}
is an
\mathcal{I}
-monomorphism for all
n\geq-1
. Assuming that
\mathcal{A}
is (Ab.4
∗
), there is an isomorphism
(\prod_{\Lambda}I_{\lambda}^{n})/B^{n}(\prod_{\Lambda}I_{\lambda}^{\bullet})\cong\prod_{\Lambda}(I_{\lambda}^{n}/B^{n}(I_{\lambda}^{\bullet}))
, so the condition becomes equivalent to the fact that
\prod_{\Lambda}(I_{\lambda}^{n}/B^{n}(I_{\lambda}^{\bullet}))\to\prod_{\Lambda}I_{\lambda}^{n+1}
is an
\mathcal{I}
-monomorphism, for all
n\geq-1
. This is then a consequence of (2), as the map in question is the product of the family of
\mathcal{I}
-monomorphisms
(I_{\lambda}^{n}/B^{n}(I_{\lambda}^{\bullet})\to I_{\lambda}^{n+1})_{\Lambda}
.
∎

The proof of the following theorem follows the same steps used in the proof of Theorem
2.9
:

Theorem 7.15

(
[
3
, Theorem 6.4]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a bicomplete Abelian category and
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
an injective class such that
\mathcal{A}
is (Ab.
4^{*}
)-
\mathcal{I}
-
k
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Given
X^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
and a tower

\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t_{2}^{\bullet}}
\textstyle{E_{2}^{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t_{1}^{\bullet}}
\textstyle{E_{1}^{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t_{0}^{\bullet}}
\textstyle{E_{0}^{\bullet}}

satisfying (1–3) in Proposition
7.11
, the map
\lambda^{\bullet}\colon X^{\bullet}\to E^{\bullet}:=\varprojlim_{\mathbb{N}}E_{n}^{\bullet}
is an
\mathcal{I}
-weak equivalence.

In particular, in the setting of the above theorem (also using Proposition
7.11
and Remark
1.2
), any complex
X^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
has an
\mathcal{I}
-fibrant replacement
\lambda^{\bullet}\colon X^{\bullet}\to E^{\bullet}
. Combining this result with Theorem
7.10
, one immediately deduces that:

Corollary 7.16
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a bicomplete Abelian category and let
\mathcal{I}
be an injective class in
\mathcal{A}
. If
\mathcal{A}
is (Ab.
4^{*}
)-
\mathcal{I}
-
k
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
, then
(\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A}),\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{I}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{I}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{I}})
is a model category.

Observe that an advantage of Corollary
7.16
is that it applies to Abelian categories that are not necessarily Grothendieck, and where the small object argument may not be applicable.

8.
Examples and applications

8.1.
On injective classes that are cogenerating

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an Abelian category. A full subcategory
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
is called
cogenerating
if, for each
A\in\mathcal{A}
, there is a monomorphism
A\to I
, for some
I\in\mathcal{I}
.

Proposition 8.1
.

The following are equivalent for an injective class
\mathcal{I}
in an Abelian category
\mathcal{A}
:

(1)

\mathcal{I}
is a cogenerating class in
\mathcal{A}
;

(2)

\mathcal{I}
-monomorphisms in
\mathcal{A}
are, in particular, also monomorphisms;

(3)

\mathcal{I}
-acyclic complexes in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
are, in particular, also acyclic;

(4)

\mathcal{I}
-weak equivalences in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
are, in particular, also quasi-isomorphisms.

Proof.

(1)
\Leftrightarrow
(2). Given an
\mathcal{I}
-monomorphism
\phi\colon A\to B
, fix a monomorphism
u\colon A\to I\in\mathcal{I}
. The surjectivity of the map
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(\phi,I)\colon\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(B,I)\to\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(A,I)
implies that
u=v\circ\phi
, for some
v\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(B,I)
and, therefore,
\phi
is a monomorphism. The converse is trivial.

(2)
\Leftrightarrow
(3). Given
X^{\bullet}=(\cdots\overset{}{\to}X^{n}\overset{d^{n}}{\longrightarrow}X^{n+1}\to\cdots)\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
, let
\bar{d}^{n}\colon X^{n}/B^{n}(X^{\bullet})\to X^{n+1}
be the morphism induced by the differential, so that
H^{n}(X^{\bullet})=\mathrm{Ker}(\bar{d}^{n})
, for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. If
X^{\bullet}
is
\mathcal{I}
-acyclic then
\bar{d}^{n}
is an
\mathcal{I}
-monomorphism (by
[
3
, Proposition 1.15(7)]
) for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, and so, by (2),
H^{n}(X^{\bullet})=\mathrm{Ker}(\bar{d}^{n})=0
, for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Conversely, a map
\psi\colon A\to B
in
\mathcal{A}
is a(n)
(
\mathcal{I}
-)
monomorphism if and only if the complex
(\cdots\to 0\rightarrow A\to B\to\mathrm{Coker}(\psi)\rightarrow 0\to\cdots)
in
\mathrm{Ch}^{\geq-1}(\mathcal{A})\cap\mathrm{Ch}^{\leq 1}(\mathcal{A})
is (
\mathcal{I}
-)acyclic. Hence, also the implication “(3)
\Rightarrow
(2)” holds.

(3)
\Leftrightarrow
(4). It is well-known that a morphism
\phi^{\bullet}\colon A^{\bullet}\to B^{\bullet}
in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
is a quasi-isomorphism if and only if
\mathrm{cone}(\phi^{\bullet})
is acyclic, while
\phi^{\bullet}
is an
\mathcal{I}
-weak equivalence if and only if
\mathrm{cone}(\phi^{\bullet})
is
\mathcal{I}
-acyclic (by
[
3
, Proposition 1.15(2)]
). These two characterizations imply the desired equivalence. ∎

Remark 8.2
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an (Ab.4
∗
) Abelian category, and
(X_{\lambda}^{\bullet})_{\Lambda}
a family in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
, then there are isomorphisms
B^{n}(\prod_{\Lambda}X^{\bullet}_{\lambda})\cong\prod_{\Lambda}B^{n}(X^{\bullet}_{\lambda})
, for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Take a cogenerating injective class
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
, and suppose that
X_{\lambda}^{\bullet}
is
\mathcal{I}
-acyclic, for all
\lambda\in\Lambda
. By Proposition
8.1
, each
X_{\lambda}^{\bullet}
is also acyclic, and so
\mathrm{Coker}(d_{\lambda}^{n-1})=X_{\lambda}^{n}/B^{n}(X_{\lambda}^{\bullet})=X_{\lambda}^{n}/Z^{n}(X^{\bullet}_{\lambda})\cong B^{n+1}(X^{\bullet}_{\lambda})
, for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Combining these two observations we deduce that
\prod_{\Lambda}X^{\bullet}_{\lambda}
is
\mathcal{I}
-acyclic if, and only if, the map
\prod_{\Lambda}B^{n}(X^{\bullet}_{\lambda})\to\prod_{\Lambda}X^{n}_{\lambda}
is an
\mathcal{I}
-monomorphism, for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Using an analogous idea, one proves that an (Ab.4
∗
) Abelian category
\mathcal{A}
is (Ab.4
∗
)-
\mathcal{I}
-
k
, with
\mathcal{I}
cogenerating, if and only if the following variation of the condition (2) in Lemma
7.12
holds for any family of relative
\mathcal{I}
-injective resolutions
(u_{\lambda}\colon A_{\lambda}\rightarrow I_{\lambda}^{\bullet})_{\Lambda}
of objects of
\mathcal{A}
:

(2’)

\prod_{\Lambda}B^{n+1}(I_{\lambda}^{\bullet})\to\prod_{\Lambda}I_{\lambda}^{n+1}
is an
\mathcal{I}
-monomorphism, for all
n>k
;

that is, we want the product functor to preserve the
\mathcal{I}
-monomorphisms
(B^{n+1}(I_{\lambda}^{\bullet})\to I_{\lambda}^{n+1})_{\Lambda}
.

Let
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
be a cogenerating injective class. Then, a short exact sequence in
\mathcal{A}
:

0\longrightarrow X\overset{\iota}{\longrightarrow}Y\longrightarrow Z\longrightarrow 0

is called
\mathcal{I}
-exact
if
\iota
is an
\mathcal{I}
-monomorphism or, equivalently, if the following sequences are exact:

0\longrightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(Z,I)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(Y,I)\overset{\iota^{*}}{\longrightarrow}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(X,I)\longrightarrow 0,\quad\text{for all $I\in\mathcal{I}$.}

We have the following variation of Corollary
7.14
for cogenerating injective classes:

Lemma 8.3
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a complete Abelian category, and
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
a cogenerating injective class. Suppose that any family of
\mathcal{I}
-exact sequences
(0\to X_{\lambda}\to Y_{\lambda}\to Z_{\lambda}\to 0)_{\Lambda}
, gives a short exact sequence
0\to\prod_{\Lambda}X_{\lambda}\to\prod_{\Lambda}Y_{\lambda}\to\prod_{\Lambda}Z_{\lambda}\to 0
, which is moreover
\mathcal{I}
-exact. Then,
\mathcal{A}
is (Ab.4
∗
)-
\mathcal{I}
.

Proof.

Let
(X_{\lambda})_{\Lambda}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
and, for each
\lambda\in\Lambda
, consider the following relative
\mathcal{I}
-injective resolution:

(8.1)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{\lambda}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u_{\lambda}}
\textstyle{E^{0}_{\lambda}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d^{0}_{\lambda}}
\textstyle{E^{1}_{\lambda}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d^{1}_{\lambda}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d^{n-1}_{\lambda}}
\textstyle{E^{n}_{\lambda}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d^{n}_{\lambda}}
\textstyle{\cdots}

with
E^{i}_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{I}
, for all
i\geq 0
. As
\mathcal{I}
is cogenerating, (
8.1
) is also an exact sequence. In particular, letting
B^{0}_{\lambda}:=X_{\lambda}
and
B^{n}_{\lambda}:=\mathrm{Im}(d^{n-1}_{\lambda})
, for all
n\geq 1
, we have the following short exact sequences, for all
n\geq 0
and
\lambda\in\Lambda
, that are also
\mathcal{I}
-exact:

0\longrightarrow B^{n}_{\lambda}\longrightarrow E_{\lambda}^{n}\longrightarrow B_{\lambda}^{n+1}\longrightarrow 0.

By hypothesis, for each
n\geq 0
, the following short exact sequence is
\mathcal{I}
-exact:

\textstyle{0\longrightarrow\prod_{\Lambda}B^{n}_{\lambda}\longrightarrow\prod_{\Lambda}E_{\lambda}^{n}\longrightarrow\prod_{\Lambda}B_{\lambda}^{n+1}\longrightarrow 0.}

These conditions tell us that the following is a relative
\mathcal{I}
-injective resolution:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\prod_{\Lambda}X_{\lambda}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\prod u_{\lambda}}
\textstyle{\prod_{\Lambda}E^{0}_{\lambda}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\prod d^{0}_{\lambda}}
\textstyle{\prod_{\Lambda}E^{1}_{\lambda}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\prod d^{1}_{\lambda}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\prod d^{n-1}_{\lambda}}
\textstyle{\prod_{\Lambda}E^{n}_{\lambda}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\prod d^{n}_{\lambda}}
\textstyle{\cdots,}

showing that
\mathcal{A}
is (Ab.4
∗
)-
\mathcal{I}
, as desired.
∎

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an Abelian category with an injective class
\mathcal{I}
. As in
[
3
]
, consider the localization of
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
with respect to
\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{I}}
, the class of
\mathcal{I}
-weak equivalences,
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A};\mathcal{I}):=\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})[\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{I}}^{-1}]
. In general this category could fail to be
locally small
(that is, the morphisms between two objects may form a proper class), while it is certainly locally small whenever
(\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A}),\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{I}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{I}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{I}})
is a model category (e.g., in the setting of Corollary
7.16
) as, in that case,
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A};\mathcal{I})
is equivalent to the corresponding homotopy category. In what follows we denote by
\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{A})
the homotopy category of
\mathcal{A}
, and by
\mathrm{Ac}(\mathcal{A})\subseteq\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{A})
(resp.,
\mathrm{Ac}_{\mathcal{I}}(\mathcal{A})\subseteq\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{A})
) its full subcategory of (
\mathcal{I}
-)acyclic complexes.

Corollary 8.4
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an Abelian category and let
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
be an injective class. Then,

(1)

if
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A};\mathcal{I})
is locally small, it is a Verdier quotient
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A};\mathcal{I})\cong\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{A})/\mathrm{Ac}_{\mathcal{I}}(\mathcal{A})
;

(2)

if both
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A};\mathcal{I})
and
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
are locally small and
\mathcal{I}
is cogenerating, then there is a canonical Verdier quotient functor
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A};\mathcal{I})\to\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
.

Proof.

(1) follows by
[
3
, Proposition 2.3 and Corollary 2.4]
.

(2) By Proposition
8.1
, there are inclusions
\text{Ac}_{\mathcal{I}}(\mathcal{A})\subseteq\text{Ac}(\mathcal{A})\subseteq\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{A})
of full triangulated subcategories. By
[
34
, Proposition 2.3.1 in Chap. II]
, we know that
\text{Ac}(\mathcal{A})/\text{Ac}_{\mathcal{I}}(\mathcal{A})
is a full triangulated subcategory of
\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{A})/\text{Ac}_{\mathcal{I}}(\mathcal{A})
and that there is a canonical triangulated functor

\pi_{\mathcal{I}}\colon\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})\cong\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{A})/\text{Ac}(\mathcal{A})\longrightarrow\frac{\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{A})/\text{Ac}_{\mathcal{I}}(\mathcal{A})}{\text{Ac}(\mathcal{A})/\text{Ac}_{\mathcal{I}}(\mathcal{A})}\cong\frac{\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A};\mathcal{I})}{\text{Ac}(\mathcal{A})/\text{Ac}_{\mathcal{I}}(\mathcal{A})}.

By Proposition
8.1
,
\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{I}}\subseteq\mathcal{W}
, so the canonical functor
p\colon\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
sends
\mathcal{I}
-weak equivalences to isomorphisms. Therefore,
p
induces a unique functor
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A};\mathcal{I})=\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})[\mathcal{W}_{I}^{-1}]\to\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
, which is a triangulated quasi-inverse to
\pi_{\mathcal{I}}
.
∎

We now give an application to the derived category relative to a generator introduced in
[
10
]
:

Example 8.5
.

Given a complete Abelian category
\mathcal{A}
with a cogenerator
E
, we can consider the cogenerating injective class
\mathcal{I}=\mathrm{Prod}(E)
of all the summands of products of copies of
E
. In particular, take
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{G}^{{\mathrm{op}}}
for a Grothendieck category
\mathcal{G}
, that is,
\mathcal{A}
is the category of strict complete topologically coherent left
R
-modules over some strict complete topologically left coherent and linearly compact ring
R
(see
[
23
]
). Then,
E
is just a generator of
\mathcal{G}
, and the localization
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A};\mathcal{I})\cong\mathcal{D}_{E}(\mathcal{G})^{\mathrm{op}}
is the dual of the
E
-derived category defined in
[
10
]
. Since
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{G})^{op}\cong\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
, Corollary
8.4
gives a Verdier quotient functor
\mathcal{D}_{E}(\mathcal{G})\to\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{G})
, for any generator
E
of
\mathcal{G}
.

Observe that, if
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
is a right complete cotorsion pair in an Abelian category
\mathcal{A}
, then
\mathcal{Y}
is cogenerating (by the right completeness). Moreover,
\mathcal{Y}
is closed under summands in
\mathcal{A}
, and it is also pre-enveloping since any right
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
approximation sequence is clearly
\mathcal{Y}
-exact.
As we have seen in Subsection
6.2
, the original proof of
[
37
, Lemma 2.1]
works if we assume that the ambient category is (Ab.
4^{*}
). In fact, the conclusion of the lemma also holds for the injective classes
\mathcal{Y}
such that
({}^{\perp_{1}}\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y})
is a right complete cotorsion pair, provided
\mathcal{A}
is (Ab.
4^{*}
)-
\mathcal{Y}
-
k
, for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
:

Remark 8.6
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a complete Abelian category and let
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
be a right complete cotorsion pair in
\mathcal{A}
such that
\mathcal{A}
is (Ab.
4^{*}
)-
\mathcal{Y}
-
k
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Given
X^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
one can find, as discussed in Subsection
7.4
, a
\mathcal{Y}
-fibrant replacement
\lambda^{\bullet}\colon X^{\bullet}\to E^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{dg}\mathcal{Y}
. As
\mathcal{Y}
is cogenerating,
\lambda^{\bullet}
is a quasi-isomorphism (by Proposition
8.1
) and we get a short exact sequence:

0\longrightarrow\Sigma^{-1}E^{\bullet}\longrightarrow\Sigma^{-1}\mathrm{cone}(\lambda^{\bullet})\longrightarrow X^{\bullet}\longrightarrow 0,

with
\Sigma^{-1}E^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{dg}\mathcal{Y}
and
\Sigma^{-1}\mathrm{cone}(\lambda^{\bullet})\in\mathcal{E}
, proving
[
37
, Lemma 2.1(1)]
under these hypotheses.

8.2.
Injective classes of injectives

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a complete Abelian category and
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
an injective class. We have seen in Subsection
8.1
that, whenever
\mathcal{I}
is cogenerating, the classical derived category
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
is a Verdier quotient of
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A};\mathcal{I})
. In this subsection we concentrate on the injective classes
\mathcal{I}
of injectives in a Grothendieck category
\mathcal{G}
, that is,
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{G})
. These injective classes have the property that
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{G};\mathcal{I})
is always a Verdier quotient of
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{G})
, so they are in some sense at the extreme opposite from the cogenerating ones; actually, the unique cogenerating injective class of injectives is
\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{G})
, for which clearly
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{G};\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{G}))\cong\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{G})
.

The key to understand the injective classes of injectives in a Grothendieck category
\mathcal{G}
is the observation that they correspond bijectively to the hereditary torsion pairs in
\mathcal{G}
(see
[
35
, Theorem 4.8]
). More precisely, an injective class
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{G})
corresponds to the hereditary torsion pair
\tau_{\mathcal{I}}=(\mathcal{T}:={}^{\perp}\mathcal{I},\mathcal{F}:=({}^{\perp}\mathcal{I})^{\perp})
, where
\mathcal{F}
can also be described as
\mathrm{Cogen}(\mathcal{I})
, and
\mathcal{I}=\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{G})\cap\mathcal{F}
.

Lemma 8.7

(
[
35
, Lemmas 4.4 and 5.6]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a Grothendieck category,
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{G})
an injective class of injectives, and let
\tau_{\mathcal{I}}=(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})
be the associated hereditary torsion pair. Then

(1)

the following are equivalent for a morphism
\phi\colon X\to Y
in
\mathcal{G}
:

(1.1)

\phi
is an
\mathcal{I}
-monomorphism;

(1.2)

the kernel of
\phi
is
\tau_{\mathcal{I}}
-torsion, that is,
\mathrm{Ker}(\phi)\in\mathcal{T}
;

(2)

the following are equivalent for a morphism
\phi^{\bullet}\colon X^{\bullet}\to Y^{\bullet}
in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G})
:

(2.1)

\phi^{\bullet}
is an
\mathcal{I}
-weak equivalence;

(2.2)

the cone
\mathrm{cone}(\phi^{\bullet})
is
\mathcal{I}
-acyclic;

(2.3)

the cone has
\tau_{\mathcal{I}}
-torsion cohomologies, that is,
H^{n}(\mathrm{cone}(\phi^{\bullet}))\in\mathcal{T}
, for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Consider the Gabriel quotient
\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T}
, that comes with an adjunction
\mathbf{Q}\dashv\mathbf{S}\colon\mathcal{G}\leftrightarrows\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T}
such that:

•

the right adjoint
\mathbf{S}
is fully faithful, so denoting by
\eta\colon\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{G}}\Rightarrow\mathbf{S}\circ\mathbf{Q}
and
\varepsilon\colon\mathbf{Q}\circ\mathbf{S}\Rightarrow\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T}}
the unit and the counit, both
\varepsilon
and
\mathbf{Q}(\eta)
are natural isomorphisms. Abusing notation, we will often write
\mathbf{Q}(\mathbf{S}(Y))=Y
and
\mathbf{Q}(X)=\mathbf{Q}(\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{Q}(X)))
, if
Y\in\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T}
and
X\in\mathcal{G}
;

•

the left adjoint
\mathbf{Q}
is an exact functor such that
\mathrm{Ker}(\mathbf{Q})=\mathcal{T}
, that is,
\mathbf{Q}(X)=0
if and only if
X\in\mathcal{T}
. In particular, given a morphism
\phi\colon X\to Y
in
\mathcal{G}
,
\mathbf{Q}(\phi)
is a monomorphism (resp., epimorphism) if, and only if,
\mathrm{Ker}(\phi)\in\mathcal{T}
(resp.,
\mathrm{Coker}(\phi)\in\mathcal{T}
). In particular,
\mathrm{Ker}(\eta_{X}),\,\mathrm{Coker}(\eta_{X})\in\mathcal{T}
, for all
X\in\mathcal{G}
;

•

as
\mathbf{Q}
is exact,
\mathbf{S}
sends injectives in
\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T}
to injectives in
\mathcal{G}
. In fact, the adjunction
\mathbf{Q}\dashv\mathbf{S}
restricts to an equivalence of categories
\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T})\cong\mathcal{F}\cap\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{G})=\mathcal{I}
.

With a slight abuse of notation, denote by
\mathbf{Q}\dashv\mathbf{S}\colon\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G})\leftrightarrows\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T})
also the adjunction induced pointwise on complexes. It is easy to reformulate the above lemma as follows:

Corollary 8.8

(
[
35
, Lemmas 4.4 and 5.6]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a Grothendieck category,
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{G})
an injective class of injectives, and let
\tau_{\mathcal{I}}=(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})
be the associated hereditary torsion pair. Then

(1)

a morphism
\phi
in
\mathcal{G}
is an
\mathcal{I}
-monomorphism if, and only,
\mathbf{Q}(\phi)
is a monomorphism in
\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T}
;

(2)

a morphism
\phi^{\bullet}
in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G})
is an
\mathcal{I}
-weak equivalence if, and only if,
\mathbf{Q}(\phi^{\bullet})
is a quasi-isomorphism or, equivalently, if
\mathbf{Q}(\mathrm{cone}(\phi^{\bullet}))
is an acyclic complex.

In particular, a morphism
\phi^{\bullet}
in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G})
is a (trivial)
\mathcal{I}
-cofibration if and only if
\mathbf{Q}(\phi^{\bullet})
is a (trivial)
\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T})
-cofibration in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T})
.

The following is a more precise version of
[
35
, Theorem 5.7]
:

Theorem 8.9
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a Grothendieck category,
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{G})
an injective class of injectives, and let
\tau_{\mathcal{I}}=(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})
be the associated hereditary torsion pair. Then:

(1)

\psi^{\bullet}
is an
\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T})
-fibration in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T})
, if and only if
\mathbf{S}(\psi^{\bullet})
is an
\mathcal{I}
-fibration;

(2)

E^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{I})\subseteq\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G})
is
\mathcal{I}
-fibrant if, and only if,
\mathbf{Q}(E^{\bullet})
is
dg
-injective in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T})
;

(3)

any complex
X^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G})
admits an
\mathcal{I}
-fibrant replacement
\lambda^{\bullet}\colon X^{\bullet}\to E^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{I})
.

In particular,
(\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G}),\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{I}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{I}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{I}})
is a model category, and the following is a Quillen equivalence

\mathbf{Q}\dashv\mathbf{S}\colon\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G})\leftrightarrows\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T}),

when
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T})
is endowed with the usual injective model structure. As a consequence, we can describe the homotopy category
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{G};\mathcal{I}):=\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G})[\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{I}}^{-1}]
as a Verdier quotient of the usual derived category as follows:
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{G};\mathcal{I})\cong\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T})\cong\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{G})/\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathcal{G})
(where
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathcal{G})
is the full, localizing, subcategory of those
X^{\bullet}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{G})
such that
H^{n}(X^{\bullet})\in\mathcal{T}
, for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
).

Proof.

Fix a morphism
\psi^{\bullet}\colon Y_{1}^{\bullet}\to Y_{2}^{\bullet}
in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T})
and let
\phi^{\bullet}\colon X_{1}^{\bullet}\to X_{2}^{\bullet}
be a trivial
\mathcal{I}
-cofibration in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G})
. By Corollary
8.8

\mathbf{Q}(\phi^{\bullet})
is a trivial
\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T})
-cofibration, and all the trivial
\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T})
-cofibrations in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T})
are of this form. Consider now the following commutative diagrams:

\textstyle{X_{1}^{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi^{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{\ \alpha_{1}^{\bullet}\ }
\textstyle{\mathbf{S}(Y_{1}^{\bullet})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{S}(\psi^{\bullet})}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Q}(X_{1})^{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{Q}(\phi^{\bullet})}
\scriptstyle{\ \beta_{1}^{\bullet}\ }
\textstyle{Y_{1}^{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi^{\bullet}}
\textstyle{X_{2}^{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\ \alpha_{2}^{\bullet}\ }
\textstyle{\mathbf{S}(Y_{2}^{\bullet})}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Q}(X_{2})^{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\ \beta_{2}^{\bullet}\ }
\textstyle{Y_{2}^{\bullet},}

where
\alpha_{i}^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G})}(X_{i}^{\bullet},\mathbf{S}(Y_{i}^{\bullet}))
and
\beta_{i}^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T})}(\mathbf{Q}(X_{i}^{\bullet}),Y_{i}^{\bullet})
(for
i=1,2
) correspond to each other via the adjunction
\mathbf{Q}\dashv\mathbf{S}
. By adjunction,
\phi^{\bullet}\boxslash\mathbf{S}(\psi^{\bullet})
if, and only if,
\mathbf{Q}(\phi^{\bullet})\boxslash\psi^{\bullet}
, proving (1). In particular,
E^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{I})\subseteq\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G})
is
\mathcal{I}
-fibrant if, and only if,
E^{\bullet}\to 0
is an
\mathcal{I}
-fibration, if and only if
\mathbf{Q}(E^{\bullet})\to 0
is an
\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T})
-fibration, i.e.,
\mathbf{Q}(E^{\bullet})\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T}))
is a
dg
-injective complex, verifying (2). For (3), let
X^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G})
and consider a
dg
-injective resolution
\lambda^{\bullet}\colon\mathbf{Q}(X^{\bullet})\to E^{\bullet}
in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T})
. We claim that the following composition is an
\mathcal{I}
-fibrant replacement

\mathbf{S}(\lambda^{\bullet})\circ\eta_{X^{\bullet}}\colon X^{\bullet}\longrightarrow\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{Q}(X^{\bullet}))\longrightarrow\mathbf{S}(E^{\bullet}).

Indeed, by Corollary
8.8
, both the unit
\eta_{X^{\bullet}}
(which is sent to an isomorphism by
\mathbf{Q}
) and the map
\mathbf{S}(\lambda^{\bullet})
(that is sent by
\mathbf{Q}
to
\lambda^{\bullet}=\mathbf{Q}(\mathbf{S}(\lambda^{\bullet}))
, which is a quasi-isomorphism by construction) are
\mathcal{I}
-weak equivalences. Hence, the composition
\mathbf{S}(\lambda^{\bullet})\circ\eta_{X^{\bullet}}
is an
\mathcal{I}
-weak equivalence, while the complex
\mathbf{S}(E^{\bullet})\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{I})
is
\mathcal{I}
-fibrant by part (2). By Theorem
7.10
, this also shows that
(\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G}),\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{I}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{I}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{I}})
is a model category. By Corollary
8.8
,
\mathbf{Q}
preserves both cofibrations and trivial cofibrations, so
\mathbf{Q}\dashv\mathbf{S}
is a Quillen adjunction. Finally, to see that this is a Quillen equivalence, take
\phi^{\bullet}\colon X^{\bullet}\to\mathbf{S}(Y^{\bullet})
in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G})
, whose adjoint map is
\psi^{\bullet}:=\mathbf{Q}(\phi^{\bullet})\colon\mathbf{Q}(X^{\bullet})\to\mathbf{Q}(\mathbf{S}(X^{\bullet}))=X^{\bullet}
; by Corollary
8.8
,
\phi^{\bullet}\in\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{I}}
if and only if
\psi^{\bullet}\in\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T})}
, concluding the proof.
∎

Observe that, for any object
X\in\mathcal{G}
, a morphism
\lambda\colon S^{0}(X)\to I^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}^{\geq 0}(\mathcal{I})
is a relative
\mathcal{I}
-injective resolution if, and only if,
\mathbf{Q}(\lambda)\colon S^{0}(\mathbf{Q}(X))\to\mathbf{Q}(I^{\bullet})
is a
dg
-injective resolution of
\mathbf{Q}(X)\in\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T}
. In particular, one can construct a resolution of a given
X\in\mathcal{G}
as follows: first build a
dg
-injective resolution
S^{0}(\mathbf{Q}(X))\to E^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}^{\geq 0}(\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T}))
of
\mathbf{Q}(X)
in
\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T}
, and then note that the composition
S^{0}(X)\to S^{0}(\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{Q}(X)))\to\mathbf{S}(E^{\bullet})
is the desired
\mathcal{I}
-injective resolution. Recall also that, being
\mathbf{S}
a right adjoint, it commutes with all limits (and, in particular, with products). Thus, given
(Y^{\bullet}_{\lambda})_{\Lambda}\subseteq\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T})
,
\mathbf{S}(\prod_{\Lambda}Y^{\bullet}_{\lambda})=\prod_{\Lambda}\mathbf{S}(Y^{\bullet}_{\lambda})
. Exploiting these two ideas, we can show that the (Ab.4
∗
)-
\mathcal{I}
-
k
condition in
\mathcal{G}
is equivalent to the “absolute” (Ab.4
∗
)-
k
condition in
\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T}
:

Proposition 8.10
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a Grothendieck category,
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{G})
an injective class of injectives, and let
\tau_{\mathcal{I}}=(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})
be the associated hereditary torsion pair. Then, the following are equivalent:

(1)

\mathcal{G}
is (Ab.4
∗
)-
\mathcal{I}
-
k
;

(2)

\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T}
is (Ab.4
∗
)-
k
(in the sense of Roos).

Proof.

Let
(X_{\lambda})_{\Lambda}\subseteq\mathcal{G}
and, for each
\lambda\in\Lambda
, take a
dg
-injective resolution
\lambda_{\lambda}\colon S^{0}(\mathbf{Q}(X_{\lambda}))\to E_{\lambda}^{\bullet}
in
\mathrm{Ch}^{\geq 0}(\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T}))
, so that
\mu_{\lambda}\colon S^{0}(X_{\lambda})\to\mathbf{S}(E_{\lambda}^{\bullet})\in\mathrm{Ch}^{\geq 0}(\mathcal{I})
is the corresponding relative
\mathcal{I}
-injective resolution, for all
\lambda\in\Lambda
, as discussed above. By Lemma
7.12
(1),
\mathcal{G}
is (Ab.4
∗
)-
\mathcal{I}
-
k
if, and only if:

(8.2)

\textstyle{H^{-n}(\mathrm{Hom}(\prod_{\Lambda}\mathbf{S}(E_{\lambda}^{\bullet}),I))=0,\qquad\text{for all $n>k$, and all $I\in\mathcal{I}$.}}

Since
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{G})
by hypothesis, the functor
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{G}}(-,I)
is exact and, therefore, it commutes with cohomologies, so (
8.2
) becomes equivalent to:

\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{G}}(H^{n}(\prod_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{S}(E_{\lambda}^{\bullet})),I)=0,\qquad\text{for all $n>k$, and all $I\in\mathcal{I}$.}}

As, by definition,
\mathcal{T}:={}^{\perp}\mathcal{I}
, the above condition means exactly that
H^{n}(\prod_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{S}(E_{\lambda}^{\bullet}))\in\mathcal{T}
, for all
n>k
, that is,
\mathbf{Q}(H^{n}(\prod_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{S}(E_{\lambda}^{\bullet})))=0
, for all
n>k
. Using that
\mathbf{Q}
commutes with cohomologies,
\mathbf{S}
commutes with products, and
\mathbf{Q}\circ\mathbf{S}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T}}
, condition (
8.2
) becomes equivalent to:

\textstyle{\prod^{(n)}_{\Lambda}\mathbf{Q}(X_{\lambda}):=H^{n}(\prod_{\Lambda}E_{\lambda}^{\bullet})=H^{n}(\mathbf{Q}\circ\mathbf{S}(\prod_{\Lambda}E_{\lambda}^{\bullet}))=0,\qquad\text{for all $n>k$.}}

This last equation is precisely the (Ab.4
∗
)-
k
condition for the quotient category
\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T}
.
∎

8.3.
The pure derived category of a locally finitely presented Grothendieck category

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a Grothendieck category and recall that an object
X
in
\mathcal{G}
is said to be
finitely presented
if
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{G}}(X,-)
commutes with directed colimits. We denote by
\mathrm{fp}(\mathcal{G})
the full subcategory of finitely presented objects in
\mathcal{G}
, and we say that
\mathcal{G}
is
locally finitely presented
provided
\mathrm{fp}(\mathcal{G})
is essentially small, and it generates
\mathcal{G}
. Observe that, whenever
\mathcal{G}
is locally finitely presented, the so-called
restricted Yoneda embedding
gives a fully faithful functor

\mathbf{y}\colon\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow[\mathrm{fp}(\mathcal{G})^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Ab}],\text{ such that $\mathbf{y}(X)=\mathbf{y}_{X}:=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{G}}(-,X)_{\restriction\mathrm{fp}(\mathcal{G})}$.}

Observe that
\mathbf{y}
is left exact, and that it commutes with products and directed colimits. The category
[\mathrm{fp}(\mathcal{G})^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Ab}]
is generated by
\mathbf{y}(\mathrm{fp}(\mathcal{G})):=\{\mathbf{y}_{P}:P\in\mathrm{fp}(\mathcal{G})\}
, the essentially small subcategory of the representable (=finitely presented projective) functors, and so
[\mathrm{fp}(\mathcal{G})^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Ab}]
is an (Ab.4
∗
) Grothendieck category (it is equivalent to a category of modules over a ring with enough idempotents). Let us also recall that:

•

F\colon\mathrm{fp}(\mathcal{G})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Ab}
is said to be
flat
if it belongs to the class
\mathcal{F}:=\varinjlim\mathbf{y}(\mathrm{fp}(\mathcal{G}))
. The restricted Yoneda embedding induces an equivalence
\mathcal{G}\cong\mathbf{y}(\mathcal{G})=\mathcal{F}
;

•

the functors in the class
\mathcal{C}:=\mathcal{F}^{\perp_{1}}=(\mathbf{y}(\mathrm{fp}(\mathcal{G})))^{\perp_{1}}
are called
cotorsion
, and
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{C})
is a complete cotorsion pair in
[\mathrm{fp}(\mathcal{G})^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Ab}]
(see
[
5
, Theorem 2.6]
);

•

a short exact sequence
0\to X\to Y\to Z\to 0
in
\mathcal{G}
is said to be
pure exact
if, and only if,
0\to\mathbf{y}_{X}\to\mathbf{y}_{Y}\to\mathbf{y}_{Z}\to 0
is exact in
[\mathrm{fp}(\mathcal{G})^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Ab}]
. We say that
X\to Y
is a
pure monomorphism
if it can be completed to a pure exact sequence
0\to X\to Y\to Z\to 0
;

•

an object
E\in\mathcal{G}
is said to be
pure injective
if it is injective with respect to all the pure exact sequences in
\mathcal{G}
, i.e., if
0\to\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{G}}(Z,E)\to\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{G}}(Y,E)\to\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{G}}(X,E)\to 0
is exact in
\mathrm{Ab}
, for any pure exact sequence
0\to X\to Y\to Z\to 0
in
\mathcal{G}
;

•

if we denote by
\mathrm{P.Inj}(\mathcal{G})
the class of pure injectives in
\mathcal{G}
, the restricted Yoneda embedding induces an equivalence
\mathrm{P.Inj}(\mathcal{G})\cong\mathbf{y}(\mathrm{P.Inj}(\mathcal{G}))=\mathcal{F}\cap\mathcal{C}
(see
[
13
, Lemma 3]
).

Proposition 8.11
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a locally finitely presented Grothendieck category, and
\mathcal{I}:=\mathrm{P.Inj}(\mathcal{G})
:

(1)

\mathcal{I}
is an injective class in
\mathcal{G}
, and the
\mathcal{I}
-monomorphisms are the pure monomorphisms;

(2)

\mathcal{G}
is (Ab.4
∗
)-
\mathcal{I}
, and so
(\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G}),\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{I}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{I}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{I}})
is a model category.

In particular, the homotopy category
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{G};\mathcal{I}):=\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{G})[\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{I}}^{-1}]
is locally small, and it is equivalent to the so-called
pure derived category

\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{pure}}(\mathcal{G})
introduced in
[
4
, Section 5.3]
when
\mathcal{G}
is a module category, and studied in
[
18
]
.

Proof.

Recall from
[
13
, Theorem 6]
that, for any
X\in\mathcal{G}
, there exists a pure exact sequence
0\to X\to PE(X)\to PE(X)/X\to 0
in
\mathcal{G}
, with
PE(X)\in\mathcal{I}
. In particular,
\mathcal{I}
is cogenerating.

(1) It is clear that, if
X\to Y
is a pure monomorphism, then it is also an
\mathcal{I}
-monomorphism (by our definition of pure injective object). On the other hand, if
f\colon X\to Y
is an
\mathcal{I}
-monomorphism, take a pure monomorphism
g\colon X\to PE(X)
, with
PE(X)\in\mathcal{I}
, and observe that (by definition of
\mathcal{I}
-monomorphism)
f^{*}\colon\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{G}}(Y,PE(X))\to\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{G}}(X,PE(X))
is surjective. In particular, there exists
\tilde{g}\colon Y\to PE(X)
, such that
\tilde{g}\circ f=:f^{*}(\tilde{g})=g
. In other words,
\mathbf{y}_{g}=\mathbf{y}_{\tilde{g}}\circ\mathbf{y}_{f}
is a monomorphism in
[\mathrm{fp}(\mathcal{G})^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Ab}]
, and so
\mathbf{y}_{f}
is a monomorphism; i.e.,
f
is a pure monomorphism.

(2) Consider a family of pure exact sequences
\{0\to X_{\lambda}\to Y_{\lambda}\to Z_{\lambda}\to 0\}_{\Lambda}
in
\mathcal{G}
, which gives a family of short exact sequences of functors
\{0\to\mathbf{y}_{X_{\lambda}}\to\mathbf{y}_{Y_{\lambda}}\to\mathbf{y}_{Z_{\lambda}}\to 0\}_{\Lambda}
. Since
[\mathrm{fp}(\mathcal{G})^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Ab}]
is (Ab.4
∗
), we obtain a short exact sequence
0\to\prod_{\Lambda}\mathbf{y}_{X_{\lambda}}\to\prod_{\Lambda}\mathbf{y}_{Y_{\lambda}}\to\prod_{\Lambda}\mathbf{y}_{Z_{\lambda}}\to 0
. Moreover, since the restricted Yoneda embedding commutes with products, we obtain the following short exact sequence:
0\to\mathbf{y}_{\prod_{\Lambda}\mathbf{y}_{\lambda}}\to\mathbf{y}_{\prod_{\Lambda}Y_{\lambda}}\to\mathbf{y}_{\prod_{\Lambda}Z_{\lambda}}\to 0
. Hence,
0\to\prod_{\Lambda}X_{\lambda}\to\prod_{\Lambda}Y_{\lambda}\to\prod_{\Lambda}Z_{\lambda}\to 0
is pure exact (i.e.,
\mathcal{I}
-exact) in
\mathcal{G}
. One can now conclude by Lemma
8.3
and Corollary
7.16
.

For the last statements, it is enough to check that the
\mathcal{I}
-acyclic complexes are precisely the pure-acyclic complexes. As both the
\mathcal{I}
-acyclic and the pure-acyclic complexes are acyclic, this reduces to prove that a short exact sequence
0\rightarrow A\to B\to C\rightarrow 0
in
\mathcal{G}
is pure if, and only if, it “is” an
\mathcal{I}
-acyclic complex which, in turn, is equivalent to prove that a monomorphism
u\colon A\to B
in
\mathcal{G}
is pure if, and only if, it is an
\mathcal{I}
-monomorphism, that follows by (1).
∎

8.4.
Categories of finite
\mathcal{I}
-global dimension

A rich source of examples of (Ab.4
∗
)-
\mathcal{I}
-
k
Abelian categories is derived from the following concepts (see Corollary
8.13
below):

Definition 8.12
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a complete Abelian category and let
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
be an injective class. For each object
A
in
\mathcal{A}
, define the following set:

\mathbb{N}(A,\mathcal{I}):=\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\text{there is a relative $\mathcal{I}$-injective resolution $A\to I^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Ch}^{\geq 0}(\mathcal{A})\cap\mathrm{Ch}^{\leq n}(\mathcal{A})$}\}.

Then, we define the
\mathcal{I}
-codimension
of
A
as follows:

\mathcal{I}\text{-}\mathrm{codim}(A):=\inf\mathbb{N}(A,\mathcal{I}),

with the convention that
\inf\emptyset:=\infty
. Finally, we define the
\mathcal{I}
-global dimension of
\mathcal{A}
as:

\mathcal{I}\text{-}\mathrm{gl.dim}(\mathcal{A}):=\sup\{\mathcal{I}\text{-}\mathrm{codim}(A):A\in\mathcal{A}\}\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}.

We have the following immediate consequence.

Corollary 8.13
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a complete Abelian category and let
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
be an injective class such that
\mathcal{I}\text{-}\mathrm{gl.dim}(\mathcal{A})=d<\infty
. Then,
\mathcal{A}
is (Ab.4
∗
)-
\mathcal{I}
-
d
.

Proof.

Let
(A_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
be a family of objects in
\mathcal{A}
and choose, for each
\lambda
, a relative
\mathcal{I}
-injective resolution
u_{\lambda}\colon A_{\lambda}\to I^{\bullet}_{\lambda}\in\mathrm{Ch}^{\geq 0}(\mathcal{A})\cap\mathrm{Ch}^{\leq d}(\mathcal{A})
. Observe then that
\prod_{\Lambda}I^{\bullet}_{\lambda}\in\mathrm{Ch}^{\geq 0}(\mathcal{A})\cap\mathrm{Ch}^{\leq d}(\mathcal{A})
and, therefore,
\mathrm{Hom}(\prod_{\Lambda}I^{\bullet}_{\lambda},I)\in\mathrm{Ch}^{\leq 0}(\mathrm{Ab})\cap\mathrm{Ch}^{\geq-d}(\mathrm{Ab})
. In particular,
H^{k}(\mathrm{Hom}(\prod_{\Lambda}I^{\bullet}_{\lambda},I))=0
, for all
k<-d
, that is,
\mathcal{A}
is (Ab.4
∗
)-
\mathcal{I}
-
d
.
∎

We are now going to describe a few concrete examples of bicomplete Abelian categories
\mathcal{A}
that admit a suitable injective class
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
for which
\mathcal{I}\text{-}\mathrm{gl.dim}(\mathcal{A})<\infty
. By the above corollary, these are also examples where
\mathcal{A}
is (Ab.4
∗
)-
\mathcal{I}
-
k
, for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
. In particular, by Corollary
7.16
, in each of these cases
(\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A}),\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{I}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{I}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{I}})
is a model category, and so the corresponding homotopy category
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A};\mathcal{I}):=\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})[\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{I}}^{-1}]
is locally small. The first two examples below have also appeared in
[
3
, Section 9]
, with different terminology.

Example 8.14
.

Let
R
be a
d
-Gorenstein ring, i.e., a (left and right) Noetherian ring that has injective dimension
=d
on both sides, for a given
d\in\mathbb{N}
, and consider the following class:

\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{GI}(R):=\{M\in\mathrm{Mod}\text{-}R:\text{ $M$ is Gorenstein injective}\}\subseteq\mathrm{Mod}\text{-}R=:\mathcal{A}.

By
[
7
, Theorem 2.3]
,
\mathcal{I}
is an injective class in
\mathrm{Mod}\text{-}R
, while it follows by
[
6
, Chapter 12]
that
\mathcal{I}\text{-}\mathrm{gl.dim}(\mathcal{A})=d
.

Example 8.15
.

Let
R
be a ring of pure global dimension
=d
, for some
d\in\mathbb{N}
(e.g., if
d>0
and
|R|\leq\aleph_{d-1}
, then
R
has pure global dimension
\leq d
). Let
\mathcal{A}:=\mathrm{Mod}\text{-}R
, and consider the injective class
\mathcal{I}:=\mathrm{P.Inj}(\mathcal{A})
of the pure-injective right
R
-modules. By definition (see
[
12
, Section 1]
), the pure global dimension of
R
is the supremum of the pure-injective dimensions of its modules. Hence,
\mathcal{I}\text{-}\mathrm{gl.dim}(\mathcal{A})=d
coincides with the pure global dimension of
R
.

In a bicomplete Abelian category
\mathcal{A}
, a given
V\in\mathcal{A}
is
1
-tilting
(
[
24
, Definition 6.1]
) if:

•

V^{\perp_{1}}:=\mathrm{Ker}(\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{A}}(V,-))=\mathrm{Gen}(V)=:\mathcal{I}
;

•

\mathcal{I}
is cogenerating in
\mathcal{A}
.

We refer to
[
24
, Section 6]
for several characterizations of
1
-tilting objects and their connection with the traditional concept of a
1
-tilting module. In the following example we show that, if
V
is
1
-tilting, then
\mathrm{Gen}(V)\text{-}\mathrm{gl.dim}(\mathcal{A})=1
; for an extension to the
n
-tilting case, see Subsection
8.5
.

Example 8.16
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be bicomplete Abelian category with a generator,
V\in\mathcal{A}
a
1
-tilting object, and let
\mathcal{I}:=\mathrm{Gen}(V)=V^{\perp_{1}}
. By
[
24
, Proposition 6.7]
, there is a generator
G
of
\mathcal{A}
together with a short exact sequence
0\rightarrow G\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u}}{{\rightarrow}}V_{0}\rightarrow V_{1}\rightarrow 0
(with
V_{0},\,V_{1}\in\mathrm{Add}(V)
), such that any coproduct of copies of the sequence remains exact. Now, given
A\in\mathcal{A}
, choose an epimorphism
\pi\colon G^{(S)}\rightarrow A
and observe that
u^{(S)}\colon G^{(S)}\rightarrow V_{0}^{(S)}
is an
\mathcal{I}
-preenvelope, since
\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}(V_{1}^{(S)},I)=0
, for all
I\in\mathcal{I}
. Taking the push-out of
u^{(S)}
along
\pi
, one gets a short exact sequence
0\rightarrow A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle v}}{{\rightarrow}}I_{A}\rightarrow V_{1}^{(S)}\rightarrow 0
, with
I_{A}\in\mathcal{I}
. By construction,
v
is clearly an
\mathcal{I}
-preenvelope, so
\mathcal{I}\text{-}\mathrm{codim}(A)\leq 1
. Thus,
\mathcal{I}\text{-}\mathrm{gl.dim}(\mathcal{A})=1
.

Example 8.17
.

Let
V
be a projective variety over a field
K
, i.e., a closed set for the Zariski topology of
\mathbf{P}^{n}(K)
, for some integer
n>0
, let
\Gamma(V)
be its (graded) ring of coordinates, and let
\mathcal{A}:=\mathrm{Gr}\text{-}\Gamma(V)
be the category of graded
\Gamma(V)
-modules. If
V
is regular of dimension
d
, let
\mathcal{I}
be the class of injective objects of
\mathrm{Gr}\text{-}\Gamma(V)
that have zero graded socle. Now, if
\mathcal{G}:=\mathrm{Qcoh}(V)
is the Grothendieck category of quasi-coherent sheaves on
V
, when we view
V
as a scheme in the usual way, the regularity of
V
implies that its dimension coincides with the global dimension of
\mathcal{G}
, i.e.,
\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{G})\text{-}\mathrm{gl.dim}(\mathcal{G})
with our terminology. A well-known result of Serre (see Proposition 7.8 in Section 59, p. 252 in
[
30
]
) says that there is an equivalence of categories
\mathcal{G}\cong(\mathrm{Gr}\text{-}\Gamma(V))/\mathcal{T}
, where
\mathcal{T}\subseteq\mathrm{Gr}\text{-}\Gamma(V)
is the hereditary torsion class of the locally finite graded
\Gamma(V)
-modules. This is exactly the torsion class generated by the class
\mathcal{S}
of graded-simple modules, and hence the corresponding torsion pair is cogenerated by
\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\cap\mathrm{Inj}(\Gamma(V))=\mathcal{I}
. By the discussion right before Proposition
8.10
, it is now easy to see that
\mathcal{I}\text{-}\mathrm{gl.dim}(\mathcal{A})=d
.

Proposition 8.18
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an Abelian category such that
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
is locally small, and let
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
be a right complete hereditary cotorsion pair. Then, the following are equivalent for any
n\geq 0
:

(1)

\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{k}(X^{\prime},X)=0
, for all
X,X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
and all
k>n
;

(2)

\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{n+1}(X^{\prime},X)=0
, for all
X,X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
;

(3)

for any
X\in\mathcal{X}
, there is an exact sequence of the form:
0\rightarrow X\rightarrow W^{0}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow W^{n}\rightarrow 0
, with
W^{i}\in\mathcal{W}:=\mathcal{X}\cap\mathcal{Y}
, for all
i=0,\dots,n
.

Moreover, if
\mathcal{Y}
-
\mathrm{gl.dim}(\mathcal{A})\leq n
, then the above equivalent conditions are all verified.

Proof.

(1)
\Rightarrow
(2) is clear.

(2)
\Rightarrow
(3). Let
X\in\mathcal{X}
and consider a right
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
-approximation
0\to X\to W^{0}\to X^{1}\to 0
, where
W^{0}\in\mathcal{Y}
,
X^{1}\in\mathcal{X}
and, since
\mathcal{X}={}^{\perp_{1}}\mathcal{Y}
is closed under extensions, also
W^{0}\in\mathcal{X}
, so that
W^{0}\in\mathcal{W}
. Continue by taking a right
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
-approximation
0\to X^{1}\to W^{1}\to X^{2}\to 0
, with
W^{1}\in\mathcal{W}
and
X^{2}\in\mathcal{X}
, and proceed inductively in this way to construct a
\mathcal{W}
-coresolution

(8.3)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{W^{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d^{0}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d^{n-1}}
\textstyle{W^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d^{n}}
\textstyle{\cdots,}

with
\mathrm{Im}(d^{i})\in\mathcal{X}
, for all
i\geq 0
. Observe that, letting
X^{\prime}:=\mathrm{Im}(d^{n})\in\mathcal{X}
, any object
W\in\mathcal{W}
is
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(X^{\prime},-)
-acyclic (i.e.,
\mathrm{Ext}^{i}_{\mathcal{A}}(X^{\prime},W)=0
, for all
i>0
), and so the
\mathcal{W}
-coresolution in (
8.3
) gives the following formula:
\mathrm{Ext}^{i}_{\mathcal{A}}(X^{\prime},X)\cong H^{i}(\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(X^{\prime},W^{\bullet}))
, for all
i>0
. By (2), we deduce that
H^{n+1}(\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(X^{\prime},W^{\bullet}))=0
or, equivalently, that the map
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(X^{\prime},W^{n})\to\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(X^{\prime},X^{\prime})
is surjective, that is,
W^{n}\cong\mathrm{Im}(d^{n-1})\oplus X^{\prime}
, showing that
\mathrm{Im}(d^{n-1})\in\mathcal{W}
, as this class is closed under summands. In particular, we obtain a
\mathcal{W}
-coresolution of the desired length:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{W^{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d^{0}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d^{n-2}}
\textstyle{W^{n-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d^{n-1}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Im}(d^{n-1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots.}

(3)
\Rightarrow
(1). Given
X,\,X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
, take a
\mathcal{W}
-coresolution
0\rightarrow X\rightarrow W^{0}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow W^{n}\rightarrow 0
and observe that, as in the previous implication,
\mathrm{Ext}^{i}_{\mathcal{A}}(X^{\prime},X)\cong H^{i}(\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(X^{\prime},W^{\bullet}))
, for all
i>0
. Given
k>n
,
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(X^{\prime},W^{k})=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(X^{\prime},0)=0
, and so
\mathrm{Ext}^{k}_{\mathcal{A}}(X^{\prime},X)\cong H^{k}(\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(X^{\prime},W^{\bullet}))=0
.

For the final statement, let
X,\,X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
. If
\mathcal{Y}
-
\mathrm{gl.dim}(\mathcal{A})\leq n
, take a relative
\mathcal{Y}
-injective resolution

(8.4)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{Y^{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d^{0}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d^{n-1}}
\textstyle{Y^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots.}

Since
\mathcal{Y}
is cogenerating, the sequence (
8.4
) is exact while, as any
Y\in\mathcal{Y}
is
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(X^{\prime},-)
-acyclic,
\mathrm{Ext}^{i}_{\mathcal{A}}(X^{\prime},X)\cong H^{i}(\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(X^{\prime},Y^{\bullet}))
, for all
i>0
. This clearly implies (1).
∎

As a direct consequence of Proposition
8.18
, we get:

Corollary 8.19
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an Abelian category such that
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
is locally small, let
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
be a right complete hereditary cotorsion pair in
\mathcal{A}
, and fix
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that the equivalent conditions of Proposition
8.18
are verified. Then,
\mathcal{Y}\text{-}\mathrm{gl.dim}(\mathcal{A})\leq n+1
and, therefore,
\mathcal{A}
is (Ab.4
∗
)-
\mathcal{Y}
-
(n+1)
.

Proof.

Given
A\in\mathcal{A}
, take a right
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
-approximation
0\rightarrow A\rightarrow Y\rightarrow X\rightarrow 0
, with
Y\in\mathcal{Y}
and
X\in\mathcal{X}
. Take now a
\mathcal{W}
-coresolution of
X
as in Proposition
8.18
(3), that is, an exact sequence
0\rightarrow X\rightarrow W^{0}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow W^{n}\rightarrow 0
, with
W^{i}\in\mathcal{W}
for all
i=0,\dots,n
. We obtain an exact sequence
0\rightarrow A\rightarrow Y\rightarrow W^{0}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow W^{n}\rightarrow 0
, that is clearly a relative
\mathcal{Y}
-injective resolution of
A
.
∎

8.5.
n
-Tilting cotorsion pairs

In this subsection we will work on an Abelian category
\mathcal{A}
that is (Ab.4), and such that
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
is locally small. In particular,
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
has arbitrary coproducts, which are computed degreewise. These conditions are always verified in each of the following cases:

•

if
\mathcal{A}
is a Grothendieck category;

•

if
\mathcal{A}
is a bicomplete (Ab.4) Abelian category with enough projectives; this follows by
[
3
, Theorem 6.4]
, or even Corollary
7.16
, applied to
\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{op}}
with the injective class
\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{op}})=(\mathrm{Proj(\mathcal{A})})^{\mathrm{op}}
.

Recall that the
projective dimension

\mathrm{p.dim}_{\mathcal{A}}(X)
of an object
X\in\mathcal{A}
is defined as follows:

\mathrm{p.dim}_{\mathcal{A}}(X):=\inf\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{k}(T,A)=0,\,\forall k>n,\,\forall A\in\mathcal{A}\},

with the convention that
\inf\emptyset=\infty
. The following definition comes from
[
22
, Definition 6.8]
:

Definition 8.20
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an (Ab.4) Abelian category such that
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
is locally small, and let
n>0
be an integer. An object
T
of
\mathcal{A}
is called
n
-tilting
when the following conditions hold:

(T.1)

\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{k}(T,T^{(I)})=0
, for any set
I
and all
k>0
;

(T.2)

\mathrm{p.dim}_{\mathcal{A}}(T)=n
;

(T.3)

there is a generating class
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
such that, for each
P\in\mathcal{P}
, there is an exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{T^{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d^{0}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d^{n-1}}
\textstyle{T^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

with
T^{k}\in\mathrm{Add}(T)
, for all
k=0,\dots,n
.

Any
n
-tilting object, has an associated cotorsion pair, that we describe in the following lemma:

Lemma 8.21
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an (Ab.4) Abelian category such that
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
is locally small, let
T\in\mathcal{A}
be
n
-tilting (for some
n\geq 1
), and let
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}):=(^{\perp_{1}}(T^{\perp_{>0}}),T^{\perp_{>0}})
be the corresponding
n
-tilting cotorsion pair
. Then, the following statements hold true:

(1)

(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
is a hereditary cotorsion pair in
\mathcal{A}
;

(2)

\mathcal{W}:=\mathcal{X}\cap\mathcal{Y}=\mathrm{Add}(T)
;

(3)

(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
is right complete and, therefore,
\mathcal{Y}
is a cogenerating injective class;

(4)

\mathcal{Y}\text{-}\mathrm{gl.dim}(\mathcal{A})\leq n
and, therefore,
\mathcal{A}
satisfies the (Ab.4
∗
)-
\mathcal{Y}
-
n
condition;

(5)

the class
\mathcal{X}
is generating in
\mathcal{A}
.

Proof.

(1) follows immediately by the definition of
n
-tilting object.

(2) The inclusion “
\mathrm{Add}(T)\subseteq\mathcal{W}
” is clear. On the other hand, given
W\in\mathcal{W}\subseteq\mathcal{Y}\subseteq\mathrm{Gen}(T)
(see
[
22
, Theorem 6.3 and Remark 6.2]
and their proofs), if we denote by
\varepsilon_{f}\colon T\to T^{(\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(T,W))}
the inclusion in the coproduct of the copy of
T
corresponding to the morphism
f\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(T,W)
, we get a canonical morphism
p_{W}\colon T^{(\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(T,W))}\rightarrow W
such that
p_{W}\circ\varepsilon_{f}=f
, for all
f\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(T,W)
. The condition
W\in\mathrm{Gen}(T)
can be expressed equivalently by saying that
p_{W}
is an epimorphism; let
W^{\prime}:=\ker(p_{W})
. The condition
f=p_{W}\circ\varepsilon_{f}=:p_{W}^{*}(\varepsilon_{f})
for all
f\colon T\to W
, shows the surjectivity of
p_{W}^{*}\colon\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(T,T^{(\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(T,W))})\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(T,W)
, so
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{A}}(T,W^{\prime})=0
by the exact sequence below:

\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(T,T^{(\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(T,W))})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{W}^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(T,W)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{A}}(T,W^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{A}}(T,T^{(\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(T,W))})=0,}

where the last term on the right is
=0
by condition (T.1). Furthermore,
\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{k}(T,W^{\prime})=0
, for all
k\geq 2
, as shown by the following exact sequence:

\textstyle{0=\mathrm{Ext}^{k-1}_{\mathcal{A}}(T,W)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Ext}^{k}_{\mathcal{A}}(T,W^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Ext}^{k}_{\mathcal{A}}(T,T^{(\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(T,W))})=0.}

Thus, we have shown that
W^{\prime}\in T^{\perp_{>0}}=\mathcal{Y}
. Moreover,
W^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
, since
\mathcal{X}
is closed under kernels of epimorphisms, and so
W^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}
. As a consequence,
\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}(W,W^{\prime})=0
, and so the exact sequence
0\to W^{\prime}\to T^{(\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(T,W))}\rightarrow W\to 0
has to split. In particular,
W\in\text{Add}(T)
as desired.

(3)-(4) By
[
22
, Lemma 6.13]
, given
A\in\mathcal{A}
, there is an exact sequence of the form:

(8.5)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d^{0}}
\textstyle{T^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d^{1}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d^{n-1}}
\textstyle{T^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

such that
Y\in\mathcal{Y}
and
T^{k}\in\mathrm{Add}(T)=\mathcal{W}
, for all
k=1,\dots,n
. Since
\mathcal{X}
is closed under kernels of epimorphisms,
X^{i-1}:=\mathrm{Ker}(d^{i-1})\in\mathcal{X}
, for all
i=2,\dots,n
. In particular,
\mathrm{Coker}(u)\in\mathcal{X}
, showing that
0\rightarrow A\rightarrow Y\rightarrow\mathrm{Coker}(u)\rightarrow 0
is a right
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
-approximation of
A
, and concluding the proof of (3). Moreover, observe that
0\rightarrow X^{i-1}\rightarrow T^{i-1}\rightarrow X^{i}\rightarrow 0
is also a right
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
-approximation (of
X^{i-1}
), for all
i=2,\dots,n
and, therefore, the exact sequence in (
8.5
) is a relative
\mathcal{Y}
-injective resolution of
A
, showing that
\mathcal{Y}\text{-}\mathrm{codim}(A)\leq n
, as desired.

(5) Given
A\in\mathcal{A}
, we can take a right
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
-approximation
0\to A\to Y\to X\to 0
of
A
, which is a short exact sequence with
Y\in\mathcal{Y}
, and
X\in\mathcal{X}
. Now, as at the beginning of the proof of part (2) above, we observe that, by results of
[
22
]
, there is an inclusion
\mathcal{Y}\subseteq\mathrm{Gen}(T)
. As a consequence, there is a canonical epimorphism
W\to Y\to 0
, where
W:=T^{(\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(T,Y))}\in\mathrm{Add}(T)=\mathcal{W}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
. Consider now the following pullback diagram:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
P.B.
\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0}

We deduce that
A
is a quotient of
P
, which belongs to
\mathcal{X}
, since
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
is hereditary.
∎

In the following theorem we give a characterization of the tilting cotorsion pairs:

Theorem 8.22
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an (Ab.4) Abelian category such that
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
is locally small, and let
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
be a right complete, hereditary cotorsion pair, with
\mathcal{X}
generating in
\mathcal{A}
. If there is
W\in\mathcal{A}
such that
\mathrm{Add}(W)=\mathcal{X}\cap\mathcal{Y}=:\mathcal{W}
, and
\mathrm{p.dim}_{\mathcal{A}}(W)=n\leq m\in\mathbb{N}
, then the following are equivalent:

(1)

\mathcal{Y}\text{-}\mathrm{gl.dim}(\mathcal{A})\leq m
;

(2)

\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{m+1}(X^{\prime},X)=0
, for all
X,X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
;

(3)

there is a subcategory
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
which is generating in
\mathcal{A}
and such that, for each
P\in\mathcal{P}
, there is an exact sequence
0\rightarrow P\rightarrow W^{0}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow W^{m}\rightarrow 0
, with
W^{i}\in\mathcal{W}
, for all
i=0,\dots,m
.

If these conditions hold, then
W
is
n
-tilting, and
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
is its associated
n
-tilting cotorsion pair.

Proof.

The implications “(1)
\Rightarrow
(2)” and “(2)
\Rightarrow
(3)” follow by Proposition
8.18
, while the implication “(3)
\Rightarrow
(1)” will follow, by Lemma
8.21
(4), as a consequence of the last part of the statement. Thus, to conclude our proof, it is enough to show that (3) implies that
W
is
n
-tilting. In fact,
W
satisfies the condition (T.1) (see Definition
8.20
), since
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
is hereditary and
W\in\mathcal{X}\cap\mathcal{W}
, while (T.2) is given by hypothesis, so we just need to take care of the axiom (T.3). We proceed by induction on
m-n\geq 0
. Indeed, if
m=n
, then clearly (3) implies (T.3), so
W
is
n
-tilting. On the other hand, if
m>n

and given
P\in\mathcal{P}
, fix the following exact sequence as in (3):

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{W^{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d^{0}}
\textstyle{W^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d^{1}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d^{m-1}}
\textstyle{W^{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

Using again and again the fact that
\mathcal{X}
is closed under taking kernels of epimorphisms, one gets that
X^{k}:=\text{Im}(d^{k})\in\mathcal{X}
, for
k=-1,0,\ldots,m
, where
X^{-1}:=P
. Hence, the induced exact sequence

0\rightarrow X^{m-n-2}\rightarrow W^{m-n-1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow W^{m}\rightarrow 0

yields an element of
\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{n+1}(W^{m},X^{m-n-2})=0
. Since
\mathcal{W}
consists of
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(W^{m},-)
-acyclic objects, an argument similar to the one in the proof of implication “(2)
\Rightarrow
(3)” of Proposition
8.18
shows that
d^{m-1}\colon W^{m-1}\rightarrow W^{m}
is a retraction, so that
X^{m-2}\in\mathcal{W}
. Letting
\overline{W}^{k}:=W^{k}
for
k=0,1,...,m-2
and
\overline{W}^{m-1}:=X^{m-2}
, we get an exact sequence
0\rightarrow P\rightarrow\overline{W}^{0}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\overline{W}^{m-1}\rightarrow 0
, so we can conclude by the inductive hypothesis.
∎

Example 8.23
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a locally Noetherian Grothendieck category, and suppose that

\sup\{\mathrm{p.dim}_{\mathcal{A}}(I):I\text{ is an indecomposable injective}\}=n>0,

and that there is a set
\mathcal{S}
of generators, each of which has injective dimension
\leq n
. A coproduct
W
of all the indecomposable injectives, one for each isoclass, is an
n
-tilting object. Indeed, as
\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{A})
is closed under coproducts, conditions (T.1–3) of Definition
8.20
hold. In particular, if
\mathcal{Y}:=W^{\perp_{>0}}=(\mathrm{Inj}(\mathcal{A}))^{\perp_{>0}}
, Corollary
8.13
and Lemma
8.21
tell us that
\mathcal{A}
is (Ab.4
∗
)-
\mathcal{Y}
-
n
.

Observe that any locally Noetherian Grothendieck category
\mathcal{A}
whose homological dimension is
n
(i.e.,
\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{n}(-,-)\neq 0=\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{n+1}(-,-)
) satisfies the conditions of the above example. In particular, we get the following case, where we can completely describe the classes
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
:

Example 8.24
.

Let
R
be a
n
-Gorenstein ring,
\mathcal{A}:=\mathrm{Mod}\text{-}R
and define
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
as in Example
8.23
. Then, the subcategory
\mathcal{Y}
is precisely that of the Gorenstein injectives, and
\mathcal{X}:={}^{\perp_{1}}\mathcal{Y}
is the subcategory of modules of finite injective (=finite projective) dimension (see
[
11
, Example 8.13]
).

Remark 8.25
.

If
\mathcal{A}
is an (Ab.4
∗
) Abelian category such that
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
is locally small, then
\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{op}}
satisfies the hypotheses listed at the beginning of this subsection. This allows one to define an (Ab.4)-
\mathcal{X}
-
n
condition and to relate it to
n
-cotilting objects and
n
-cotilting cotorsion pairs.

8.6.
Examples and applications for categories of quasi-coherent sheaves

In this final subsection we show that the Grothendieck category
\mathrm{Qcoh}(X)
, where
X
is a scheme, is (Ab.4
∗
)-
n
(for some
n
), whenever
X
is quasi-compact and semi-separated (see Theorem
8.27
).

The idea of the following technical lemma comes from
[
14
, Lemma 3.2 and Remark 3.3]
. Here we prefer to give a more elementary inductive proof, avoiding the use of spectral sequences. We refer to
[
14
]
for other examples of (Ab.4
∗
)-
n
Grothendieck categories in geometric contexts.

Lemma 8.26
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a complete Abelian category with enough injectives, and take a finite coresolution of the identity
\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{A}}\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}
, i.e., an exact sequence of endofunctors of
\mathcal{A}
of the form:

(8.6)

0\overset{}{\Longrightarrow}\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{A}}\overset{\alpha_{0}}{\Longrightarrow}F_{1}\overset{\alpha_{1}}{\Longrightarrow}\cdots\overset{\alpha_{n}}{\Longrightarrow}F_{n+1}\overset{}{\Longrightarrow}0,\qquad\text{for some $n\geq 0$.}

If
\prod^{(k)}_{J}\circ F_{i}=0
, for any set
J
,
i=1,\ldots,n+1
, and all
k\geq 1
, then
\mathcal{A}
is (Ab.4
∗
)-
n
.

Proof.

For each
i=1,\dots,n
, let
K_{i}:=\ker(\alpha_{i})\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}
, i.e.,
K_{i}(A)=\ker((\alpha_{i})_{A})
, for all
A\in\mathcal{A}
, and let
K_{n+1}:=F_{n+1}
. The exactness of (
8.6
) allows us to build the following short exact sequences:

(
{\dagger}_{i}
)

0\Longrightarrow K_{i}\Longrightarrow F_{i}\Longrightarrow K_{i+1}\Longrightarrow 0,

for all
i=1,\dots,n
(of course, in (
{\dagger}_{1}
), we have
K_{1}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{A}}
). Given a set of objects
\{A_{j}\}_{J}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
, consider, for each
j\in J
, the short exact sequence
0\to K_{i}(A_{j})\to F_{i}(A_{j})\to K_{i+1}(A_{j})\to 0
, obtained evaluating (
{\dagger}_{i}
) at
A_{j}
. Taking products, we get the following long exact sequences:

(
{\ddagger}_{i}
)

0\to\prod_{J}K_{i}(A_{j})\to\prod_{J}F_{i}(A_{j})\to\prod_{J}K_{i+1}(A_{j})\to\prod^{(1)}_{J}K_{i}(A_{j})\to\prod^{(1)}_{J}F_{i}(A_{j})-

\to\prod^{(1)}_{J}K_{i+1}(A_{j})\to\prod^{(2)}_{J}K_{i}(A_{j})\to\prod^{(2)}_{J}F_{i}(A_{j})-\cdots\to\prod^{(k-1)}_{J}K_{i+1}(A_{j})\to\prod^{(k)}_{J}K_{i}(A_{j})\to\prod^{(k)}_{J}F_{i}(A_{j})\cdots

for all
i=1,\dots,n
. By hypothesis,
\prod^{(k)}_{J}F_{i}(A_{j})=0
(for all
k\geq 1
and
i=1,\ldots,n+1
). Hence, by the exactness of (
{\ddagger}_{n}
), also
\prod^{(k)}_{J}K_{n}(A_{j})=0
, for all
k\geq 2
, as it fits in between of the two vanishing objects
\prod^{(k-1)}_{J}F_{n}(A_{j})=0=\prod^{(k)}_{J}F_{n+1}(A_{j})=\prod^{(k)}_{J}K_{n+1}(A_{j})
. Similarly, for
1\leq i<n
, the exactness of (
{\ddagger}_{i}
), plus the conditions
\prod^{(k)}_{J}F_{i}(A_{j})=0=\prod^{(k-1)}_{J}K_{i+1}(A_{j})
, for all
k\geq n-i+2
, imply that
\prod^{(k)}_{J}K_{i}(A_{j})=0
, for all
k\geq n-i+2
. In particular, taking
i=1
, this gives
\prod^{(k)}_{J}A_{j}=\prod^{(k)}_{J}K_{1}(A_{j})=0
(as
K_{1}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{A}}
), for all
k\geq n+1
. Thus,
\mathcal{A}
is (Ab.4
∗
)-
n
, as desired.
∎

Theorem 8.27
.

Let
X
be a semi-separated, quasi-compact scheme that admits an affine open cover of the form
\{V_{1},\dots,V_{n+1}\}
(for some
n\geq 0
). Then,
\mathrm{Qcoh}(X)
is (Ab.4
∗
)-
n
.

Proof.

X
being semi-separated is equivalent to its family of open affines being closed under finite intersections (see
[
1
, Section 2]
). Hence, the inclusion of an open affine
\iota_{V}\colon V\hookrightarrow X
is an affine morphism of schemes and, therefore, both
\iota_{V}^{*}=(-)_{\restriction V}\colon\mathrm{Qcoh}(X)\to\mathrm{Qcoh}(V)\cong\mathrm{Mod}\text{-}{\mathcal{O}_{V}}
and its right adjoint
(\iota_{V})_{*}\colon\mathrm{Mod}\text{-}{\mathcal{O}_{V}}\to\mathrm{Qcoh}(X)
are exact: for the former just use that an open immersion is always a flat morphism of schemes, while for the latter we refer to
[
32
,
Lemma 01XC
]
. Then
(\iota_{V})_{*}
preserves products (being a right adjoint), it sends injective objects to injective objects (as its left adjoint is exact), and it also commutes with cohomology (since it is exact): this shows that
(\iota_{V})_{*}
commutes with
\prod_{J}^{(k)}
(for all
k\geq 0
, and any set
J
). Of course, the same holds if we take instead the coproduct (i.e., the “disjoint union”) of a finite number of affine opens in
X
.

For
i=1,\ldots,n+1
, let
U_{i}:=\bigsqcup\{V_{j_{1},\dots,j_{i}}:=V_{j_{1}}\cap\ldots\cap V_{j_{i}}:1\leq j_{1}<\dots<j_{i}\leq n+1\}
, denote by
\varphi_{i}:=\bigsqcup\iota_{V_{j_{1},\dots,j_{i}}}\colon U_{i}\to X
the natural map induced by the inclusions, and define an endofunctor
F_{i}:=(\varphi_{i})_{*}\circ\varphi^{*}_{i}\colon\mathrm{Qcoh}(X)\to\mathrm{Qcoh}(X)
. Consider the following exact sequence of endofunctors of
\mathrm{Qcoh}(X)
, which gives the usual (ordered) Čech coresolution with respect to the cover
\{V_{1},\dots,V_{n+1}\}
(see
[
32
,
Tag 01FG
]
):

0\overset{}{\Longrightarrow}\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{A}}\overset{}{\Longrightarrow}F_{1}\overset{}{\Longrightarrow}\cdots\overset{}{\Longrightarrow}F_{n+1}\overset{}{\Longrightarrow}0,

and observe that, for any set
J
, any
k\geq 1
, and any
i=1,\dots,n+1
, the following holds:

\textstyle\prod_{J}^{(k)}\circ F_{i}=\prod_{J}^{(k)}\circ(\varphi_{i})_{*}\circ\varphi_{i}^{*}=(\varphi_{i})_{*}\circ\left(\prod_{J}^{(k)}\circ\varphi_{i}^{*}\right)=(\varphi_{i})_{*}\circ 0=0,

where the first equality comes from the definition of
F_{i}
, the second one is discussed in the first half of the proof, and third one holds since
\mathrm{Qcoh}(U_{i})\cong\mathrm{Mod}\text{-}{\mathcal{O}_{U_{i}}}\cong\prod\mathrm{Mod}\text{-}{\mathcal{O}_{V_{j_{1},\dots,j_{i}}}}
is (Ab.4
∗
), being equivalent to a category of modules. One can now conclude by Lemma
8.26
.
∎

Under the hypotheses of the above theorem, Positselski
[
25
]
proved that
\mathrm{Qcoh}(X)
has a generator of projective dimension
\leq n+1
, deducing that it is (Ab.4
∗
)-
n+1
. He then asked in
[
25
, Question 3.7]
if this bound is sharp: his question is completely answered by the above theorem.

—————————————
